License: CC BY 4.0 arXiv:2607.14923v1 [cs.DS] 16 Jul 2026 Random Access to LZ-End: Faster and Deterministic Itai Boneh Affiliation: University of Wrocław Paweł Gawrychowski Affiliation: University of Wrocław Abstract The LZ-End parsing of a length- n n string is a variation of Lempel-Ziv compression introduced by Kreft and Navarro [DCC 2010],
motivated by the lack of a linear-size structure with O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) access time for the classical variant.
While the original paper was only able to provide efficient extraction from the
phrase boundaries, recently Kempa and Saha [SODA 2022] established that, for a string S S whose LZ-End parsing consists of z z phrases, there exists a random access data structure that uses O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space and guarantees O ⁡ ( log 4 ⁡ n ⋅ log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) O(\log^{4}n\cdot\log\log n) query time.
However, their proof does not yield an efficient construction algorithm, and their data structure is inherently randomized. We resolve both limitations by providing a deterministic, O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) -space data structure that supports random access queries in polylogarithmic time and can be constructed in O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ⁡ ( n / z ) ) O(z\log^{2}(n/z)) time directly from the LZ-End parsing.
In addition to eliminating randomness and providing an efficient construction algorithm, the query time of our data structure is O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ ( n / z ) ) O(\log^{2}(n/z)) , significantly improving upon the query time of Kempa and Saha. We also show that our techniques can be used to support the more general substring-extraction.
Namely, we present a data structure with the same space and the same construction time that given two indices i i and j j , outputs S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] in O ⁡ ( j − i + log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(j-i+\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time. 1 Introduction The natural approach to compressing data is to identify its repeating fragments. Then,
for every subsequent occurrence of such a fragment, instead of spelling it out explicitly again, we
can write down a pointer to the previous occurrence. This is the underlying high-level idea of the
well-known Lempel-Ziv compression scheme [ 27 ] used in popular
compressors such as ZIP , gzip , RAR , PKZIP , and PNG . While being able to compress the input to obtain its hopefully significantly smaller compressed
representation that can be still decompressed to obtain the original input is already interesting,
in most applications we would actually prefer to avoid the decompression and instead directly
operate on the compressed representation. Ideally, we would like to augment the compressed
representation with a data structure of roughly the same size that allows querying the original input.
We refer to Navarro’s survey [ 21 ] for an overview of such results
for different compression methods. While the ultimate goal is designing a compressed index ,
which is a data structure that allows for efficient pattern matching queries [ 3 , 5 , 8 , 19 , 22 , 11 , 25 , 9 ] ,
the first step (and in fact an important building block in many of the compressed indexes) is
a compressed random access structure. A compressed random access structure for a string S [ 1 . . n ] S[1..n] should use space roughly proportional
to the size of the compressed representation of the input and allow for extracting any character S ⁡ [ i ] S[i] efficiently.
Ideally, we would like such a query to take logarithmic time in the length of the string.
Whether this is known to be possible depends on the chosen compression method. A Lempel-Ziv (LZ77) representation of a string S [ 1 . . n ] S[1..n] is a factorization
of the string into z z blocks called phrases: S [ 1 . . n ] = P 1 P 2 … P z S[1..n]=P_{1}P_{2}\dots P_{z} . Each phrase P i P_{i} is either a single character, that is, | P i | = 1 |P_{i}|=1 , or has an earlier occurrence in S S . This allows
us to encode each phrase is constant space by either specifying its only character, or the position
and the length of the previous occurrence. It is known [ 20 ] that
greedily choosing each phrase to be as long as possible results in the smallest number of phrases.
A related (provably weaker, but easier to operate on) compression method is a straight-line
program (SLP) representation, which is simply a context-free grammar with exactly one production
for each non-terminal that derives exactly one string S S . It can be seen that an SLP consisting
of g g productions can be converted into a LZ77 parse of size g g . In the other direction,
Charikar et al. [ 7 ] and Rytter [ 24 ] independently
established that a LZ77 parse of size z z can be converted into a SLP consisting of O ⁡ ( z ​ log ⁡ ( n / z ) ) O(z\log(n/z)) productions. In fact, the obtained SLP is balanced , meaning that the
depth of the derivation tree is O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) . This has the advantage of automatically providing
a compressed random access structure with query time O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) and size O ⁡ ( z ​ log ⁡ ( n / z ) ) O(z\log(n/z)) ,
which simply operates by descending down in the grammar. Verbin and Yu [ 26 ] proved that, roughly speaking, this is very close to optimal. Namely, any structure of size O ⁡ ( z ​ polylog ⁡ n ) O(z\polylog n) must have Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ n / log ⁡ log ​ n ) \Omega(\log n/\log\log n) query time, for the case where n = z 1 + ϵ n=z^{1+\epsilon} .
This however does not exclude the possibility of providing a compressed random access structure with size roughly proportional
to the size of the compressed representation and, say, logarithmic query time. This goal has been in fact achieved for grammar compression. Bille et al. [ 6 ] showed that, assuming the Word RAM model, given a SLP representation of size g g representing a string S [ 1 . . n ] S[1..n] ,
we can build a data structure of size O ⁡ ( g ) O(g) that supports random access queries in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) time.
Later, Ganardi, Jeż, and Lohrey [ 12 ] showed a stronger version of this statement:
any SLP of size g g representing a string S [ 1 . . n ] S[1..n] can be converted into a balanced SLP of size O ⁡ ( g ) O(g) representing the same string.
This can be seen as the ultimate random access structure for SLPs, although the lower bound of Verbin and Yu [ 26 ] still leaves the possibility of designing a structure of size O ⁡ ( g ) O(g) and allowing random access in optimal O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n / log ⁡ log ​ n ) O(\log n/\log\log n) time.
Somewhat surprisingly, the best known bounds for a LZ77 compressed random access structure
follow by converting the LZ77 parse to a grammar, and achieve O ⁡ ( z ​ log ⁡ ( n / z ) ) O(z\log(n/z)) space with O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) query time
(see [ 2 ] for an alternative solution achieving the same time-space bounds).
The logarithmic space increase is a major drawback of such a solution: after all, the compression ratio
might actually be of a similar magnitude (as is the case for a random input), so by building such a structure
we are not benefiting at all from using a compressed representation. Fully understanding
the complexity of random access for LZ77 compressed strings remains to be a major open problem in the area. Motivated by the lack of progress on understanding the complexity of random access for LZ77 compressed strings,
Kreft and Navarro [ 19 ] introduced a restricted variant of LZ77, called LZ-End,
in which the previous occurrence of each phrase needs to end at a phrase boundary. With this restriction,
the greedy parsing is not necessarily optimal.
However, finding the optimal LZ-End parsing of a string was shown to be NP-hard [ 1 ] , when the greedy parse can be found efficiently [ 17 ] ,
and still behaves very well in practice [ 16 ] .
While already the original paper established that extracting substrings ending at a boundary
can be done efficiently with only O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space for LZ-End parse consisting of z z phrases (in fact, this can
be seen as the reason for restricting the parsing in such a way), it was not known whether LZ-End
admits better compressed random access structures than that of a LZ77 until 10 years later Kempa and Saha [ 18 ] showed that, in fact, LZ-End does admit a data structure of size O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) that supports random access queries
in polylogarithmic time. However, their construction has two major drawbacks. 1. The original proof is purely existential: that is, they only showed that, for any string S [ 1 . . n ] S[1..n] , there
exists a data structure of size O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) with polylogarithmic query time. The construction crucially uses probabilistic method,
so we can either verify whether the obtained data structure is correct after construction, or we can compromise on
a data structure for which the bound on the query time holds with high probability. 2. The guarantee on the query time in the original proof, while polylogarithmic, is actually quite high: O ⁡ ( log 4 ⁡ n ⋅ log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) O(\log^{4}n\cdot\log\log n) . This should be compared with the only known lower bound, which is Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ n / log ⁡ log ​ n ) \Omega(\log n/\log\log n) for structures of size O ⁡ ( z ​ polylog ⁡ n ) O(z\polylog n) . Further, the time-space product of the original structure
(a natural measure for data structures) is as high as O ⁡ ( z ​ log 4 ​ n ⋅ log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) O(z\log^{4}n\cdot\log\log n) , while for the LZ77 compression
it is a much more reasonable O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ⁡ ( n / z ) ) O(z\log^{2}(n/z)) . Our results. Our contribution is twofolds. First, we adapt the high-level idea used by Kempa and Saha to obtain,
for a string of length n n described by a LZ-End parse consisting of z z phrases, a random access structure
of size O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) and polylogarithmic query time that can be constructed deterministically in O ~ ​ ( z ) \tilde{O}(z) time.
As explicitly mentioned by Kempa and Saha, their data structure can be either constructed
in expected O ⁡ ( poly ⁡ ( n ) ) O(\poly(n)) time with worst-case queries or in worst-case O ⁡ ( poly ⁡ ( n ) ) O(\poly(n)) time
with expected-time queries. Thus, we not only completely remove the randomization, but further
bring down the construction time to depend on the size of the compressed representation of the text
instead of its length. Second, with further combinatorial insight we are able to significantly improve the query time,
which was as high as O ⁡ ( log 4 ⁡ n ⋅ log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) O(\log^{4}n\cdot\log\log n) in the original construction. More specifically,
our query time is only O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ ( n z ) ) O(\log^{2}(\frac{n}{z})) . 1.1 . Given an LZ-End factorization 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} consisting of z z phrases of a string S [ 1 . . n ] S[1..n] , we can compute in time O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ ( n z ) ) O(z\log^{2}(\frac{n}{z})) a data structure of size O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) supporting a random access query in O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ ( n z ) ) O(\log^{2}(\frac{n}{z})) time. We note that the time-space tradeoff of our structure is O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ ( n z ) ) O(z\log^{2}(\frac{n}{z})) , which matches the best known tradeoffs
for the LZ77 random access structure. Our techniques can be applied and enhanced to additionally support the stronger substring extraction query. 1.2 . Given an LZ-End factorization 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} consisting of z z phrases of a string S [ 1 . . n ] S[1..n] , we can compute in time O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ ( n z ) ) O(z\log^{2}(\frac{n}{z})) a data structure of size O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) that given two indices i , j ∈ [ n ] i,j\in[n] outputs S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] in O ⁡ ( j − i + log 2 ⁡ ( n z ) ) O(j-i+\log^{2}(\frac{n}{z})) time. 2 Preliminaries Integer intervals. We use standard notation to denote consecutive sets of integers.
For integers i , j i,j , we denote [ i . . j ] = { i , i + 1 , … , j } [i..j]=\{i,i+1,\ldots,j\} (if j < i j<i then [ i . . j ] = ∅ [i..j]=\emptyset ).
We also denote [ i ] = [ 1 . . i ] [i]=[1..i] , and ( i . . j ) = ( i . . j − 1 ] = [ i + 1 . . j ) = [ i + 1 . . j − 1 ] (i..j)=(i..j-1]=[i+1..j)=[i+1..j-1] . Strings. A string S S with length n n over alphabet Σ \Sigma is a sequence of symbols S ⁡ [ 1 ] ​ S ​ [ 2 ] ​ … ​ S ​ [ n ] S[1]S[2]\ldots S[n] where S ⁡ [ i ] ∈ Σ S[i]\in\Sigma for every i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] .
For integers i , j ∈ [ n ] i,j\in[n] we denote S [ i . . j ] = S [ i ] S [ i + 1 ] . . S [ j ] S[i..j]=S[i]S[i+1]..S[j] .
We call S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] a substring of S S . LZ-End factorization. An LZ-End factorization of a string S [ 1 . . n ] S[1..n] is a partition of S S into substrings P 1 , P 2 , … ​ P z P_{1},P_{2},\ldots P_{z} called phrases.
For each i i , we have P i = S [ a i . . b i ] P_{i}=S[a_{i}..b_{i}] , with a 1 = 1 a_{1}=1 , b z = n b_{z}=n , and a i = b i − 1 + 1 a_{i}=b_{i-1}+1 for every i ∈ ( 1 . . z ] i\in(1..z] .
Each phrase satisfies one of the following conditions.
Either P i P_{i} is a phrase of length one, i.e. P i = S ⁡ [ a i ] P_{i}=S[a_{i}] , we call such a phrase a terminal phrase.
Or, P i = S [ a i . . b i ] P_{i}=S[a_{i}..b_{i}] , and there is some reference phrase P j P_{j} with j < i j<i such that S ( b j − | P i | . . b j ] = P i S(b_{j}-|P_{i}|..b_{j}]=P_{i} .
We call such a phrase a reference phrase, and say that P j P_{j} is the source of P i P_{i} .
When we are given an LZ-End factorization, we assume that every reference phrase P i P_{i} is given alongside the index j j of its source phrase.
We stress that, in contrast to prior work [ 18 ] ,
we are not requiring that the given parsing is greedy, that is, it is not necessarily the case that every P i P_{i} is the longest phrase that occurs earlier ending at a phrase boundary. In Appendix B , we show that any LZ-End factorization with z z phrases of a string with length n n can be efficiently transformed into a similar size LZ-End factorization of the same string, with all the phrases having size at most n z \frac{n}{z} . 2.1 . There is an algorithm that given 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} , an LZ-End partition of a string S S , returns 𝒵 ′ \mathcal{Z}^{\prime} , an LZ-End partition of S S with size at most 4 ​ z 4z such that every phrase P ′ ∈ 𝒵 ′ P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime} has | P ′ | ≤ n z |P^{\prime}|\leq\frac{n}{z} .
Here, n = | S | n=|S| and z = | 𝒵 | z=|\mathcal{Z}| .
The algorithm runs in time O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) . Throughout the paper, we often partition integer from [ 0 . . n z ] [0..\frac{n}{z}] into exponential levels.
We fix the notation 𝕂 = { 1.5 i ∣ i ∈ [ 0 . . log 1.5 n z ] } \mathbb{K}=\{1.5^{i}\mid i\in[0..\log_{1.5}\frac{n}{z}]\} for the set of integer powers of 1.5 1.5 smaller than n z \frac{n}{z} . Computational model. We describe our algorithm in the standard Word RAM model, see e.g. [ 14 ] .
We assume that n n fits in a single machine word, and basic arithmetical operations on numbers that fit in a constant
number of machine words take constant time. In particular, constant-time indirect addressing is available, allowing us
to implement arrays with constant lookup time. In this model, we have the following result (called deterministic perfect hashing). 2.2 [ 23 ] . Given a set S S consisting of n n integers, we can build in deterministic O ⁡ ( n ​ ( log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) 2 ) O(n(\log\log n)^{2}) time a data structure
of size O ⁡ ( n ) O(n) that allows checking if x ∈ S x\in S and accessing its associated information in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time. 3 Framework and Overview Let us start by establishing notation and terminology to be used for the rest of this section.
We are given as input an LZ-end factorization 𝒵 = P 1 , P 2 , … , P z \mathcal{Z}=P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{z} of string S S with length n n .
Each phrase is given with its endpoints a i a_{i} and b i b_{i} such that P i = S [ a i . . b i ] P_{i}=S[a_{i}..b_{i}] .
Additionally, each phrase P i P_{i} with length at least 2 2 is given with the index j < i j<i such that P j P_{j} is the source of P i P_{i} .
For every index i ∈ [ 1 . . n ] i\in[1..n] in the text, we denote as r i r_{i} and ℓ i \ell_{i} the distance to the closest phrase boundary to the right of i i and to the left of i i , respectively.
Formally, if the phrase containing index i i is P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] , then r i = b − i r_{i}=b-i and ℓ i = i − a \ell_{i}=i-a .
We call r i r_{i} and ℓ i \ell_{i} the r r -value and the ℓ \ell -value of i i , respectively. 3.1 Intuition and High-level Approach Let us first describe the approach of Kempa and Saha [ 18 ] .
We are storing an array B B with B ⁡ [ i ] = S ⁡ [ b i ] B[i]=S[b_{i}] for every i ∈ [ z ] i\in[z] .
For an index i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] with r i = 0 r_{i}=0 , we have that i = b j i=b_{j} for some j ∈ [ z ] j\in[z] and we can output S ⁡ [ i ] = B ⁡ [ j ] S[i]=B[j] .
Our goal is to reduce an arbitrary index i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] given at query time to an index i ′ ∈ [ n ] i^{\prime}\in[n] with r i ′ = 0 r_{i^{\prime}}=0 and S ⁡ [ i ] = S ⁡ [ i ′ ] S[i]=S[i^{\prime}] .
Kempa and Saha [ 18 ] achieve this goal by defining a ’step’ rule N ⁡ ( i ) N(i) for every index i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] .
This step rule maps i i with r i > 0 r_{i}>0 to another index N ⁡ ( i ) < i N(i)<i such that S ⁡ [ i ] = S ⁡ [ N ⁡ ( i ) ] S[i]=S[N(i)] .
The rule defined by Saha and Kempa is both compactly computable and terminates quickly .
By compactly computable, we mean that there is a data structure with size O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) that allows computing N ⁡ ( i ) N(i) in polylogarithmic time.
By terminates quickly, we mean that for every i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] , repeatedly applying N ​ ( i ) , N 2 ​ ( i ) , … N(i),N^{2}(i),\ldots will result in some index i ′ i^{\prime} with r i ′ = 0 r_{i^{\prime}}=0 after a polylogarithmic number of steps.
It should be clear that given such a step rule, one can support random access in O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space and polylogarithmic time. We provide a high level description of the step rule of [ 18 ] .
The step function N N is a composition of three different functions: J J , s ​ J sJ , and M M ( "Jump" , "Stable Jump" , and "Marked" ).
The function J J is defined for every index i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] with r i > 0 r_{i}>0 , while s ​ J sJ and M M are only defined for a strict subset of those indices.
For an index i i with r i > 0 r_{i}>0 , we will prioritize using M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) as the step from i i , i,e, we have N ⁡ ( i ) = M ⁡ ( i ) N(i)=M(i) if M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) is defined.
Otherwise, we set N ⁡ ( i ) = s ​ J ​ ( i ) N(i)=sJ(i) , and only if both M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) and s ​ J ​ ( i ) sJ(i) are undefined, we have N ⁡ ( i ) = J ⁡ ( i ) N(i)=J(i) . Consider a sequence i , N ⁡ ( i ) , N 2 ​ ( i ) , … i,N(i),N^{2}(i),\ldots of N N steps.
Each of J J , s ​ J sJ , and M M have a particular role in guaranteeing that this sequence quickly converges to an index i ′ i^{\prime} with r i ′ = 0 r_{i^{\prime}}=0 .
This goal is achieved in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) step sequences called epochs .
An epoch is a subsequence of N N steps such that at the end of the epoch, the r r -value is in a smaller exponential level than the one seen at the start of the epoch.
Since initially r i ≤ n r_{i}\leq n , after applying O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) epochs we reach an index with r r -value 0 0 as required.
Let us formally define the goal of the epoch.
We fix the set 𝕂 n = { 1.5 k ∣ k ∈ [ 0 . . log 1.5 n ] } \mathbb{K}_{n}=\{1.5^{k}\mid k\in[0..\log_{1.5}n]\} of integer powers of 1.5 1.5 smaller than n n .
An epoch starts at an index i i that has r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) for some k ∈ 𝕂 n k\in\mathbb{K}_{n} , and ends upon reaching an index j j with S ⁡ [ i ] = S ⁡ [ j ] S[i]=S[j] and r j < k r_{j}<k . Let us describe the role of each function in an epoch.
First of all, all the functions share the property of being r r -non increasing.
That is, we will have r F ⁡ ( i ) ≤ r i r_{F(i)}\leq r_{i} for every F ∈ { J , s ​ J , M } F\in\{J,sJ,M\} for which F ⁡ ( i ) F(i) is defined. The function J J will act as a naive ’searching’ step.
It transforms i i into an index to the left of i i , without increasing the r r -value and while maintaining S ⁡ [ i ] = S ⁡ [ J ⁡ ( i ) ] S[i]=S[J(i)] .
We are not guaranteed to have any progress towards lowering the value of r r when using J J , but we ’hope’ that by repeatedly applying J J , we will eventually reach an index for which M M is defined.
We call the part of the epoch before M M is activated for the first time the naive jumping part. The function M M has the role of a trigger function and a progress function, and it has a symbiotic relationship with s ​ J sJ .
By trigger, we mean that after M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) applies, the naive jumping part of the epoch ends, and it enters a different state called the stable part .
The epoch remains in the stable part until the end of the epoch.
During the stable part, all N N steps are guaranteed to be either M M or s ​ J sJ until we will eventually reach some index i ′ i^{\prime} with r i ′ < 2 3 ​ r i r_{i^{\prime}}<\frac{2}{3}r_{i} , finishing the epoch. When we say that M M is a progress function, we mean that M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) is guaranteed to have a significantly reduced ℓ \ell -value or a significantly reduced r r -value.
Namely, it holds that min ⁡ ( r M ⁡ ( i ) , ℓ M ⁡ ( i ) ) ≤ min ⁡ ( r i / 2 , ℓ i / 2 ) \min(r_{M(i)},\ell_{M(i)})\leq\min(r_{i}/2,\ell_{i}/2) .
Following this notion, we call an index i i for which M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) is defined a progress index . The key property of s ​ J sJ is that it is ℓ \ell -non-decreasing, in the following sense.
For every index i i for which j = s ​ J ​ ( i ) j=sJ(i) is defined, either ℓ j ≤ ℓ i \ell_{j}\leq\ell_{i} or r j < 2 3 ​ r i r_{j}<\frac{2}{3}r_{i} .
Notice that in the latter case, the epoch is finished.
In words, s ​ J sJ guarantees that in each step we either do not increase ℓ \ell , or we achieve the end of the epoch.
We note that the function M M also has this property. We have that throughout the stable part, both r r and ℓ \ell values are non-increasing.
Furthermore, every time we reach a progress index, we have that r r or ℓ \ell are reduced by a constant factor.
Since ℓ i ≤ n \ell_{i}\leq n , it can only be reduced by a constant factor O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) times throughout the epoch.
It follows that after visiting O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) progress indices, we will finally reach some i ′ i^{\prime} with r i ′ < 2 3 ​ r i r_{i^{\prime}}<\frac{2}{3}r_{i} , finishing the epoch. The last important property of M M is the property of being common.
That is, for every index i i , it is guaranteed that withing a polylogarithmic number of N N steps, we reach a progress index. Therefore, we are guaranteed to reach a progress index after a polylogarithmic number of steps, triggering the start of the stable part.
The epoch ends after we visit O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) progress indices, and we reach a new progress index every polylogarithmic number of steps.
It follows that the epoch ends within a polylogarithmic number of steps. i i S NJ S NJ … \dots S NJ b j b_{j} O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) Figure 1 : The high level structure of the query algorithm of Kempa and Saha.
Starting with the input index i i , we apply a sequence of O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) epochs (depicted as larger rectangles).
Every epoch consists of two parts: the naive jumping part (depicted as an ’NJ’ labeled square) and the stable part (’S’ labeled square).
Each epoch outputs an index with r r -value in a smaller exponential level than that of the input index of the epoch.
After the last epoch, we conclude in some right boundary of a phrase b j b_{j} , as the r r -value is 0 0 .
In our implementation of this approach, the native jumping part corresponds to Lemma 3.1 and the stable part corresponds to Lemma 3.2 .
Each is implemented in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time, and the number epochs is reduced to O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) due to Lemma 2.1 . Implementing J J , s ​ J sJ , and M M . The implementation of M M follows from a well-known approach from random access on Lempel-Ziv compressed strings.
A phrase P = S [ a . . b ] ∈ 𝒵 P=S[a..b]\in\mathcal{Z} can be processed such that every index i ∈ [ a . . b ] i\in[a..b] is mapped to an index j j with S ⁡ [ j ] = S ⁡ [ i ] S[j]=S[i] , and either min ⁡ ( r j , ℓ j ) ≤ min ⁡ ( r i / 2 , ℓ i / 2 ) \min(r_{j},\ell_{j})\leq\min(r_{i}/2,\ell_{i}/2) (and in either case, r j ≤ r i r_{j}\leq r_{i} ).
However, in order to support an O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) computation of this mapping, one needs to use O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) space. The O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) in the space complexity for supporting M M arises from a partition of P P into Θ ⁡ ( log ⁡ | P | ) \Theta(\log|P|) intervals.
For each interval, the data structure stores O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) space to support computing M M for indices in this interval.
Kempa and Saha choose one random interval from each phrase, thus supporting the computation of M M only with probability 1 / log ⁡ n 1/\log n for an index in P P .
By making this compromise, they reduce the space consumption per phrase to O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) .
It should be easy to see that if we design N N to always move between phrases (i.e, N ⁡ ( i ) N(i) is always in a phrase to the left of the phrase containing i i ), the common property of M M is satisfied with high probability (with an appropriate choice of a polylogarithmic function). J J is the simplest function to implement: for every index i i contained in a phrase P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] with source S [ a ′ . . b ′ ] S[a^{\prime}..b^{\prime}] , we define J ⁡ ( i ) = i − b + b ′ J(i)=i-b+b^{\prime} .
Notice that this maps i i to the index aligned with i i in S [ b ′ − | P | + 1 . . b ′ ] = P S[b^{\prime}-|P|+1..b^{\prime}]=P , so we have both S ⁡ [ i ] = S ⁡ [ J ⁡ ( i ) ] S[i]=S[J(i)] and r J ⁡ ( i ) ≤ r i r_{J(i)}\leq r_{i} . In order to define the function s ​ J sJ , Kempa and Saha introduce the 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{pre} function.
The function 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) receives two parameters b , e b,e representing an interval of indices within S S .
The output of the function corresponds to result of the following process:
check if b b and e e are contained in the same phrase of 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} .
If they are not then return b b .
Otherwise, replace ( b , e ) (b,e) with ( J ⁡ ( b ) , J ⁡ ( e ) ) (J(b),J(e)) and repeat.
Intuitively, one can think of 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) as a pointer to an occurrence S [ b ′ . . e ′ ] S[b^{\prime}..e^{\prime}] of S [ b . . e ] S[b..e] that is achieved by following the source links of 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} (potentially many times), and contains a phrase boundary.
In order to support efficient computation of s ​ J ​ ( i ) sJ(i) , the data structure of Kempa and Saha stores 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) values for O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) pairs ( b , e ) (b,e) . b b e e 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) Figure 2 : A demonstration of the function 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) .
The dotted arrows point from a right-end point of a phrase to the right endpoint of its source phrase.
The bottom arrows demonstrate the process of finding another copy of S [ b . . e ] S[b..e] to the left repeatedly, as long as the leftmost copy found so far is contained in a phrase. The challenges of constructing [ 18 ] . We wish to provide an efficient, deterministic construction based on the ideas of Kempa and Saha.
There are two primary obstacles. Firstly, the construction of Kempa and Saha requires computing O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) values of 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) .
A straightforward computation of 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) can potentially take O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) time, for a total of O ⁡ ( z 2 ) O(z^{2}) running time across all required 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) values.
We overcome this challenge by providing a batched algorithm for computing multiple queries ( b , e ) (b,e) simultaneously,
reminiscent of the winding phase of Farach and Thorup’s algorithm for pattern matching in LZ-compressed text [ 10 ] .
This is achieved by processing a batch I = ( b 1 , e 1 ) , ( b 2 , e 2 ) , … I=(b_{1},e_{1}),(b_{2},e_{2}),\ldots of queries via a single right-to-left scan of 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} .
When a phrase P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] is processed, we can ’shift’ all queries contained within P P to the source of P P as a single block in polylogarithmic time.
Then, we can find every ( b i , e i ) (b_{i},e_{i}) such that b i b_{i} and e i e_{i} are separated by a a in polylogarithmic time per such query.
This leads to a total running time of O ⁡ ( ( z + | I | ) ​ polylog ⁡ ( n z ) ) O((z+|I|)\polylog(\frac{n}{z})) . The second challenge is deterministically implement M M .
We take a different approach than the one of Kempa and Saha for reducing the total space complexity for M M .
While they partially computed the function M M for every phrase, we pick a subset 𝒵 M ⊆ 𝒵 \mathcal{Z}_{M}\subseteq\mathcal{Z} of size O ⁡ ( z / log ⁡ n z ) O(z/\log\frac{n}{z}) , and compute M M fully for every phrase in 𝒵 M \mathcal{Z}_{M} . Intuitively, one would like to pick a set 𝒵 M \mathcal{Z}_{M} that acts as a cover in the following sense.
Consider for every i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] the sequence P ⁡ ( i ) P(i) of polylog ⁡ n \polylog n phrases visited by iteratively applying J J exactly polylog ⁡ n \polylog n times (for some fixed polylog ⁡ n \polylog n ) starting from i i .
A good choice of 𝒵 M \mathcal{Z}_{M} corresponds to a set of phrases that is both compact, and covers all P ⁡ ( i ) P(i) sequences.
Since we aim for a preprocessing time of O ⁡ ( z ​ polylog ⁡ ( n z ) ) O(z\polylog(\frac{n}{z})) , we can not even afford to inspect all P ⁡ ( i ) P(i) within our preprocessing time. We sidestep this issue by substituting the demand that 𝒵 M \mathcal{Z}_{M} covers all such sequences with a relaxed notion of covering that is satisfactory for our purpose.
First, let us simplify our analysis by assuming that prior to M M , we only make naive steps using J J (and never use s ​ J sJ ).
Now, for every i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] , we say that 𝒵 M \mathcal{Z}_{M} light-covers i i if there is some x ∈ [ 0 . . log n z ] x\in[0..\log\frac{n}{z}] such that J x ​ ( i ) J^{x}(i) is either contained in some phrase of 𝒵 M \mathcal{Z}_{M} or J x ​ ( i ) J^{x}(i) has r r -value less than 2 3 ​ r i \frac{2}{3}r_{i} .
In words, we do not require 𝒵 M \mathcal{Z}_{M} to intersect the first log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} steps following i i in the event when we get ’lucky’, and the repeated applications of J J happen to incur a significant reduction in r r -value without invoking M M . With this notion of covering, our task is reduced to covering all sequences i i , J ⁡ ( i ) J(i) , J 2 ​ ( i ) J^{2}(i) , … \ldots , J log ⁡ ( n z ) ​ ( i ) J^{\log(\frac{n}{z})}(i) such that the starting and ending r r -values are within the same exponential level.
As it turns out, sequences with this property have a very strict structure.
Namely, we observe that given i i in a phrase P P with r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) for some k ∈ 𝕂 n k\in\mathbb{K}_{n} , if J ⁡ ( i ) J(i) has r r -value that is also in [ k ..1.5 k ) [k..1.5k) , there is only one phrase P ′ P^{\prime} (independent of i i ) that may contain J ⁡ ( i ) J(i) .
In other words: all ’bad steps’ from phrase P P are mapped to the same phrase P ′ P^{\prime} .
This induces a tree structure over the phrases, where the parent of each phrase P P is the unique phrase P ′ P^{\prime} that may be its successor in a sequence of bad steps.
Given such a structure, we can apply the standard approach of selecting a sparse tree layer as a compact path-covering set. Improving the query time. While our implementation is inspired by the construction of Kempa and Saha, our approach is different in the following sense.
Kempa and Saha defined a ’step’ function N N , and prove that it has certain properties.
Then, the query algorithm iteratively applies the function, and it is guaranteed to converge quickly to a boundary index due to the properties of the function. We use similar function definitions to the ones of Kempa and Saha, but our query algorithm does not iteratively apply these functions.
Instead, our goal is to find the critical value x x such that N x ​ ( i ) N^{x}(i) has a sufficiently small r r -value, and compute N x ​ ( i ) N^{x}(i) .
By further studying the properties of N N (and slightly modifying its definition), we are able to design algorithms that find x x and N x ​ ( i ) N^{x}(i) without explicitly iterating over x = 0 , 1 , 2 , … x=0,1,2,\ldots . For instance, the component of our algorithm equivalent to the stable part of Kempa and Saha does not use M M .
We only use M M to trigger the stable part, but not to make progress within it.
Instead, we simply consider a sequence of applications of s ​ J sJ throughout the stable part of the epoch.
Since we gave up the progress obtained by occasionally reaching M M , the sequence of s ​ J sJ applications before some progress in r r is achieved may be arbitrarily large.
However, by dropping M M we obtain a cleaner structure to the stable part.
That structure allows us to find the first x x such that s ​ J x ​ ( i ) sJ^{x}(i) has a significantly reduced r r -value in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time. Finally, since we wish to have O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) epochs rather than O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) , we introduce a method for reducing any LZ-end partition with z z phrases into a partition with O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) phrases such that every phrase has length O ⁡ ( n z ) O(\frac{n}{z}) ( Lemma 2.1 ).
This allows us to assume that the initial r r -value is at most n z \frac{n}{z} . Formally, we break the task of previn Theorem 1.1 into the following two lemmas. 3.1 Naive Jumping Part. Given 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} an LZ-end factorization of S S , we can construct a data structure supporting the following query.
Given an index i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] with r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) for some k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , compute an index j j satisfying r j ≤ r i r_{j}\leq r_{i} , S ⁡ [ i ] = S ⁡ [ j ] S[i]=S[j] , and either r j < k r_{j}<k or ℓ j ≤ 1.5 ​ k \ell_{j}\leq 1.5k . The data structure can be constructed in O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O(z\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time, consumes O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space, and supports queries in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time. Lemma 3.1 corresponds to applying J J repeatedly until the r r -value is either reduced significantly, or an index i i for which M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) is defined is met.
We choose a set 𝒵 M \mathcal{Z}_{M} for the definition of M M such that this is guaranteed to happen within O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) applications of J J .
The O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) query time is achieved by introducing shortcuts, which allows us to compute J log ⁡ n z J^{\log{\frac{n}{z}}} in constant time, under some conditions. 3.2 Stable Part. Given 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} an LZ-end factorization of S S , we can construct a data structure supporting the following query.
Given an index i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] with r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) for some k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} and ℓ i ≤ 1.5 ​ k \ell_{i}\leq 1.5k , compute an index j j satisfying r j < k r_{j}<k and S ⁡ [ i ] = S ⁡ [ j ] S[i]=S[j] . The data structure can be constructed in O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O(z\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time, consumes O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space, and supports queries in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time. Lemma 3.2 corresponds to receiving the first index of the stable part, and efficiently finding the last index of the epoch.
As we discussed before, it is achieved by analyzing the structure of the stable part to obtain a clean characterization of the first index with r r -value below the required threshold. By combining Lemma 3.1 and Lemma 3.2 , we obtain our main theorem. Proof of Theorem 1.1 . Given 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} an LZ-End parsing of S S , we store the array B B with B i = S ⁡ [ b i ] B_{i}=S[b_{i}] for every i ∈ [ z ] i\in[z] .
We then construct the data structures of both Lemma 3.1 and Lemma 3.2 in O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O(z\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time. Given a query index i i with r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) for some k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , we apply the data structure of Lemma 3.1 to obtain i ′ i^{\prime} with S ⁡ [ i ′ ] = S ⁡ [ i ] S[i^{\prime}]=S[i] and either r i ′ < k r_{i^{\prime}}<k or ℓ i ′ ≤ 1.5 ​ k \ell_{i^{\prime}}\leq 1.5k .
If the latter occurs, we apply the data structure of Lemma 3.2 with input i ′ i^{\prime} to obtain j ′ j^{\prime} with r j ′ < k r_{j^{\prime}}<k and S ⁡ [ j ′ ] = S ⁡ [ i ′ ] = S ⁡ [ i ] S[j^{\prime}]=S[i^{\prime}]=S[i] .
In both cases, we spend O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time and obtained some index t ∈ { i ′ , j ′ } t\in\{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\} with S ⁡ [ t ] = S ⁡ [ i ] S[t]=S[i] and r t < k r_{t}<k .
We call the above routine an epoch.
We repeatedly apply epochs, each receiving as input the output of the previous one, until we obtain an index with r r -value zero.
Since each epoch results in an index i i with r i r_{i} bounded by a smaller element of 𝕂 \mathbb{K} , we will reach an index with r j ′ = 0 r_{j^{\prime}}=0 and S ⁡ [ j ′ ] = S ⁡ [ i ] S[j^{\prime}]=S[i] within O ⁡ ( | 𝕂 | ) = O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(|\mathbb{K}|)=O(\log\frac{n}{z}) epochs. In particular, j ′ = b j j^{\prime}=b_{j} for some phrase P j P_{j} .
We can find the phrase P j P_{j} (and the index j j ) containing j ′ j^{\prime} in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time (see Lemma 4.7 ) and then report S ⁡ [ i ] = S ⁡ [ j ′ ] = S ⁡ [ b j ] = B ⁡ [ j ] S[i]=S[j^{\prime}]=S[b_{j}]=B[j] . The running time of each epoch is O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) , and there are O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) epochs, so the total running time of a query is O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time. The space used by our data structure consists of sorting an array of length z z storing all S ⁡ [ b i ] S[b_{i}] , and of storing the data structures of Lemma 3.1 and of Lemma 3.2 , which require O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space each.
The total space complexity is O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) , as required.
∎ The rest of the paper is dedicated to proving Lemma 3.1 and Lemma 3.2 .
We prove Lemma 3.1 in Section 4 and Lemma 3.2 in Section 5 In Appendix B , we prove Lemma 2.1 , which allows us to assume without loss of generality that there are no long phrases in our input factorization 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} .
In Sections 7 , A and 6 we separately provide proofs for various auxiliary data structures used throughout the paper.
Finally, in Appendix C we show how to modify and enhance our techniques to provide a compressed data structure for substring extraction. 4 Naive Jumping In this section, we prove Lemma 3.1 .
We start by presenting a high-level description of the algorithm and of the concepts used by the algorithm, and then proceed to present the full version with all the required definitions and proofs. 4.1 High-Level Overview The objective of this overview is to equip the reader with a conceptual understanding of the machinery behind Lemma 3.1 . The framework developed in this section provides an intuitive abstraction of our method, meaning that the formal definitions and lemmas stated here will not be explicitly invoked in the full description of the data structure. Finally, we express all running times using log ⁡ n \log n instead of log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} to ensure a smoother presentation. For every phrase P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] with source P ′ = S [ a ′ . . b ′ ] P^{\prime}=S[a^{\prime}..b^{\prime}] , denote δ P = b − b ′ \delta_{P}=b-b^{\prime} .
Clearly, for every index i ∈ [ a . . b ] i\in[a..b] , the index j = i − δ P j=i-\delta_{P} satisfies S ⁡ [ i ] = S ⁡ [ j ] S[i]=S[j] and r j ≤ r i r_{j}\leq r_{i} .
We denote by J J the function that maps any i i in a phrase P P to J ⁡ ( i ) = i − δ P J(i)=i-\delta_{P} . Recall that our goal is to efficiently support the following query.
Given some index i i with r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) , return j j with r j ≤ r i r_{j}\leq r_{i} , S ⁡ [ j ] = S ⁡ [ i ] S[j]=S[i] , and either r j < k r_{j}<k or ℓ j < 1.5 ​ k \ell_{j}<1.5k . Consider the following simple algorithm for achieving this goal:
Given an input index i i , let P P be the phrase containing i i in 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} .
Notice that | P | ≠ 1 |P|\neq 1 , as | P | = 1 |P|=1 would imply r i = 0 r_{i}=0 , which contradicts r i ≥ k r_{i}\geq k .
Therefore, | P | > 1 |P|>1 and P P has a source.
Consider j = J ⁡ ( i ) j=J(i) .
If r j < k r_{j}<k , return j j .
Otherwise, repeat this process recursively on j j . This algorithm clearly terminates, as every recursive call is for an index j j strictly smaller than i i .
However, it may be very inefficient.
Our key insight is that the pathological case where this simple algorithm fails is highly structured.
This structure can be described as follows. 4.1 . For every k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} there is a forest T k T_{k} with node set 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} such that every index i ∈ [ 1 . . n ] i\in[1..n] contained in phrase P P with r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) and j = J ⁡ ( i ) j=J(i) , one of the following holds. 1. r j < k r_{j}<k . 2. j j is in the parent of P P in T k T_{k} . The tree T k T_{k} can be computed in O ⁡ ( z ​ log ⁡ n ) O(z\log n) time given 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} . In words, a sequence of applications of J J where the r r -value remains large corresponds to an upwards path in T k T_{k} (see Fig. 5 ). To take advantage of the tree structure of the bad steps, we use the concept of a good mapping. 4.2 . A good mapping M M for a phrase P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] is a data structure that given an index i ∈ [ a . . b ] i\in[a..b] returns in constant time an index j = M ⁡ ( i ) j=M(i) with r j ≤ r i r_{j}\leq r_{i} and S ⁡ [ j ] = S ⁡ [ i ] S[j]=S[i] .
Further, min ⁡ ( r j , ℓ j ) ≤ min ⁡ ( r i / 2 , ℓ i / 2 ) \min(r_{j},\ell_{j})\leq\min(r_{i}/2,\ell_{i}/2) . Notice that if r i < 1.5 ​ k r_{i}<1.5k , a good mapping M M maps i i to j = M ⁡ ( i ) j=M(i) with min ⁡ ( r j , r i ) < k \min(r_{j},r_{i})<k .
Therefore, if i i is in a phrase for which we have a good mapping, we can immediately return a valid output. We prove that given a set M ⊆ 𝒵 M\subseteq\mathcal{Z} of phrases, it is possible to construct good mappings for every phrase in M M with the following complexities. 4.3 . Given 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} and a set M ⊆ 𝒵 M\subseteq\mathcal{Z} of phrases, we can compute a good mapping for each phrase in M M in O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n ) O(z\log^{2}n) time.
The total space consumed by the good mappings is O ⁡ ( z + | M | ​ log ⁡ n ) O(z+|M|\log n) . Notice that we cannot afford to apply Lemma 4.3 with M = 𝒵 M=\mathcal{Z} , as this would result in a data structure with O ⁡ ( z ​ log ⁡ n ) O(z\log n) space.
Instead, we apply Lemma 4.3 with a sparse set that covers all paths of length log 2 ⁡ n \log^{2}n in T k T_{k} .
Such a set can be obtained by partitioning the vertices of T k T_{k} into log 2 ⁡ n \log^{2}n sets based on their distance from the root modulo log 2 ⁡ n \log^{2}n .
From the pigeonhole principle, we have that one of these sets has size at most z / log 2 ⁡ n z/\log^{2}n - we pick M k M_{k} to be this set.
Notice that M k M_{k} intersects any upwards path of length log 2 ⁡ n \log^{2}n in T k T_{k} . The total size of M k M_{k} sets over all values of k k is O ⁡ ( z / log 2 ⁡ n ⋅ | 𝕂 | ) = O ⁡ ( z / log ⁡ n ) O(z/\log^{2}n\cdot|\mathbb{K}|)=O(z/\log n) .
Therefore, by applying Lemma 4.3 to the union of all M k M_{k} ’s, we obtain a good mapping for every phrase within some M k M_{k} in total space O ⁡ ( z + z / log ⁡ n ⋅ log ⁡ n ) = O ⁡ ( z ) O(z+z/\log n\cdot\log n)=O(z) . We are almost ready to present an O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space data structure with a slightly slower O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n ) O(\log^{2}n) running time for Lemma 3.1 .
To this end, we only require the following two technical subroutines.
First, we need a data structure for efficiently finding the phrase P P containing an index i i .
This can be trivially obtained in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) time via binary search.
Second, we require a data structure that given a phrase P P and k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , finds the parent of P P in T k T_{k} in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
Notice that we cannot afford to store all trees T k T_{k} within our O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space limitation.
We show in Lemma 7.7 that such a data structure can be constructed in O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space and O ⁡ ( z ​ log ⁡ n ) O(z\log n) construction time. Now, given an index i i , we first find the phrase P P containing i i in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) time.
If P ∈ M k P\in M_{k} , we simply return M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) .
Otherwise, we compute j = J ⁡ ( i ) j=J(i) and P ′ P^{\prime} the k k -bad parent of P P .
If j j is not contained in P ′ P^{\prime} , we are guaranteed to have r j < k r_{j}<k .
We can therefore return j j .
If j j is contained in P ′ P^{\prime} , we check if r j < k r_{j}<k and if it is we return j j .
If r j ≥ k r_{j}\geq k , we recurse on j j .
Because j j is guaranteed to be in P ′ P^{\prime} , we additionally send P ′ P^{\prime} to the recursive call.
Thus, successive recursive calls do not need to dedicate O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) time to compute the phrase containing j j . Every recursive call except for the first one is implemented in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
From the path covering property of M k M_{k} , we have that if more than log 2 ⁡ n \log^{2}n calls are made, it is guaranteed to reach an index i i contained in a phrase of M k M_{k} .
The running time is therefore O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n ) O(\log^{2}n) . In order to obtain further speedup, we apply a shortcut strategy.
To this end, we analyze a more general notion of ’lack-of-progress’ in the simple algorithm.
In our former analysis, we focused on the case where a single application of J J leads to an index with a large r r -value.
Now, we wish to analyze the case where x x consecutive applications of J J still lead to a large r r -value. We present the notion of an x x -bad index.
Consider a phrase P P and the sequence P = P ( 0 ) , P ( 1 ) , P ( 2 ) , … , P ( x ) P=P^{(0)},P^{(1)},P^{(2)},\ldots,P^{(x)} of ancestors of P P in T k T_{k} (with P ( x ) P^{(x)} denoting the x x -th parent of P P in T k T_{k} ).
We say that an index i i in P P is x x -bad if for every y ∈ [ 0 . . x ] y\in[0..x] , it holds that J y ​ ( i ) J^{y}(i) is in P ( y ) P^{(y)} .
It can be shown by induction that the set of x x -bad indices within P P forms a consecutive interval in P P .
We call this the x x -bad interval of P P . Furthermore, we can use the fact that the sequence of visited phrases is shared by all x x -bad indices in P P to efficiently compute J ( x ) ​ ( i ) J^{(x)}(i) for an x x -bad index i i .
Namely, it holds that for an x x -bad index i i , we have J x ​ ( i ) = i − Δ P J^{x}(i)=i-\Delta_{P} where Δ P = δ P + δ P ( 1 ) + … + δ P ( x − 1 ) \Delta_{P}=\delta_{P}+\delta_{P^{(1)}}+\ldots+\delta_{P^{(x-1)}} . It follows from the above discussion that given the x x -bad interval of P P and Δ P \Delta_{P} , we can decide for an index i i in P P if it is x x -bad, and if it is, find J x ​ ( i ) J^{x}(i) , all in constant time.
Fix x x to be log ⁡ n \log n from now on. Since the total size of all T k T_{k} is O ⁡ ( z ​ log ⁡ n ) O(z\log n) , we cannot afford to store this information for every node in every T k T_{k} .
Instead, as in the previous algorithm, we chose a sparse set M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} of size O ⁡ ( z / log ⁡ n ) O(z/\log n) for each T k T_{k} .
We only store this shortcut information for the vertices in M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} .
We also store for each phrase in M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} its nearest ancestor in T k T_{k} that is marked (i.e, in M k M_{k} ). We are ready to describe our faster query (see Fig. 7 ).
First, apply at most log ⁡ n \log n steps of J J .
If at any point we reach an index j j with r j < k r_{j}<k or in M k M_{k} , we halt and return as in the previous algorithm. Otherwise, after log ⁡ n \log n steps the algorithm must reach some phrase in M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} .
At this point, we start using shortcuts instead.
In every shortcut step, we check if the current i i is ( log ⁡ n ) (\log n) -bad using the ( log ⁡ n ) (\log n) -bad interval.
Additionally, we check the distance d d from the phrase P P containing i i to its nearest ancestor in T k T_{k} that is also in M k M_{k} . If i i is not ( log ⁡ n ) (\log n) -bad, or if d < log ⁡ n d<\log n , apply J J at most log ⁡ n \log n more times instead of using a shortcut – we claim that the algorithm terminates with a valid output within those log ⁡ n \log n steps.
If none of these steps reach an index j j with r j < k r_{j}<k , it must hold that i i is ( log ⁡ n ) (\log n) -bad.
Therefore, this case must have been trigger due to d < log ⁡ n d<\log n .
It follows that one of the log ⁡ n \log n phrases visited after i i is in M k M_{k} , and we can use the good mapping associated with it to return a valid output. Otherwise, we have that i i is x x -bad, and we use Δ P \Delta_{P} to obtain J log ⁡ n ​ ( i ) J^{\log n}(i) in constant time.
Notice that since M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} is selected as a set of all phrases with the same depth modulo log ⁡ n \log n in T k T_{k} , it is guaranteed that J log ⁡ n J^{\log n} is in a phrase of M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} . Let us analyze the running time.
We start using shortcuts after applying J J at most log ⁡ n \log n times.
Then, every shortcut reduces our distance from a phrase in M k M_{k} by log ⁡ n \log n .
This distance is initially at most log 2 ⁡ n \log^{2}n .
Therefore, we will use a shortcut at most O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) times.
Once we stop using shortcut for any reason, we are guaranteed to terminate with a valid output within O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) applications of J J .
The total running time is therefore O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) , as required. In order to implement the above in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) time, we also require a mechanism for testing in constant time if a phrase P P is in M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} .
This can be obtained by storing a machine word M P M_{P} for every phrase in 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} , where the i i ’th bit indicates whether or not P P is in M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} for k = 1.5 i k=1.5^{i} . 4.2 Data Structure for Naive Jumping To prove Lemma 3.1 , we study the process of following the source phrase of the phrase P P containing i i , ’jumping’ to the index aligned with i i within the occurrence of P P ending with the source.
To formalize this, we define a jumping rule J ⁡ ( i ) J(i) .
Our ultimate goal is to find an index j j with r j < k r_{j}<k and S ⁡ [ i ] = S ⁡ [ j ] S[i]=S[j] .
Intuitively, we make progress towards this goal when we jump to an index with a lower r r -value, and lose progress if we jump to an index with a higher r r -value.
This motivates us to define jumping rules that do not lose progress, in the following sense. 4.4 Legal Jumping Rule. For an index i ∈ [ 1 . . n ] i\in[1..n] , we say that j ≤ i j\leq i is a legal jump for i i if S ⁡ [ i ] = S ⁡ [ j ] S[i]=S[j] and r j ≤ r i r_{j}\leq r_{i} . For a phrase P i = S [ a i . . b i ] P_{i}=S[a_{i}..b_{i}] with source P j = S [ a j . . b j ] P_{j}=S[a_{j}..b_{j}] , we denote δ P i = b i − b j \delta_{P_{i}}=b_{i}-b_{j} .
We define the jumping rule J J based on δ P i \delta_{P_{i}} . 4.5 Jumping Rule J J . For an index i i contained in the phrase P P , we define J ⁡ ( i ) = i − δ P J(i)=i-\delta_{P} .
If P P does not have a source, J ⁡ ( i ) J(i) is undefined. The following lemma follows directly from the definition of LZ-end. 4.6 . J J is a legal jumping rule. Proof. Let i i be an index contained in reference phrase P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] with source P ′ = S [ a ′ . . b ′ ] P^{\prime}=S[a^{\prime}..b^{\prime}] .
Since S [ a . . b ] = S ( b ′ − | P | . . b ′ ] S[a..b]=S(b^{\prime}-|P|..b^{\prime}] , we have that S ⁡ [ i ] = S ⁡ [ b − r i ] = S ⁡ [ b ′ − r i ] = S ⁡ [ J ⁡ ( i ) ] S[i]=S[b-r_{i}]=S[b^{\prime}-r_{i}]=S[J(i)] .
Also, since b ′ b^{\prime} is a phrase boundary, we have r J ⁡ ( i ) ≤ b ′ − J ⁡ ( i ) = ( b − δ P ) − ( i − δ P ) = b − i = r i r_{J(i)}\leq b^{\prime}-J(i)=(b-\delta_{P})-(i-\delta_{P})=b-i=r_{i} , as required.
∎ Since J ⁡ ( i ) J(i) is defined based on the phrase containing i i , our algorithms are often required to find the phrase containing an index i i .
This can be supported in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) time and O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space by storing { a i ∣ i ∈ [ z ] } \{a_{i}\mid i\in[z]\} in a balanced search tree.
We would like to support this query slightly faster.
To this end, we provide the following data structure. 4.7 . 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} can be processed in O ⁡ ( z ​ log ⁡ n z ) O(z\log\frac{n}{z}) time to construct a data structure that given i i returns j j such that i ∈ [ a j . . b j ] i\in[a_{j}..b_{j}] in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time.
The data structure uses O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space. Proof. We partition the universe [ 1 . . n ] [1..n] into z z uniform pieces U 0 , U 1 , … ​ U z − 1 U_{0},U_{1},\ldots U_{z-1} such that U x = ( x n z . . ( x + 1 ) n z ] U_{x}=(x\frac{n}{z}..(x+1)\frac{n}{z}] .
We scan the elements of A 𝒵 = { a i ∣ i ∈ [ z ] } A_{\mathcal{Z}}=\{a_{i}\mid i\in[z]\} and partition them in O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) time to obtain A x = U x ∩ A 𝒵 A_{x}=U_{x}\cap A_{\mathcal{Z}} for every x ∈ [ 0 . . z − 1 ] x\in[0..z-1] .
We construct a balanced search tree over each of A x A_{x} in O ⁡ ( | A x | ​ log ⁡ | A x | ) = O ⁡ ( | A x | ​ log ⁡ n z ) O(|A_{x}|\log|A_{x}|)=O(|A_{x}|\log\frac{n}{z}) time and O ⁡ ( | A x | ) O(|A_{x}|) space.
The total construction time is O ⁡ ( z ​ log ⁡ n z ) O(z\log\frac{n}{z}) and the total space is O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) , as required.
We also store, for every x ∈ [ 0 . . z − 1 ] x\in[0..z-1] , the maximal index a x ∗ a^{*}_{x} in A 𝒵 ∩ [ 1 . . x n z ] A_{\mathcal{Z}}\cap[1..x\frac{n}{z}] and the corresponding phrase P x ∗ P^{*}_{x} starting in a x ∗ a^{*}_{x} .
These values can be computed straightforwardly in O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) time. Given a query index i i , we first find in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time the value x x such that i ∈ U x i\in U_{x} .
It can be easily verified that a j a_{j} such that P j P_{j} contains i i is either in U x U_{x} or is a x ∗ a^{*}_{x} .
We use the balanced search tree of U x U_{x} to find the predecessor of i i in U x U_{x} and check which of the two candidates contains i i .
The query time is O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) , and then verify which one of the candidates contains i i in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
∎ Given an index i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] , we can use Lemma 4.7 to find the phrase P P containing i i in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time.
Since every phrase P i P_{i} is stored alongside the index j j indicating its source phrase P j P_{j} , we can compute δ P i \delta_{P_{i}} in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time given P i P_{i} , by accessing the array storing the phrases alongside their reference data. We proceed to define the function 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{pre} , which is used to define the second jumping rule used in the naive jumping part. 4.8 The function 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{pre} , see Fig. 2 . For a string S S and two integers b ≤ e ∈ [ 1 . . n ] b\leq e\in[1..n] we define 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) as follows.
If b b and e e are contained in the same phrase P ∈ 𝒵 P\in\mathcal{Z} , and P P has a source, then 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) = 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( J ⁡ ( b ) , J ⁡ ( e ) ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e)=\mathsf{pre}(J(b),J(e)) .
Otherwise, 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) = b \mathsf{pre}(b,e)=b . Additionally, we define δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( b , e ) = b − 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(b,e)=b-\mathsf{pre}(b,e) In words, 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) is obtained by finding previous occurrences of S [ b . . e ] S[b..e] by following the source links of 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} , until it is no longer possible.
Halting this process can occur either because b b and e e are separated by a phrase boundary, or because the phrase P P containing [ b . . e ] [b..e] has no source (which can only occur if P = S ⁡ [ b ] = S ⁡ [ e ] P=S[b]=S[e] ).
The left endpoint of the occurrence on which we stop in this process is 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) .
Since 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) is obtained by applying J J repeatedly, the following is a consequence of Lemma 4.6 . 4.9 . Let [ b . . e ] ⊆ [ 1 . . n ] [b..e]\subseteq[1..n] be an interval.
For every i ∈ [ b . . e ] i\in[b..e] , it holds that i − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( b , e ) i-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(b,e) is a legal jump for i i . We will define a jumping rule M M that relies on δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{} .
Unlike δ P \delta_{P} , that is essentially given alongside P P in 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} , computing δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( b , e ) \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(b,e) from 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} require some non-trivial work.
A naive approach would be to check if [ b . . e ] [b..e] contains a phrase boundary, and if it does not - proceed according to the recursive definition.
This may result in O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) time for computing a single 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) value.
In Section 6 , we present the following algorithm, which will be instrumental for efficiently computing δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{} -dependent jumping rules. 4.10 . Given 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} an LZ-End factorization of a string S S and a set of pairs I ⊆ [ 1 . . n ] 2 I\subseteq[1..n]^{2} such that e ∈ [ b . . b + n z ] e\in[b..b+\frac{n}{z}] for every ( b , e ) ∈ I (b,e)\in I , we can compute 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e) for every [ b . . e ] ∈ I [b..e]\in I in O ⁡ ( ( z + | I | ) ​ log 2 ⁡ ( n / z ) ) O((z+|I|)\log^{2}(n/z)) time.
Here, z = | 𝒵 | z=|\mathcal{Z}| and n = | S | n=|S| . In order to define M M , we need to introduce the halved canonical partition of an interval.
This partition appears under a variety of names in compressed random access data structures (in particular in [ 18 ] ). 4.11 Canonical Partition, Halved Canonical Partition. For an interval [ a . . b ] [a..b] of integers, we define the canonical partition of [ a . . b ] [a..b] as the following intervals. 1. For every i ∈ [ 0 . . log ( b − a 2 ) ] i\in[0..\log(\frac{b-a}{2})] , the interval [ a + 2 i − 1 . . min ( a + 2 i + 1 − 2 , b − a 2 ) ] [a+2^{i}-1..\min(a+2^{i+1}-2,\frac{b-a}{2})] 2. For every i ∈ [ 0 . . log ( b − a 2 ) ] i\in[0..\log(\frac{b-a}{2})] , the interval [ max ( b − 2 i + 1 + 2 , b − a 2 ) . . b − 2 i + 1 ] [\max(b-2^{i+1}+2,\frac{b-a}{2})..b-2^{i}+1] We define the Halved Canonical Partition based on the Canonical Partition.
The halved canonical partition of [ a . . b ] [a..b] is obtained by taking the canonical partition and splitting every interval larger than 1 into two equal length interval.
Formally, for every interval I = [ x . . y ] I=[x..y] in the canonical partition of [ a . . b ] [a..b] such that | I | > 1 |I|>1 , the halved canonical partition of [ a . . b ] [a..b] contains [ x . . x + | I | / 2 ) [x..x+|I|/2) and [ x + | I | / 2 . . y ] [x+|I|/2..y] . a a b b Figure 3 : A demonstration of a canonical partition. The key property of the canonical partition is that for an index i ∈ [ a . . b ] i\in[a..b] , the interval I I that contains i i in the canonical partition has length at most m = min ⁡ ( r i , ℓ i ) + 1 m=\min(r_{i},\ell_{i})+1 .
It immediately follows that in the halved canonical partition, the length of the interval containing i i is bounded by max ⁡ ( m / 2 , 1 ) \max(m/2,1) .
We are now ready to define the jumping rule M M . 4.12 Jumping Rule M M . Let i i be an index contained in a marked phrase P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] .
Let [ x . . y ] [x..y] be the interval in the halved canonical partition of [ a . . b ] [a..b] that contains i i .
We define M ⁡ ( i ) = i − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( x , y ) M(i)=i-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(x,y) . It follows from observation 4.9 that M M is a legal jumping rules. 4.13 . M M is a legal jumping rule. Notice that M ⁡ ( i ) = i − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( b , e ) M(i)=i-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(b,e) transforms i ∈ [ b . . e ] i\in[b..e] into an interval of size | [ b . . e ] | |[b..e]| that contains a phrase boundary.
Therefore M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) has a phrase boundary that is quite close to it either from the left or from the right, inducing a significant decrease either of r r -value or of ℓ \ell -value compared to i i .
Formally, we prove the following useful property of M M . 4.14 . For an index i i that is contained in a phrase P ∈ M P\in M , denote j = M ⁡ ( i ) j=M(i) .
It holds that r j ≤ m r_{j}\leq m or ℓ j ≤ m \ell_{j}\leq m where m = min ⁡ ( r i / 2 , ℓ i / 2 ) m=\min(r_{i}/2,\ell_{i}/2) . Proof. Let [ x . . y ] [x..y] be the interval in the halved canonical partition of P P containing i i .
Notice that | [ x . . y ] | ≤ max ( m , 1 ) |[x..y]|\leq\max(m,1) .
If | [ x . . y ] | = 1 |[x..y]|=1 it must be the case that S ⁡ [ j ] S[j] is a phrase of length 1.
Therefore r j = ℓ j = 0 ≤ m r_{j}=\ell_{j}=0\leq m , as required. Otherwise, we have | [ x . . y ] | ≤ m |[x..y]|\leq m .
Let x ′ = 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( x , y ) x^{\prime}=\mathsf{pre}(x,y) and y ′ = x ′ + | [ x . . y ] | y^{\prime}=x^{\prime}+|[x..y]| .
The index j = M ⁡ ( i ) j=M(i) is in [ x ′ . . y ′ ] [x^{\prime}..y^{\prime}] , and from the definition of 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{pre} , there is some phrase boundary b ′ ∈ [ x ′ . . y ′ ] b^{\prime}\in[x^{\prime}..y^{\prime}] .
If j ≤ b ′ j\leq b^{\prime} , then r j ≤ b ′ − j ≤ | [ x ′ . . y ′ ] | = | [ x . . y ] | ≤ m r_{j}\leq b^{\prime}-j\leq|[x^{\prime}..y^{\prime}]|=|[x..y]|\leq m .
Otherwise, j > b ′ j>b^{\prime} and we have ℓ j ≤ j − b ′ ≤ | [ x ′ . . y ′ ] | ≤ m \ell_{j}\leq j-b^{\prime}\leq|[x^{\prime}..y^{\prime}]|\leq m .
∎ claim 4.14 implies that if our sequence of applications of J J reaches upon some index j j in a marked phrase, then M ⁡ ( j ) M(j) is a valid output for Lemma 3.1 .
Our first goal will be to define a ’good’ set M M , such that every sufficiently long sequence of J J applications eventually reaches M M (Roughly, see Lemma 4.16 for the precise definition).
In order to exploit this property, our data structure has to support efficient computation of M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) for an index i i in a marked phrase.
We present the following data structure for efficient M M computation. 4.15 . Given 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} , we can construct a data structure of size O ⁡ ( | M | ​ log ⁡ n z ) O(|M|\log\frac{n}{z}) that given i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] , and the phrase P P containing i i , computes M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
The data structure can be constructed in O ⁡ ( ( M ​ log ⁡ n z + z ) ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O((M\log\frac{n}{z}+z)\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time. Proof. We apply Lemma 4.10 to compute for every P = S [ a . . b ] ∈ M P=S[a..b]\in M and every I = [ x . . y ] I=[x..y] in the halved canonical partition of P P the value P I = 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( x , y ) P_{I}=\mathsf{pre}(x,y) in O ⁡ ( ( | M | ​ log ⁡ n z + z ) ​ log 2 ​ n ) O((|M|\log\frac{n}{z}+z)\log^{2}n) total time (Due to Lemma 2.1 , the halved canonical partition of every P ∈ 𝒵 P\in\mathcal{Z} has O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) intervals).
Notice every query interval is contained in some phrase P i P_{i} , and | P i | ≤ n z |P_{i}|\leq\frac{n}{z} according to Lemma 2.1 .
Therefore, every ( b , e ) (b,e) query has e ∈ [ b . . b + n z ] e\in[b..b+\frac{n}{z}] , and this is a legal input for Lemma 4.10 . We then compute for each interval I = [ x . . y ] I=[x..y] in the halved canonical partition of P P the value δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( I ) = x − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( x , y ) \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(I)=x-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(x,y) , and store those values in an array A P A_{P} specifically constructed for P P .
We store in each phrase P P a link to its corresponding array A P A_{P} . Given an index i i and its containing phrase P P , we can retrieve the interval I I containing i i in the halved canonical partition of P P in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time using O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) bitwise operations on a , b , i a,b,i (We need the most significant bit set to 1 1 in i − a i-a if i i is in the first half of P P , or the most significant bit set to 1 1 in b − i b-i otherwise).
We can then use A P A_{P} to find δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( I ) \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(I) and return M ⁡ ( i ) = i − 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( I ) M(i)=i-\mathsf{pre}(I) .
If P P is not linked to an array A P A_{P} , we reach upon the conclusion that P ∉ M P\notin M , and therefore M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) is undefined. The construction time is dominated by O ⁡ ( ( | M | ​ log ⁡ n z + z ) ​ log 2 ​ n ) O((|M|\log\frac{n}{z}+z)\log^{2}n) , the query time is O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) for applying a predecessor query, and the size is O ⁡ ( M ​ log ⁡ n z ) O(M\log\frac{n}{z}) for storing all δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{} values.
∎ 4.3 Finding a Good Set of Marked Phrases Our first goal is to find a good set M M of marked phrases.
Before formally defining a good set (see Lemma 4.16 ), let us provide some intuition.
We wish to find a set M M that is both sparse and covering .
We require M M to be small, since we will support a query for M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) using Lemma 4.15 , which consumes O ⁡ ( z + | M | ​ log ⁡ n z ) O(z+|M|\log\frac{n}{z}) space.
Since we wish to have O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space complexity, we need to have | M | ∈ O ⁡ ( z / log ⁡ n z ) |M|\in O(z/\log\frac{n}{z}) . As for the covering property, we would like to guarantee that every long enough sequence of steps i , J ⁡ ( i ) , J 2 ​ ( i ) , … i,J(i),J^{2}(i),\ldots eventually reaches some index contained in a phrase of M M .
Instead, we will obtain M M that satisfies a weaker, yet sufficient, notion of covering.
Namely, we will guarantee that every long enough sequence of steps either reaches a phrase of M M , or reaches an index with small enough r r -value. Formally, we prove the following. 4.16 . There is an algorithm that given 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} , outputs a set M M of size O ⁡ ( z / log ⁡ n z ) O(z/\log\frac{n}{z}) such that for every index i i with r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) for some k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , there is x ∈ [ 1 . . log 2 n z ] x\in[1..{\log}^{2}\frac{n}{z}] such that either j = J x ​ ( i ) j=J^{x}(i) is contained in a phrase of M M , or r j < k r_{j}<k . The algorithm works in O ⁡ ( z ​ log ⁡ n z ) O(z\log\frac{n}{z}) time. We introduce the concept of a bad jump, that corresponds to a step J ⁡ ( i ) J(i) that remains in the same exponential level. 4.17 Bad Jump, Good Jump. Let i i be an index and let k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} be the unique integer power of 1.5 1.5 such that r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) .
We say that i i is a k k -bad jump if j = J ⁡ ( i ) j=J(i) has r j ≥ k r_{j}\geq k .
If i i is not a k k -bad jump, we say i i is a k k -good jump. When k k is clear from context or irrelevant, we omit it from the notion of k k -bad jumps and k k -good jumps, using bad jump and good jumps instead. The existence of bad jumps is the main obstacle in finding a value j j such that r j < k r_{j}<k via iterative application of J J .
The pathological case would be where the sequence i , J ⁡ ( i ) , J 2 ​ ( i ) , … , J x ​ ( i ) i,J(i),J^{2}(i),\ldots,J^{x}(i) consists only of bad jumps for high values of x x .
However, we show that bad jumps are very structured.
Namely, we show that the phrase P ′ P^{\prime} that contains J ⁡ ( i ) J(i) for some k k -bad jump i i can be decided only based on the phrase P P containing i i and k k .
In other words, all k k -bad jumps within a phrase P P share some uniform behavior.
We exploit this structure to efficiently cover every long sequence of bad jumps. We make the following claim regarding bad jumps. 4.18 . Let k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} .
Let P P be a phrase and let i i and i ′ i^{\prime} be two k k -bad jumps in P P with r i , r i ′ ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i},r_{i}^{\prime}\in[k..1.5k) .
Then, J ⁡ ( i ) J(i) and J ⁡ ( i ′ ) J(i^{\prime}) are in the same phrase. Proof. For an illustration, see Fig. 4 .
Assume i < i ′ i<i^{\prime} .
Denote j = J ⁡ ( i ) j=J(i) and j ′ = J ⁡ ( i ′ ) j^{\prime}=J(i^{\prime}) .
Since both r i r_{i} and r i ′ r_{i^{\prime}} are in [ k ..1.5 k ) [k..1.5k) , and i i and i ′ i^{\prime} are in the same phrase, we have i ′ − i < 0.5 ​ k i^{\prime}-i<0.5k .

It can be seen that the existence of such phrase boundary leads to r j < k r_{j}<k . Following Lemma 4.18 , we introduce the terminology of a k k -bad parent.
For a phrase P P and k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , we call ’the k k -bad parent’ of P P the unique phrase P ′ P^{\prime} such that every bad jump i i in P P with r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) has J ⁡ ( i ) ∈ P ′ J(i)\in P^{\prime} (uniqueness follows from Lemma 4.18 ).
If there is no such bad jump in P P , then P P does not have a k k -bad parent.
Notice that P ′ ≠ P P^{\prime}\neq P , since J ⁡ ( i ) J(i) is necessarily in a phrase strictly to the left of the phrase containing i i .
As implied by the parent terminology, the k k -bad parent relation induces a forest structure over the phrases of 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} .
We denote as T k T_{k} the forest with vertices being the phrases of 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} , and the parent of every P P is the k k -bad parent of P P . In Section 7 , we prove the following. 4.19 . Given 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} , there is a data structure supporting the following query: Given P ∈ 𝒵 P\in\mathcal{Z} and k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , return the k k -bad parent of P P , or report that P P does not have a k k -bad parent.
The construction time of the data structure is O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O(z\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) , the space is O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) and the query time is O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) . This value of c c exists due to the pigeonhole principle.

So far, we have guaranteed that if we repeatedly apply J J starting from some index i i , we will either reach a marked phrase or reduce the r r -value to the next exponential level within O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) jumps. Now, we introduce ’shortcuts’, allowing us to find J log ⁡ n z ​ ( i ) J^{\log\frac{n}{z}}(i) of an index i i in constant time rather than by computing J J iteratively step-by-step.
The shortcut mechanism can only be applied if the next log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} steps are all ’bad’.
This will allow us to retrieve a valid output for the naive jumping part in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time, as if one of the next log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} steps are not bad - we can afford to make these steps one-by-one as we reach a good enough index after reaching a good step. Notice that every index i i contained in a phrase P P is ( 0 , k ) (0,k) -bad.

We proceed to show that the ( x , k ) (x,k) -bad interval can be computed efficiently. 4.24 . Let P P be a phrase, let k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} and let x x be a non-negative integer.

We use Lemma 4.23 to compute the ( x , k ) (x,k) -interval of a phrase P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] iteratively. Firstly, the ( 0 , k ) (0,k) -bad interval is trivially [ a . . b ] [a..b] .

We accumulate the δ \delta values of the ancestors of P P as we ascend up the tree T k T_{k} , so we have access to δ y ​ ( P ) \delta_{y}(P) when computing the ( y , k ) (y,k) -bad interval of P P .
It follows that we can compute the ( y , k ) (y,k) -bad interval from the ( y − 1 , k ) (y-1,k) -bad interval in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
In total, the time to obtain the ( x , k ) (x,k) -bad interval is O ⁡ ( x ) O(x) , as required.

From P 0 P_{0} to P 2 P_{2} the algorithm advances by applying J J , which leads the algorithm to the bad parent of the current node at every step (assuming that all steps are bad).
Since P 2 P_{2} is in M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} , it has a pointer to its log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} ancestor in T k T_{k} (and auxiliary information that allows check if all next log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} jumps from the current index are bad).
Then, we keep applying shortcut jumps until we finally reach a layer that is within log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} from a marked layer ( P 2 , P 3 , P 4 P_{2},P_{3},P_{4} ).
When we are finally close enough to a marked layer, we return to jumping one-by-one ( P 4 , P 5 , P 6 P_{4},P_{5},P_{6} ).
At every initial point throughout this process, we may terminate prematurely since some jump was good.
In this event, the containing phrase is not the parent of the previously visited phrase in T k T_{k} , but the good jumps leads to an index with a small r r -value. Proof. We start by describing the construction of the data structure.
We construct the set M M of O ⁡ ( z / log ⁡ n z ) O(z/\log\frac{n}{z}) marked phrases using Lemma 4.16 , and then construct the data structure for calculating M ⁡ ( i ) M(i) for an index i i in a marked phrase using Lemma 4.15 .
We store a binary array A M A_{M} of size z z representing M M with A M ​ [ i ] = 1 A_{M}[i]=1 if and only if P i ∈ M P_{i}\in M .

We also construct the data structure of Lemma 4.19 for finding k k -bad parents in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
The construction of Lemma 4.19 requires O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O(z\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time and O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space. For every k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , we construct T k T_{k} and do the following.
If the depth of T k T_{k} is less than log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} , we do not store any additional information.
Otherwise, we pick some c ∈ [ 0 . . log n z ) c\in[0..\log\frac{n}{z}) such that the number of phrases in T k T_{k} of depth that has remainder c c modulo log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} is at most z / log ⁡ n z z/\log\frac{n}{z} .
Such c c should exist due to the pigeonhole principle.
Let M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} be the set of vertices in T k T_{k} with height c c modulo log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} .
For every phrase P ∈ M k ′ P\in M^{\prime}_{k} , we store: 1. The ( log ⁡ n z − 1 , k ) (\log\frac{n}{z}-1,k) -bad interval of P P 2. δ ( log ⁡ n z − 1 ) ​ ( P ) \delta_{(\log\frac{n}{z}-1)}(P) 3. The distance from P P to the nearest marked ancestor of P P 4. The ( log ⁡ n z , k ) (\log\frac{n}{z},k) -bad parent of P P . While computing M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} for all k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , the values of m P m_{P} can be set without asymptotically affecting the running time. We use O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) space for each P ∈ M k ′ P\in M^{\prime}_{k} so for each value of k k we use O ⁡ ( | M k ′ | ) = O ⁡ ( z / log ⁡ n z ) O(|M^{\prime}_{k}|)=O(z/\log\frac{n}{z}) space for a total of O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space over all k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} .
We also store the word m P m_{P} for each P ∈ 𝒵 P\in\mathcal{Z} which requires additional O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space. Finding the correct modulo c c takes O ⁡ ( | T k | ) = O ⁡ ( z ) O(|T_{k}|)=O(z) time.
Computing the ( log ⁡ n z , k ) (\log\frac{n}{z},k) -bad parent and δ ( log ⁡ n z − 1 ) ​ ( P ) \delta_{(\log\frac{n}{z}-1)}(P) can be straightforwardly done in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time for every vertex in M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} by traversing log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} edges towards the root of T k T_{k} and accumulating the δ \delta values of the visited nodes.
Computing the distance to the nearest marked ancestor for every P ∈ M k ′ P\in M^{\prime}_{k} , and the nearest strict ancestor in M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} could be implemented in O ⁡ ( | T k | ) = O ⁡ ( z ) O(|T_{k}|)=O(z) time by a simple iteration over T k T_{k} . Computing the ( log ⁡ n z − 1 , k ) (\log\frac{n}{z}-1,k) -bad interval is done in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time per node in M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} by applying Lemma 4.24 .
The total running time per k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} is O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ⋅ | M k ′ | ) = O ⁡ ( z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}\cdot|M^{\prime}_{k}|)=O(z) .
Over all values of k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , the total running time is O ⁡ ( z ​ log ⁡ n z ) O(z\log\frac{n}{z}) . Query. For an illustration of the query algorithm, see Fig. 7 .
Let i i be some index query and let k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} such that r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) .
