Notice that each of the above conditions can be checked in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time given j j and P P (using the endpoints of P P , the array representation of M M , and m P m_{P} , respectively). We find J ​ ( i ) , J 2 ​ ( i ) ​ … J(i),J^{2}(i)\ldots as follows.
For the input index i i , we use Lemma 4.7 to find the phrase containing i i in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time. Now, we show how given some i ′ i^{\prime} and the phrase P P containing i ′ i^{\prime} , we can find J ⁡ ( i ′ ) J(i^{\prime}) and the phrase P ′ P^{\prime} containing J ⁡ ( i ′ ) J(i^{\prime}) in constant time.
To be more precise, we will either find the phrase P ′ P^{\prime} containing J ⁡ ( i ′ ) J(i^{\prime}) , or guarantee that one of our conditions are met for J ⁡ ( i ′ ) J(i^{\prime}) .
Assume we already have i ′ i^{\prime} and P P at hand.
We find j = J ⁡ ( i ′ ) j=J(i^{\prime}) using δ P \delta_{P} in constant time, and we also find the bad parent P ′ P^{\prime} of P P in constant time using Lemma 7.7 .
If j j is in P ′ P^{\prime} , we have the phrase containing j j at hand.
Otherwise, we to have r j < k r_{j}<k due to the definition of a bad parent.
We can therefore return j j and terminate.
It follows that we can calculate J ⁡ ( i ) , J 2 ​ ( i ) , … ​ J y ​ ( i ) J(i),J^{2}(i),\ldots J^{y}(i) such that J y ​ ( i ) J^{y}(i) satisfies one of our conditions in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z + y ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}+y) time. If we reach, at any point, some j j with r j < k r_{j}<k , the algorithm returns j j and terminates.
If we reach some j j in a marked phrase P ∈ M P\in M , the algorithm returns M ⁡ ( j ) M(j) and terminates. If we end up reaching some i ′ ∈ P i^{\prime}\in P for some P P in M k ′ M^{\prime}_{k} , we stop iteratively applying J J and switch to the following strategy instead.
We use the stored data of P P to check, in constant time, if i ′ i^{\prime} is in the ( log ⁡ n z − 1 , k ) (\log\frac{n}{z}-1,k) -bad interval of P P , and if the distance from P ′ P^{\prime} to its nearest ancestor in M M is less than log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} .
We consider several cases depending on results of these two tests. Case 1. The distance to the next marked ancestor is more than log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} , and i ′ i^{\prime} is in the ( log ⁡ n z − 1 , k ) (\log\frac{n}{z}-1,k) -bad interval of P P . In this case, we compute j ′ = J log ⁡ n z ​ ( i ′ ) j^{\prime}=J^{\log\frac{n}{z}}(i^{\prime}) in constant time, using δ log ⁡ n z − 1 ​ ( P ) \delta_{\log\frac{n}{z}-1}(P) and applying Corollary 4.22 .
We also have the phrase P ′ P^{\prime} containing j ′ j^{\prime} stored in P P as the ( log ⁡ n z , k ) (\log\frac{n}{z},k) -bad parent of P P , so we can find it P ′ P^{\prime} in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
Notice that P ′ ∈ M k ′ P^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}_{k} since it has depth c c modulo log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} .
Therefore, the algorithm will keep iteratively using shortcuts as long as we remain in Case 1. Case 2. i ′ i^{\prime} is not in the ( log ⁡ n z − 1 , k ) (\log\frac{n}{z}-1,k) -bad interval of P P . In this case, we switch back to applying J J as at the start of the algorithm.
We keep applying J J until we reach an index j j with r j < k r_{j}<k .
We will later show that this process is guaranteed to terminate within O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) steps. Case 3. The distance to the next marked ancestor is less than log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} . In this case, we switch back to applying J J until we reach an index j j contained in a marked phrase P ′ ∈ M P^{\prime}\in M or with r j < k r_{j}<k .
When this occurs, we return j j if r j < k r_{j}<k or M ⁡ ( j ) M(j) if j j is contained in P ′ ∈ M P^{\prime}\in M .
We will later show that we are guarantee to find such j j within O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) steps. Running time. The first part of the algorithm runs in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z + y ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}+y) time where j = J y ​ ( i ) j=J^{y}(i) is the first index we meet satisfying one of the specified conditions.
Notice that if we jump from i ∈ P i\in P to a vertex i ′ = J ⁡ ( i ) i^{\prime}=J(i) that is not the k k -bad parent of P P , the algorithm terminates because r i ′ < k r_{i^{\prime}}<k by the definition of a bad parent.
Therefore, the phrases containing i , J ⁡ ( i ) , J 2 ​ ( i ) ​ … i,J(i),J^{2}(i)... correspond to an upwards path in T k T_{k} , as long as the algorithm does not terminate.
It follows that we are guaranteed to reach a phrase P P that has level c mod ( log ⁡ n z ) c\bmod(\log\frac{n}{z}) in T k T_{k} , or terminate for some other reason within O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) steps.
We have shown that y ∈ O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) y\in O(\log\frac{n}{z}) and therefore the running time of the first part of the algorithm is O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) . If the first part of the algorithm reaches some index j j with r j < k r_{j}<k or in a phrase P ∈ M P\in M , the algorithm terminates in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) , returning either j j or M ⁡ ( j ) M(j) . Otherwise, the algorithm reaches some index j j in a phrase P ∈ M k ′ P\in M^{\prime}_{k} .
In this case, the algorithm checks into which of the three cases j j falls and applies either Case 1, Case 2, or Case 3. Case 1 is applied in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
We claim that it can be applied at most log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} times.
After log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} application, we reach some j = J y ​ ( i ) j=J^{y}(i) for y ≥ log 2 ⁡ n z ≥ x y\geq\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}\geq x .
Since we did not reach or skip over a marked phrase of P ∈ M P\in M , we have that J x ​ ( i ) ∉ M J^{x}(i)\notin M and therefore the r r -value of J x ​ ( i ) J^{x}(i) is less than k k .
Since applying J J never increases the r r -value ( Lemma 4.6 ), we have r j ≤ r J x ​ ( i ) < k r_{j}\leq r_{J^{x}(i)}<k , which means that the algorithm terminates and returns j j . It remains to analyze the running times of Case 2 and Case 3.
Notice that each of them, by definition, occurs only once - when each of these two cases occur, they define the behavior of the remainder of the algorithm. Case 2 occurs where i ′ i^{\prime} is not in the ( log ⁡ n z − 1 , k ) (\log\frac{n}{z}-1,k) -bad interval of P ′ P^{\prime} .
Therefore, there is some y ∈ [ 0 . . log n z ) y\in[0..\log\frac{n}{z}) such that J y ​ ( i ′ ) J^{y}(i^{\prime}) is a good jump, which means that we will reach an index satisfying one of the desired conditions with O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) jumps. Case 3 occurs when the distance to the nearest marked ancestor is less than log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} .
Clearly, either there is y ∈ [ log ⁡ n z ] y\in[\log\frac{n}{z}] such that J y ​ ( i ′ ) J^{y}(i^{\prime}) is a good jump, leading to termination as in the previous case, or all log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} jumps following i ′ i^{\prime} are contained in the ancestors of P P containing i ′ i^{\prime} , in the corresponding order.
In the latter case, we are guaranteed to reach some j j contained in P ∈ M P\in M within log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} steps, as required. In conclusion, the first part of the algorithm is executed in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time, Case 1 may be applied up to O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) times, with each application taking O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time, and Case 2 and 3 may take O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time.
The total running time of the query algorithm is O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) . Correctness. The algorithm terminates by finding some j = J y ​ ( i ) j=J^{y}(i) that either has r j < k r_{j}<k or that is contained in a marked phrase.
In the first case, the algorithm returns j j which is clearly a valid output.
In the latter case, the algorithm returns M ⁡ ( j ) M(j) which satisfies either r j < r i / 2 < 1.5 ​ k / 2 < k r_{j}<r_{i}/2<1.5k/2<k or ℓ j ≤ r i / 2 < 1.5 ​ k \ell_{j}\leq r_{i}/2<1.5k due to claim 4.14 .
Therefore, M ⁡ ( j ) M(j) is a valid output.
In both cases, we have S ⁡ [ x ] = S ⁡ [ i ] S[x]=S[i] and r x ≤ r i r_{x}\leq r_{i} (where x x is the output index) since x x is obtained by iteratively applying legal jumping rules (either J J or M M ) starting from i i .
∎ 5 The Stable Part In this section, we prove Lemma 3.2 . The jumping rule s ​ J sJ . We define the jumping rule s ​ J sJ .
For every P = S [ a . . b ] ∈ 𝒵 P=S[a..b]\in\mathcal{Z} , we denote L ( P ) = S [ a . . a + ⌊ 2 3 | P | ⌋ − 1 ] L(P)=S[a..a+\left\lfloor\frac{2}{3}|P|\right\rfloor-1] the prefix of size ⌊ 2 3 ​ | P | ⌋ \left\lfloor\frac{2}{3}|P|\right\rfloor of P P .
Let us denote the set 𝕃 = { L ⁡ ( P ) ∣ P ∈ 𝒵 } \mathbb{L}=\{L(P)\mid P\in\mathcal{Z}\} . We define s ​ J ​ ( i ) sJ(i) only for indices i i that are contained in some L ∈ 𝕃 L\in\mathbb{L} . 5.1 Jumping Rule s ​ J sJ . For an index i ∈ [ 1 . . n ] i\in[1..n] contained in the phrase P [ a . . b ] P[a..b] .
If i ∈ [ a . . a + ⌊ 2 3 | P | ⌋ − 1 ] i\in[a..a+\left\lfloor\frac{2}{3}|P|\right\rfloor-1] , we define s ​ J ​ ( i ) = i − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( a , a + ⌊ 2 3 ​ | P i | ⌋ − 1 ) sJ(i)=i-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(a,a+\left\lfloor\frac{2}{3}|P_{i}|\right\rfloor-1) .
Otherwise, s ​ J ​ ( i ) sJ(i) is undefined. Although not explicitly stated as a jumping rule, the logic expressed by s ​ J sJ is used in [ 18 ] as well.
It follows from observation 4.9 that s ​ J sJ is a legal jumping rule. On several occasions throughout the section, we are required to prove that s ​ J ​ ( i ) sJ(i) is defined for some index i i .
The following provides a clean characterization of the event in which s ​ J ​ ( i ) sJ(i) is defined for an index i i . 5.2 . s ​ J ​ ( i ) sJ(i) is defined for an index i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] if and only if ℓ i ≤ 2 ​ r i \ell_{i}\leq 2r_{i} . As we did for M M in Section 4 , we use Lemma 4.10 to construct the following data structure for computing s ​ J sJ . 5.3 . Given 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} , we can construct a data structure of size O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) that given i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] and the phrase P P containing i i , computes s ​ J ​ ( i ) sJ(i) in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
The data structure can be constructed in O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O(z\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time. Proof. We apply Lemma 4.10 to compute for every P = S [ a . . b ] ∈ 𝒵 P=S[a..b]\in\mathcal{Z} the value P a = 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( a , a + ⌊ 2 3 ​ | P | ⌋ − 1 ) P_{a}=\mathsf{pre}(a,a+\left\lfloor\frac{2}{3}|P|\right\rfloor-1) in O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O(z\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time.
Notice that every pair ( b , e ) (b,e) in the query set satisfies [ b . . e ] ⊆ [ a i . . b i ] [b..e]\subseteq[a_{i}..b_{i}] for some P i P_{i} , and we can assume due to Lemma 2.1 that | P i | ≤ n z |P_{i}|\leq\frac{n}{z} .
Therefore, we indeed have e ∈ [ b . . b + n z ] e\in[b..b+\frac{n}{z}] for every query pair, so this is a valid input for Lemma 4.10 . We then compute for each phrase P P the value δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( P ) = δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( a , a + ⌊ 2 3 ​ | P | ⌋ − 1 ) = a − P a \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(P)=\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(a,a+\left\lfloor\frac{2}{3}|P|\right\rfloor-1)=a-P_{a} .

Now, given an index i i and the phrase P P containing i i , we can simply inspect the array to obtain δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( i ) \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(i) and return s ​ J ​ ( i ) = i − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( i ) sJ(i)=i-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(i) .
The construction time is dominated by O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O(z\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) , the query time is O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) , and the size is O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) for storing all δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( P ) \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(P) values.
∎ We assume that for every phrase P P with L ( P ) = S [ b . . e ] L(P)=S[b..e] , we have access to δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( b , e ) \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(b,e) .
This is achieved as a consequence of applying Lemma 4.10 with the query set 𝕃 \mathbb{L} . High-level idea for proving Lemma 3.2 Let us fix some index i 0 i_{0} as the input for the stable part, and denote as k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} the unique integer power of 1.5 1.5 such that r i 0 ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i_{0}}\in[k..1.5k) .
We are interested in finding the first integer x ∗ x^{*} such that s ​ J x ∗ ​ ( i 0 ) sJ^{x^{*}}(i_{0}) has r r -value less than k k .
As in Section 4 , we will show that there is a tree structure capturing the bad sequences of applications of s ​ J sJ .
Unlike in Section 4 , where we had a separate tree T k T_{k} for every possible k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} capturing the bad sequences with r r -values roughly k k , here we will have a single tree T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} capturing all bad sequences. This allows us to store T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} and preprocess it for level-ancestor queries.
We provide a characterization of the sought value x ∗ x^{*} such that s ​ J x ∗ ​ ( i 0 ) sJ^{x^{*}}(i_{0}) has r r -values less than k k .
The characterization allows us to assign a numerical values δ ℓ ​ ( P ) \delta_{\ell}(P) and δ r ​ ( P ) \delta_{r}(P) to every phrase P P .
Then, when given i 0 i_{0} contained in some phrase P 0 P_{0} , we can identify x ∗ x^{*} as a level in T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} where the aggregated sums of δ ℓ ​ ( P ) \delta_{\ell}(P) and δ r ​ ( P ) \delta_{r}(P) on the path from P 0 P_{0} to its ancestor in this level reach some threshold.
We compute this sum in logarithmic time using level-ancestor queries.
Given x ∗ x^{*} , and auxiliary information stored in T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} , we can obtain s ​ J x ∗ ​ ( i 0 ) sJ^{x^{*}}(i_{0}) in constant time. Existence of x ∗ x^{*} Recall that s ​ J ​ ( i ) sJ(i) is not defined for every index in i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] .
Therefore, it is not immediate that x ∗ x^{*} exists, as it may be the case that when repeatedly applying s ​ J sJ , we reach an index i ′ = s ​ J x ′ ​ ( i 0 ) i^{\prime}=sJ^{x^{\prime}}(i_{0}) for which s ​ J ​ ( i ′ ) sJ(i^{\prime}) is undefined.
In the following lemma, we prove that if this process reaches such an index, we have r i ′ < k r_{i^{\prime}}<k , which means that x ′ = x ∗ x^{\prime}=x^{*} .
In [ 18 ] , the following properties are proven, but they are hidden within the proof of another claim (See Lemma 4.4 in [ 18 ] ).
We repeat their proof for the sake of self-containment. 5.4 Stable Part Properties. Let i i be an index with r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) and ℓ i < 1.5 ​ k \ell_{i}<1.5k .

For the rest of the section, we show how to efficiently find x ∗ x^{*} and s ​ J x ∗ ​ ( i 0 ) sJ^{x^{*}}(i_{0}) . Tree structure. We override the definition of bad jumps from Section 4 to match our current context.
First, we define the bad phrase and the bad suffix of L ∈ 𝕃 L\in\mathbb{L} as follows. 5.6 Bad Suffix ,Bad Phrase. For L = S [ a . . b ] ∈ 𝕃 L=S[a..b]\in\mathbb{L} , the bad phrase P ′ P^{\prime} of L L is the phrase containing s ​ J ​ ( b ) = b − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( a , b ) sJ(b)=b-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(a,b) .
The bad suffix of L L is the suffix [ c . . b ] [c..b] containing exactly the indices i i such that s ​ J ​ ( i ) sJ(i) is in P ′ P^{\prime} .
We lift this terminology to phrases.
The bad suffix (resp. bad phrase) of a phrase P P is the bad suffix (resp. bad phrase) of L ⁡ ( P ) L(P) (notice that the bad suffix of P P is typically not a suffix of P P ). Notice that unlike in the previous section, where we associated each phrase P P with possibly | 𝕂 | ∈ O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) |\mathbb{K}|\in O(\log\frac{n}{z}) ’bad’ phrases, here we only define one bad phrase for P P . In the following, we establish the importance of bad phrases and bad suffixes, linking them to the terminal step that finally leads to an index with sufficiently small r r -value. 5.7 . Let P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] be a phrase.
Let i i be an index in L ⁡ ( P ) L(P) with r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) for some k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} and i ′ = s ​ J ​ ( i ) i^{\prime}=sJ(i) .
If r i ′ ≥ k r_{i^{\prime}}\geq k , then i i is in the bad suffix of L L . Proof. Denote L = L ( P ) = S [ a . . c ] L=L(P)=S[a..c] and notice that c = a + ⌊ 2 3 ​ | P | ⌋ − 1 c=a+\left\lfloor\frac{2}{3}|P|\right\rfloor-1 .
Notice that r i = ( c − i ) + ⌈ 1 3 ​ | P | ⌉ r_{i}=(c-i)+\left\lceil\frac{1}{3}|P|\right\rceil .
Also, since i i is in the phrase P P , we have r i < | P | r_{i}<|P| .
It follows that c − i < 2 3 ​ r i c-i<\frac{2}{3}r_{i} .
Let 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ( a , c ) = S [ a ′ . . c ′ ] \mathsf{pre}(a,c)=S[a^{\prime}..c^{\prime}] .
Clearly, c ′ c^{\prime} is in the rightmost phrase that intersects [ a ′ . . c ′ ] [a^{\prime}..c^{\prime}] .
We also have that s ​ J ​ ( c ) = c ′ sJ(c)=c^{\prime} and c − i = c ′ − i ′ c-i=c^{\prime}-i^{\prime} since s ​ J sJ subtracts δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( a , c ) \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(a,c) from both i i and c c .
We will now show that if i ′ i^{\prime} is not in the same phrase as c ′ c^{\prime} , then r i ′ < k r_{i^{\prime}}<k .
If i ′ i^{\prime} and c ′ c^{\prime} are not in the same phrase, there is a boundary between i ′ i^{\prime} and c ′ c^{\prime} .
This would imply that r i ′ ≤ | i ′ − c ′ | = | i − c | < 2 3 ​ r i < k r_{i^{\prime}}\leq|i^{\prime}-c^{\prime}|=|i-c|<\frac{2}{3}r_{i}<k , as required.
∎ We define a tree structure over the phrases.
Let T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} be a tree with vertices being the phrases of 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} .
For every phrase P P , the parent of P P in T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} is the bad phrase of P P .
We denote the x x ’th ancestor of P P in T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} as π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( x ) ​ ( P ) \mathsf{\pi}^{(x)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P) . Similarly to Section 4 , we have that if applying s ​ J ​ ( i ) sJ(i) does not result in an r r -value below k k , the index i i is in the bad suffix of P P .
Therefore, we focus on analyzing the structure of a sequence of s ​ J ​ ( i ) sJ(i) applications in which every step falls within the bad suffix of the phrase containing it.
We make the following simple observation, showing that the difference in ℓ \ell and r r values as a result of applying s ​ J ​ ( i ) sJ(i) is identical across all i i in the bad suffix of a phrase P P . 5.8 . Let i 1 i_{1} and i 2 i_{2} be two indices in the bad suffix of a phrase P P .
Let j 1 = s ​ J ​ ( i 1 ) j_{1}=sJ(i_{1}) and j 2 = s ​ J ​ ( i 2 ) j_{2}=sJ(i_{2}) .
It holds that ℓ i 1 − ℓ j 1 = ℓ i 2 − ℓ j 2 \ell_{i_{1}}-\ell_{j_{1}}=\ell_{i_{2}}-\ell_{j_{2}} and r i 1 − r j 1 = r i 2 − r j 2 r_{i_{1}}-r_{j_{1}}=r_{i_{2}}-r_{j_{2}} . Proof. Let L = S [ x . . y ] = L ( P ) L=S[x..y]=L(P) .
We have j 1 = i 1 − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( x , y ) j_{1}=i_{1}-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(x,y) and j 2 = i 2 − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( x , y ) j_{2}=i_{2}-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(x,y) .
Therefore, i 1 − i 2 = j 1 − j 2 i_{1}-i_{2}=j_{1}-j_{2} .
Denote P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] and let P ′ = S [ a ′ . . b ′ ] P^{\prime}=S[a^{\prime}..b^{\prime}] be the bad phrase of P P .
By definition, r i 1 = b − i 1 r_{i_{1}}=b-i_{1} , r j 1 = b ′ − j 1 r_{j_{1}}=b^{\prime}-j_{1} , r i 2 = b − i 2 r_{i_{2}}=b-i_{2} , and r j 2 = b ′ − j 2 r_{j_{2}}=b^{\prime}-j_{2} .
It immediately follows that r i 1 − r j 1 = r i 2 − r j 2 r_{i_{1}}-r_{j_{1}}=r_{i_{2}}-r_{j_{2}} .
The claim follows for ℓ \ell -values in a similar manner.
∎ Following Lemma 5.8 , we introduce additional notation to capture the common change in ℓ \ell -values and in r r -values among indices in the bad suffix.
For a phrase P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] , with L ( P ) = L = [ a . . b ′ ] L(P)=L=[a..b^{\prime}] , we define δ ℓ ​ ( P ) = ℓ b ′ − ℓ s ​ J ​ ( b ′ ) \delta_{\ell}(P)=\ell_{b^{\prime}}-\ell_{sJ(b^{\prime})} and δ r ​ ( P ) = r b ′ − r s ​ J ​ ( b ′ ) \delta_{r}(P)=r_{b^{\prime}}-r_{sJ(b^{\prime})} .
Notice that b ′ b^{\prime} , as the last index of L L , is in the bad suffix of L L . The following is directly implied by Lemma 5.8 . 5.9 . Let i i be an index in the bad suffix of a phrase P P and let j = s ​ J ​ ( i ) j=sJ(i) .
It holds that r j = r i − δ r ​ ( P ) r_{j}=r_{i}-\delta_{r}(P) and ℓ j = ℓ i − δ ℓ ​ ( P ) \ell_{j}=\ell_{i}-\delta_{\ell}(P) . We notice that for every P P we have that δ ℓ ​ ( P ) \delta_{\ell}(P) and δ r ​ ( P ) \delta_{r}(P) are always non-negative. 5.10 . For every phrase P ∈ 𝒵 P\in\mathcal{Z} , it holds that δ r ​ ( P ) ≥ 0 \delta_{r}(P)\geq 0 and δ ℓ ​ ( P ) ≥ 0 \delta_{\ell}(P)\geq 0 . Proof. Let P = S [ a . . c ] P=S[a..c] with L ( P ) = S [ a . . b ] L(P)=S[a..b] .
Let a ′ = 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( a , b ) a^{\prime}=\mathsf{pre}(a,b) and b ′ = a ′ + | L ⁡ ( P ) | − 1 b^{\prime}=a^{\prime}+|L(P)|-1 .
By definition, we have s ​ J ​ ( b ) = b − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( a , b ) = b ′ sJ(b)=b-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(a,b)=b^{\prime} .
From the definition of 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{pre} , we have some phrase boundary b ^ ∈ [ a ′ . . b ′ ] \hat{b}\in[a^{\prime}..b^{\prime}] .
Therefore, ℓ b ′ ≤ b ′ − b ^ ≤ b ′ − a ′ = b − a = ℓ b \ell_{b^{\prime}}\leq b^{\prime}-\hat{b}\leq b^{\prime}-a^{\prime}=b-a=\ell_{b} .
It follows that δ ℓ ​ ( P ) = ℓ b − ℓ b ′ ≥ 0 \delta_{\ell}(P)=\ell_{b}-\ell_{b^{\prime}}\geq 0 , as required. The fact that δ r ​ ( P ) ≥ 0 \delta_{r}(P)\geq 0 follows from s ​ J sJ being a legal jumping step ( observation 4.9 ), which implies r b ′ ≤ r b r_{b^{\prime}}\leq r_{b} .
∎ Let us define the set of ( x ) (x) -bad indices of a phrase. 5.11 . Recall that for an index i i in L ⁡ ( P ) L(P) , it holds that s ​ J ​ ( i ) = i − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( a ′ , b ′ ) sJ(i)=i-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}) .
The following follows directly from the definition of an ( x ) (x) -bad jump. 5.12 . If i i is a ( x ) (x) -bad in a phrase P P for some non-negative integer x x , it holds that J x + 1 ​ ( i ) = i − ∑ y = 0 x δ 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ​ ( π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( y ) ​ ( P ) ) J^{x+1}(i)=i-\sum_{y=0}^{x}\delta_{\mathsf{bad}}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(y)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)) . We exploit observation 4.21 to show that we can quickly find s ​ J x + 1 ​ ( i ) sJ^{x+1}(i) , provided that i i is ( x ) (x) -bad. 5.13 . Given T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} , we can construct in O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) time a data structure of size O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) supporting the following query.

The query time is O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) . Proof. We attach to every node of T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} the number Δ 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ′ ​ ( P ) \Delta^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P) that is equal to the sum of δ 𝖻𝖺𝖽 \delta_{\mathsf{bad}} values on the path from P P to the root.
Formally, Δ 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ′ ​ ( P ) = ∑ y = 0 d δ 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ​ ( π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( y ) ​ ( P ) ) \Delta^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)=\sum_{y=0}^{d}\delta_{\mathsf{bad}}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(y)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)) where d d is the depth of P P in T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} .
This concludes the construction of the data structure. Given a query i i , x x , P P , and P ′ = π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( x ) ​ ( P ) P^{\prime}=\mathsf{\pi}^{(x)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P) , we retrieve Δ 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ​ ( P ) \Delta_{\mathsf{bad}}(P) and Δ 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ​ ( π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( x + 1 ) ​ ( P ) ) \Delta_{\mathsf{bad}}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(x+1)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)) from T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} .
Notice that we can find π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( x + 1 ) ​ ( P ) \mathsf{\pi}^{(x+1)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P) in constant time, as the parent of P ′ P^{\prime} .
We compute δ = Δ 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ′ ​ ( π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( x + 1 ) ​ ( P ) ) − Δ 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ′ ​ ( P ) + δ 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ​ ( P ) = ∑ y = 0 x δ 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ​ ( π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( y ) ​ ( P ) ) \delta=\Delta^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bad}}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(x+1)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P))-\Delta^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)+\delta_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)=\sum_{y=0}^{x}\delta_{\mathsf{bad}}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(y)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)) .
We output s ​ J x + 1 ​ ( i ) = i − δ sJ^{x+1}(i)=i-\delta , which is correct by observation 5.12 . The preprocessing time consists of finding all Δ 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ′ \Delta^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bad}} values, which can be done in O ⁡ ( | T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 | ) O(|T_{\mathsf{bad}}|) via a straightforward iteration on T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} (given the values δ 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ​ ( P ) \delta_{\mathsf{bad}}(P) of all phrases).
The total construction time is O ⁡ ( | T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 | ) = O ⁡ ( z ) O(|T_{\mathsf{bad}}|)=O(z) , as required. The query consists of retrieving data stored in the tree.
Assuming that every phrase stores a link to the corresponding tree node, the stored data can be retrieved in constant time, as required.

Notice that δ ℓ ​ ( P ) \delta_{\ell}(P) is always equal to the offset between the occurrence of L ⁡ ( P ) L(P) mapped by 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{pre} and the bad phrase of P P .
The index i 0 i_{0} is in the bad suffix of P 0 P_{0} since ℓ i 0 ≤ δ P 0 \ell_{i_{0}}\leq\delta_{P_{0}} .

Characterizing x ∗ x^{*} . We will use the δ r \delta_{r} and δ ℓ \delta_{\ell} values of the phrases to provide a characterization of the first integer x ∗ x^{*} such that j = s ​ J x ∗ ​ ( i 0 ) j=sJ^{x^{*}}(i_{0}) has r j < k r_{j}<k .
Before formally providing the characterization (see Lemma 5.14 ), let us provide some intuition. Consider the process of iteratively applying s ​ J sJ , starting from i 0 i_{0} until j j is reached.
Before j j is reached, it should be clear that all visited indices are bad, and it follows from Lemma 5.7 that every visited index is in the bad suffix of the phrase containing it.
Therefore, the phrases visited correspond to an upwards path in T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} .
From Corollary 5.9 , it follows that the decrease in ℓ \ell and r r values when we jump from i i to j j is exactly the δ ℓ \delta_{\ell} and δ r \delta_{r} values of the phrase containing i i , respectively. In particular, if i D = s ​ J D + 1 ​ ( i 0 ) i_{D}=sJ^{D+1}(i_{0}) for some integer D D , it holds that ℓ i D = ℓ i 0 − ∑ y = 0 D δ ℓ ​ ( P ( y ) ) \ell_{i_{D}}=\ell_{i_{0}}-\sum_{y=0}^{D}\delta_{\ell}(P^{(y)}) and similarly, r i D = r i 0 − ∑ y = 0 D δ r ​ ( P ( y ) ) r_{i_{D}}=r_{i_{0}}-\sum_{y=0}^{D}\delta_{r}(P^{(y)}) .
The two sums representing the total decrease in ℓ \ell and r r correspond to the sum δ ℓ \delta_{\ell} values and δ r \delta_{r} values of an upwards path from P P in T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} , respectively.
We can use level ancestor queries to quickly identify the lowest ancestor of P P in which the sum crosses a given threshold. A natural threshold to consider for the sum of δ r \delta_{r} is k − r i 0 k-r_{i_{0}} , as once this threshold is surpassed, the r r -value drops below k k , which means that we have reached an index with sufficiently small r r -value.
However, this bound alone is not sufficiently precise.
It may be the case that at some point, before the δ r \delta_{r} values accumulate to k − r i 0 k-r_{i_{0}} , we visit some index j j that is not in the bad suffix of the phrase containing it.
If that is the case, our claim that the r r value decreases according to the δ r \delta_{r} values no longer holds (as its jumps no longer correspond to an upward path).
Fortunately, according to Lemma 5.7 , if such j ′ j^{\prime} is reached, either j ′ j^{\prime} or its successor s ​ J ​ ( j ′ ) sJ(j^{\prime}) have r r -value below k k .
Furthermore, it turns out that the first such j j can be characterized using the accumulated δ ℓ \delta_{\ell} values. To see this relationship between the δ ℓ \delta_{\ell} values in the first j j that is not in a bad suffix, consider first δ ℓ ​ ( P ) \delta_{\ell}(P) (See Fig. 9 ).
Let L = S [ a ′ . . b ′ ] L=S[a^{\prime}..b^{\prime}] be the bad suffix of P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] .
It should be relatively easy to see that δ ℓ ​ ( P ) = a ′ − a \delta_{\ell}(P)=a^{\prime}-a .
Now, it is clear that i i is in the bad suffix of P P if and only if ℓ i = i − a ≥ a ′ − a = δ ℓ P \ell_{i}=i-a\geq a^{\prime}-a=\delta_{\ell_{P}} .
Assume that i i is indeed in the bad suffix of P P and consider the bad phrase P ′ P^{\prime} of P P that contains j = s ​ J ​ ( i ) j=sJ(i) .
We can apply the same reasoning and show that j j is in the bad suffix of P ′ P^{\prime} if and only if ℓ j ≥ δ ℓ ​ ( P ′ ) \ell_{j}\geq\delta_{\ell}(P^{\prime}) .
But when we reached P ′ P^{\prime} , we already decreased the ℓ \ell value of i i by δ ℓ ​ ( P ) \delta_{\ell}(P) .
So, the condition ℓ j ≥ δ ℓ ​ ( P ′ ) \ell_{j}\geq\delta_{\ell}(P^{\prime}) translates to ℓ i ≥ δ ℓ ​ ( P ) + δ ℓ ​ ( P ′ ) \ell_{i}\geq\delta_{\ell}(P)+\delta_{\ell}(P^{\prime}) . This pattern persists inductively, and we have that s ​ J x ​ ( i ) sJ^{x}(i) is in the bad suffix of the phrase containing it ’if and only if’ the sum of δ ℓ \delta_{\ell} values does not exceed ℓ i \ell_{i} .
Again, ’if and only if’ is not completely precise here, because it may be the case that the r r -value drops below k k while visiting a bad suffix.
Recall that we are only guaranteed to have the value of s ​ J sJ well defined for the indices we meet in this process as long as their r r -value exceeds k k ( Corollary 5.5 ). In conclusion, there are two events that may lead to the r r -value dropping below k k for the first time.
One event corresponds to passing a certain threshold of accumulated δ r \delta_{r} values and the other event corresponds to passing a certain threshold of accumulated δ ℓ \delta_{\ell} values.
We need to specifically find the earlier of the two events, as the characterization of the r r -values and the ℓ \ell -values on which we rely persists only as long as none of these two events occur. We formalize the above intuition as follows.
For any phrase P ∈ 𝒵 P\in\mathcal{Z} and integer B B , we define Δ L ​ ( P , B ) = ∑ x = 0 B δ ℓ ​ ( π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( x ) ​ ( P ) ) \Delta_{L}(P,B)=\sum_{x=0}^{B}\delta_{\ell}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(x)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)) .
Similarly, we define Δ R ​ ( P , B ) = ∑ x = 0 B δ r ​ ( π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( x ) ​ ( P ) ) \Delta_{R}(P,B)=\sum_{x=0}^{B}\delta_{r}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(x)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)) . We prove the following fact. 5.14 . Let P P be the phrase containing i 0 i_{0} with ℓ i 0 = L \ell_{i_{0}}=L and r i 0 = R r_{i_{0}}=R .
Let B B be a non-negative integer. 1. If i 0 i_{0} is a ( B ) (B) -bad index, than Δ L ​ ( P , B ) ≤ L \Delta_{L}(P,B)\leq L . 2. If Δ L ​ ( P , B ) ≤ L \Delta_{L}(P,B)\leq L and Δ R ​ ( P , B − 1 ) < R − k \Delta_{R}(P,B-1)<R-k , then i 0 i_{0} is a ( B ) (B) -bad index. Proof. For the sake of clear presentation, we omit P P from the notations Δ L ​ ( P , B ) \Delta_{L}(P,B) and Δ R ​ ( P , B ) \Delta_{R}(P,B) , and write Δ L ​ ( B ) \Delta_{L}(B) and Δ R ​ ( B ) \Delta_{R}(B) instead, respectively. Let us first prove the first statement.
Assume that i 0 i_{0} is B B -bad.
Let j = s ​ J B + 1 ​ ( i 0 ) j=sJ^{B+1}(i_{0}) Since for every x ∈ [ 0 . . B ] x\in[0..B] we have that s ​ J x ​ ( i 0 ) sJ^{x}(i_{0}) is in the bad suffix of π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( x ) ​ ( P ) \mathsf{\pi}^{(x)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P) , Corollary 5.9 yields that ℓ j = L − Δ L ​ ( B ) \ell_{j}=L-\Delta_{L}(B) .
Since ℓ j \ell_{j} is never negative, we have Δ L ​ ( B ) ≤ L \Delta_{L}(B)\leq L as required. We proceed to prove the second claim by induction on B B . Base case. Let P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] and let the bad interval of L ⁡ ( P ) L(P) be S [ a ′ . . c ] S[a^{\prime}..c] .
For B = 0 B=0 , we have that Δ L ​ ( 0 ) = δ ℓ ​ ( P ) \Delta_{L}(0)=\delta_{\ell}(P) .
By definition, ℓ a ′ = a ′ − a \ell_{a^{\prime}}=a^{\prime}-a .
Notice that j ′ = s ​ J ​ ( a ′ ) j^{\prime}=sJ(a^{\prime}) must be the first index of the bad parent of P P , so ℓ j ′ = 0 \ell_{j^{\prime}}=0 .
By Corollary 5.9 , we have δ ℓ ​ ( P ) = a ′ − a \delta_{\ell}(P)=a^{\prime}-a . Recall that L = i 0 − a L=i_{0}-a , and observe that i 0 i_{0} is in the bad suffix of P P if and only if a ′ − a ≤ i − a a^{\prime}-a\leq i-a which is equivalent to Δ ℓ ≤ L \Delta_{\ell}\leq L , as required. Induction step. Assume that the claim is true for B ≥ 1 B\geq 1 .
Assume that Δ L ​ ( B + 1 ) ≤ L \Delta_{L}(B+1)\leq L and Δ R ​ ( B ) ≤ R − k \Delta_{R}(B)\leq R-k .
We have that Δ L ​ ( B ) = Δ L ​ ( B + 1 ) − δ ℓ ​ ( π B ( B ) ​ ( P ) ) \Delta_{L}(B)=\Delta_{L}(B+1)-\delta_{\ell}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(B)}_{B}(P)) and Δ R ​ ( B ) = Δ R ​ ( B ) − δ r ​ ( π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( B − 1 ) ​ ( P ) ) \Delta_{R}(B)=\Delta_{R}(B)-\delta_{r}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(B-1)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)) .
Since δ ℓ \delta_{\ell} and δ r \delta_{r} value of a phrase are never negative ( Lemma 5.10 ), we have that Δ L ​ ( B ) ≤ L \Delta_{L}(B)\leq L and Δ R ​ ( B − 1 ) ≤ R − k \Delta_{R}(B-1)\leq R-k .
Therefore, from the induction hypothesis we have that i 0 i_{0} is B B -bad.
We will show that j = s ​ J B + 1 ​ ( i 0 ) j=sJ^{B+1}(i_{0}) is in the bad suffix of π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( B + 1 ) ​ ( P ) \mathsf{\pi}^{(B+1)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P) . It follows from Corollary 5.9 that ℓ j = L − Δ L ​ ( B ) \ell_{j}=L-\Delta_{L}(B) and that r j = R − Δ R ​ ( B ) r_{j}=R-\Delta_{R}(B) .
Since r j = R − Δ R ​ ( B ) ≥ k r_{j}=R-\Delta_{R}(B)\geq k , we have that s ​ J ​ ( j ) sJ(j) is well defined (i.e., is in j ∈ L ⁡ ( P ) j\in L(P) ) due to Corollary 5.5 .
Since j j is the result of B + 1 B+1 jumps from bad suffixes, we have that j j is in π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( B + 1 ) ( P ) = S [ a ′ . . b ′ ] \mathsf{\pi}^{(B+1)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)=S[a^{\prime}..b^{\prime}] .
So ℓ j = j − a ′ \ell_{j}=j-a^{\prime} .
Recall that δ ℓ ​ ( π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( B + 1 ) ​ ( P ) ) = a ^ − a ′ \delta_{\ell}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(B+1)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P))=\hat{a}-a^{\prime} such that a ^ \hat{a} is the leftmost index in the bad suffix of π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( B + 1 ) ​ ( P ) \mathsf{\pi}^{(B+1)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P) . ∎ We proceed to use Lemma 5.14 to characterize x ∗ x^{*} .
Recall that i 0 i_{0} is the input index with r i 0 = R ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i_{0}}=R\in[k..1.5k) for k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} We also denote L = ℓ i 0 L=\ell_{i_{0}} .
Let P P be the phrase containing i 0 i_{0} .
For an integer D D , we denote Δ R ​ ( D ) = ∑ x = 0 D δ r ​ ( π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( x ) ​ ( P ) ) \Delta_{R}(D)=\sum_{x=0}^{D}\delta_{r}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(x)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)) and Δ L ​ ( D ) = ∑ x = 0 D δ r ​ ( π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( x ) ​ ( P ) ) \Delta_{L}(D)=\sum_{x=0}^{D}\delta_{r}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(x)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)) .
In words, Δ R ​ ( D ) \Delta_{R}(D) (resp. δ L ​ ( D ) \delta_{L}(D) ) is the sum of δ r \delta_{r} values (resp. δ ℓ \delta_{\ell} values) on the path of length D D from P P towards the root of T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} . We denote as D R D_{R} the maximal integer such that Δ R ​ ( D R ) ≤ R − k \Delta_{R}(D_{R})\leq R-k .
Similarly, we denote D L D_{L} as the maximal integer satisfying Δ L ​ ( D L ) ≤ L \Delta_{L}(D_{L})\leq L .
Notice that Δ L ​ ( − 1 ) = Δ R ​ ( − 1 ) = 0 \Delta_{L}(-1)=\Delta_{R}(-1)=0 , and that L ≥ 0 L\geq 0 and R ≥ k R\geq k , so D L D_{L} and D R D_{R} are well defined. We prove the following fact. 5.15 . Let D = max ⁡ ( 0 , min ⁡ ( D L , D R + 1 ) ) D=\max(0,\min(D_{L},D_{R}+1)) .
We have that for every x ∈ [ 0 . . D ] x\in[0..D] , the r r -values of s ​ J x ​ ( i 0 ) sJ^{x}(i_{0}) is at least k k , and for j = s ​ J D + 1 ​ ( i 0 ) j=sJ^{D+1}(i_{0}) we have r j < k r_{j}<k . Proof. We start by considering the case in which either D L = − 1 D_{L}=-1 .
Notice that if this occurs, we have D = 0 D=0 and the claim for x ∈ [ 0 . . D ] = { 0 } x\in[0..D]=\{0\} holds since s ​ J 0 ​ ( i 0 ) = i 0 sJ^{0}(i_{0})=i_{0} and r i 0 ≥ k r_{i_{0}}\geq k .
If D L = − 1 D_{L}=-1 , we have that Δ L ​ ( 0 ) = δ ℓ ​ ( P ) > L \Delta_{L}(0)=\delta_{\ell}(P)>L .
According to Lemma 5.14 , it means that i 0 i_{0} is not ( 0 ) (0) -bad.

∎ Having established the connection between D L D_{L} , D R D_{R} , and the sought value x ∗ x^{*} , we are now interested in constructing a data structure for efficiently computing D L D_{L} and D R D_{R} .
We prove the following. 5.16 . Given T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} , we can construct in O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) time a data structure taking O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space such that given i 0 i_{0} , we can retrieve D L D_{L} and D R D_{R} in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time.
Within the same query time, the data structure also returns the corresponding ancestors π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( x ) ​ ( P ) \mathsf{\pi}^{(x)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P) for the phrase containing P P and x ∈ { D L , D R } x\in\{D_{L},D_{R}\} . Proof. We will describe a data structure with O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) query time.
We will later describe how the running time can be improved to O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) . We build two level-ancestor data structure over the same forest T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} .
In the first data structures, every phrase P P is assigned Δ L ′ ​ ( P ) \Delta^{\prime}_{L}(P) which is the sum of δ ℓ \delta_{\ell} values of phrases on the path from P P to the root (excluding P P ).
Formally, Δ L ′ ​ ( P ) = ∑ i = 1 d δ ℓ ​ ( π B ( i ) ​ ( P ) ) \Delta^{\prime}_{L}(P)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{\ell}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(i)}_{B}(P)) where d d is the depth of P P in T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} . Similarly, we build another level ancestor data structure in which each phrase P P is associated with the sum of δ r \delta_{r} values on the path to the root (excluding P P ).
Formally, Δ R ′ ​ ( P ) = ∑ i = 1 d δ r ​ ( π 𝖻𝖺𝖽 ( i ) ​ ( P ) ) \Delta^{\prime}_{R}(P)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{r}(\mathsf{\pi}^{(i)}_{\mathsf{bad}}(P)) . Given i 0 i_{0} with ℓ i 0 = L \ell_{i_{0}}=L and r i 0 = R r_{i_{0}}=R in phrase P P , we query the level-ancestor data structure for the lowest ancestor A L A_{L} of P P with Δ L ′ ​ ( A L ) ≥ δ ℓ ​ ( P ) + Δ L ′ ​ ( P ) − L \Delta^{\prime}_{L}(A_{L})\geq\delta_{\ell}(P)+\Delta^{\prime}_{L}(P)-L .
Similarly, we query for the lowest ancestor A R A_{R} with Δ R ′ ​ ( A P ) ≥ δ r ​ ( P ) + Δ R ′ ​ ( P ) − ( R − k ) \Delta^{\prime}_{R}(A_{P})\geq\delta_{r}(P)+\Delta^{\prime}_{R}(P)-(R-k) .
A more natural interpretation of A R A_{R} (and A L A_{L} ) is the lowest ancestor of P P such that the path from P P to A P A_{P} (including both endpoints) has total δ r \delta_{r} values at least R − k R-k (resp. the path from P P to A L A_{L} has total δ ℓ \delta_{\ell} values at least L L ).
Clearly, the depth difference between P P and A R A_{R} is exactly D R D_{R} , and the depth difference between P P and A L A_{L} is exactly D L D_{L} (and if either of A L , A R A_{L},A_{R} two does not exist, the corresponding D L D_{L} or D R D_{R} value is − 1 -1 ). We have found each of D L D_{L} and D R D_{R} and the corresponding ancestors using a single weighted ancestor query, so the query running time is O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) . The construction time consists of finding all Δ L ′ ​ ( P ) \Delta^{\prime}_{L}(P) and Δ R ′ ​ ( P ) \Delta^{\prime}_{R}(P) values and constructing two level-ancestor data structures for T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} equipped with these values.
We can compute δ ℓ ​ ( P ) \delta_{\ell}(P) and δ r ​ ( P ) \delta_{r}(P) for every P P in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) straightforwardly by taking index b b such that L ( P ) = S [ a . . b ] L(P)=S[a..b] , compute with b ′ = s ​ J ​ ( b ) b^{\prime}=sJ(b) and subtract the ℓ \ell and r r values of b b and b ′ b^{\prime} (to find the r r and ℓ \ell values of b ′ b^{\prime} , we need access to the phrase containing b ′ b^{\prime} which we have as it is the parent of P P in T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} ). Computing Δ L ′ ​ ( P ) \Delta^{\prime}_{L}(P) and Δ R ′ ​ ( P ) \Delta^{\prime}_{R}(P) for all P P can be implemented in O ⁡ ( | T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 | ) = O ⁡ ( z ) O(|T_{\mathsf{bad}}|)=O(z) time by an iteration on T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} , keeping track of cost of root-to-node path at every step.
The total construction time is therefore O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) , as required. Improving the query time to O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) . In the above approach, the query boils down to performing weighted ancestor queries.
We notice that the weighted ancestor queries required by the algorithm have the following property:
When we query for the weighted ancestor of P P with weight X X , the weight of P P is at most X + n z X+\frac{n}{z} . Let us justify this claim.
The algorithm queries for the first ancestor of P P such that the path from P P to P ′ P^{\prime} has weight at most R − k R-k or L L , where L L and R R are ℓ \ell and r r values of the query index i 0 i_{0} .
Due to Lemma 2.1 , we can assume that the phrase containing i 0 i_{0} has length at most n z \frac{n}{z} .
It follows that L , R ≤ n z L,R\leq\frac{n}{z} and the property we stated holds. Assume that the weights assigned to the tree T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} never result in a child having the same weight as its parent.
Under this assumption, (and noticing that all weights are integers) we have that each child has weight at least 1 more than its parent.
It follows that the answer for our query is at unweighted hight at most n z \frac{n}{z} above P P .
We can find this answer by preprocessing T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} for constant time unweighted level ancestor queries [ 4 ] , and binary searching the range [ 0 . . n z ] [0..\frac{n}{z}] for the first parent with weight below a certain threshold in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time. We can enforce the assumption that a child never has the same weight as its parent by contracting edges between same weight parent-child paris.

Similarly, the algorithm finds the lowest ancestor such that the sub om δ r \delta_{r} values is at least k − R k-R .

According to Lemma 5.15 , we have r s ​ J x ​ ( i 0 ) ≥ k r_{sJ^{x}(i_{0})}\geq k for every x ∈ [ 0 . . D ] x\in[0..D] and j = s ​ J D + 1 j=sJ^{D+1} has r j < k r_{j}<k .
If D = 0 D=0 , we simply return j = s ​ J ​ ( i 0 ) j=sJ(i_{0}) using Lemma 5.3 .
Otherwise, we have D = min ⁡ ( D L , D R + 1 ) D=\min(D_{L},D_{R}+1) and therefore Δ L ​ ( D ) ≤ L \Delta_{L}(D)\leq L and Δ R ​ ( D − 1 ) ≤ R − k \Delta_{R}(D-1)\leq R-k .
It follows from Lemma 5.14 that i 0 i_{0} is D D -bad.
We can therefore use the data structure of Lemma 5.13 to obtain j = s ​ J D + 1 ​ ( i 0 ) j=sJ^{D+1}(i_{0}) in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time, and return it as a valid output. The construction time and the space of the data structure consist of the construction and space complexities of the data structures of Lemmas 5.3 , 4.7 , 5.16 and 5.13 , which are all bounded by O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space and O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O(z\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time.
The query time is dominated by one query to each of the data structures of Lemmas 5.16 and 5.13 , which is dominated by O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) , as required. ∎ 6 Batched pre Computation In this section, we prove Lemma 4.10 . We use the following data structure that has been designed (in a slightly more restricted variant) by Farach and Thorup [ 10 ] . For completeness, we provide a complete proof in Appendix A . 6.1 . There is a data structure storing a set X X of balanced search trees where each tree in X X has elements in [ 1 . . n ] [1..n] .
The data structure supports the following updates: 1. Initialize X X as a single empty balanced search tree. 2. Apply an insertion/deletion operation to a tree in X X . 3. 𝖲𝗉𝗅𝗂𝗍 ⁡ ( T , a ) \mathsf{Split}(T,a) : Remove T 1 T_{1} from X X , and add T 1 T_{1} over the set T ∩ [ 1 . . a ] T\cap[1..a] and T 2 T_{2} over the set T ∩ [ a + 1 . . n ] T\cap[a+1..n] to X X . 4. 𝖬𝖾𝗋𝗀𝖾 ⁡ ( T 1 , T 2 ) \mathsf{Merge}(T_{1},T_{2}) : Remove T 1 T_{1} and T 2 T_{2} from X X , and add a tree T T to X X over the set T 1 ∪ T 2 T_{1}\cup T_{2} . 5. 𝖲𝗁𝗂𝖿𝗍 ⁡ ( T , δ ) \mathsf{Shift}(T,\delta) : Add δ \delta to every item in the tree T T . The initialization takes O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
Each update is implemented in amortized time O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ​ log ⁡ N ) O(\log n\log N) where N N is an upper bound on the size of each tree in X X . P t P_{t} P t ′ P_{t^{\prime}} P t P_{t} P t ′ P_{t^{\prime}} Figure 11 : An illustration of the algorithm of Lemma 4.10 .
Every vertex v v in the tree is represented as an interval with a dot end representing 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) \mathsf{val}(v) and a line end representing E ⁡ ( v ) E(v) .
At iteration t t , we take all the intervals with dots contained in P t P_{t} and shift them to the source of P t P_{t} (displayed as P t ′ P_{t^{\prime}} ).
Then, every remaining vertex with E E value that exceeds into P t P_{t} is removed and reported (in the figure, there is only one such vertex). We first present an algorithm with O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n ) O(z\log^{2}n) running time.
We will later show how to replace the log ⁡ n \log n factors with log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} .
Denote I = ( b 1 , e 1 ) , ( b 2 , e 2 ) , … , ( b | I | , e | I | ) I=(b_{1},e_{1}),(b_{2},e_{2}),\ldots,(b_{|I|},e_{|I|}) .
Denote B = { b i ∣ i ∈ [ | I | ] } B=\{b_{i}\mid i\in[|I|]\} .
Let Z = { a i ∣ i ∈ [ z ] } Z=\{a_{i}\mid i\in[z]\} be the starting indices of the phrases of the Lempel-Ziv End partition 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} of S S .
We define a node for every query in I I as follows.
For every ( b i , e i ) ∈ I (b_{i},e_{i})\in I , we define a node v i v_{i} with initial value 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v i ) = b i \mathsf{val}(v_{i})=b_{i} .
The node v i v_{i} contains as auxiliary information E ⁡ ( v i ) = e i E(v_{i})=e_{i} and I ⁡ ( v i ) = i I(v_{i})=i .
We initialize a tree data of Lemma 6.1 and add to X X a balanced tree T T containing all the vertices { v i ∣ i ∈ [ | I | ] } \{v_{i}\mid i\in[|I|]\} .
The tree is sorted in increasing order of 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) \mathsf{val}(v) . For every node v ∈ T v\in T , we store as auxiliary information the value M ⁡ ( v ) M(v) which is the maximal E ⁡ ( v ) E(v) value of a node in the subtree rooted at v v . We maintain the auxiliary information as the tree is manipulated by the operations of the data structure.
Throughout the algorithm, when a node v v is in a tree T ′ T^{\prime} that is applied a 𝖲𝗁𝗂𝖿𝗍 ⁡ ( T , δ ) \mathsf{Shift}(T,\delta) operation, we consider E ⁡ ( v ) E(v) to be shifted by the same amount.
However, I ⁡ ( v ) I(v) does not change throughout the running time of the algorithm. The algorithm (See Fig. 11 ) The algorithm initializes an array A A of size | I | |I| intended to store the answers to each query.
Initially, A ⁡ [ i ] = 𝗇𝗂𝗅 A[i]=\mathsf{nil} for every i ∈ [ | I | ] i\in[|I|] .
We will show that when the algorithm terminates, A ⁡ [ i ] = 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b i , e i ) A[i]=\mathsf{pre}(b_{i},e_{i}) .
Throughout the algorithm, we will remove nodes from T T .
When we remove a node v v from T T , we assign A ⁡ [ I ⁡ ( v ) ] = 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) A[I(v)]=\mathsf{val}(v) . The algorithm consists of z z iterations indexed in decreasing order from z z to 1 1 .
We maintain the invariant that at the start of every iteration, X X contains a single balanced search tree.
Initially, T = T z T=T_{z} is the tree described above.
For every t ∈ [ 1 . . z ] t\in[1..z] , in decreasing order, the algorithm applies the following. 1. Let T T be the single tree in X X at the start of iteration t t . Let P t = S [ a t . . b t ] P_{t}=S[a_{t}..b_{t}] be the t t -th phrase in 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} with source S [ a t ′ . . b t ′ ] S[a^{\prime}_{t}..b^{\prime}_{t}] , and let δ t = b t − b t ′ \delta_{t}=b_{t}-b^{\prime}_{t} .
If P t P_{t} does not have a source, let δ t = 0 \delta_{t}=0 . 2. If δ t ≠ 0 \delta_{t}\neq 0 : (a) Split: split T T into two trees T 1 = T ∩ [ 1 . . a t − 1 ] T^{1}=T\cap[1..a_{t}-1] and T 2 = T ∩ [ a t . . b t ] T^{2}=T\cap[a_{t}..b_{t}] using a 𝖲𝗉𝗅𝗂𝗍 ⁡ ( T , a t − 1 ) \mathsf{Split}(T,a_{t-1}) operation. In each iteration, the algorithm applies O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) operations of type 𝖲𝗉𝗅𝗂𝗍 \mathsf{Split} , 𝖲𝗁𝗂𝖿𝗍 \mathsf{Shift} , and 𝖬𝖾𝗋𝗀𝖾 \mathsf{Merge} to the data structure.
Additionally, the subroutine deletes nodes, with each node deleted at most once throughout the running time of the algorithm.
Navigating to nodes with maximal E E value can be done in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) using the auxiliary M M information.
Since we apply | I | |I| deletions and z z operations of types 𝖲𝗉𝗅𝗂𝗍 , 𝖲𝗁𝗂𝖿𝗍 , 𝖬𝖾𝗋𝗀𝖾 \mathsf{Split},\mathsf{Shift},\mathsf{Merge} , the total running time is O ⁡ ( ( z + | I | ) ​ log 2 ​ n ) O((z+|I|)\log^{2}n) . We proceed to show that the algorithm correctly computes all 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{pre} values.

∎ We now prove the following invariant, intuitively stating that when we change the value of a node throughout the algorithm, the newly created node represents an equivalent query. 6.3 . Let v v be a vertex in T T at any time throughout the algorithm.
It holds that 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b I ⁡ ( v ) , e I ⁡ ( v ) ) = 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) , E ⁡ ( v ) ) \mathsf{pre}(b_{I(v)},e_{I(v)})=\mathsf{pre}(\mathsf{val}(v),E(v)) . Proof. At the start of the algorithm, the claim immediately follows, as in the initial tree for every i ∈ [ | I | ] i\in[|I|] we have B ⁡ ( v i ) = b i B(v_{i})=b_{i} and E ⁡ ( v i ) = e i E(v_{i})=e_{i} . Consider an iteration t ∈ [ 2 . . z ] t\in[2..z] in which the 𝗏𝖺𝗅 \mathsf{val} and E E values of a vertex v v with I ⁡ ( v ) = i I(v)=i change.
Let v ′ v^{\prime} be the modified v v with the new 𝗏𝖺𝗅 \mathsf{val} and E E values.
Denote b = v ​ a ​ r ​ ( v ) b=var(v) , e = E ⁡ ( b ) e=E(b) , b ′ = v ​ a ​ r ​ ( v ′ ) b^{\prime}=var(v^{\prime}) and e ′ = E ⁡ ( v ′ ) e^{\prime}=E(v^{\prime}) .
Let P t = S [ a t . . b t ] P_{t}=S[a_{t}..b_{t}] be the t t ’th phrase in 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} and let S [ a t ′ . . b t ′ ] S[a^{\prime}_{t}..b^{\prime}_{t}] be the source of P t P_{t} .
Notice that all vertices that have their value change in the t t -th iteration have both their 𝗏𝖺𝗅 \mathsf{val} and E E decreased by δ t = b t − b t ′ \delta_{t}=b_{t}-b^{\prime}_{t} .
So in particular, we have b ′ = b − δ t b^{\prime}=b-\delta_{t} and e ′ = e − δ t e^{\prime}=e-\delta_{t} . Due to Lemma 6.2 , we have that e ≤ b t e\leq b_{t} .
Since the value of v v was changed by the subroutine, it must be the case that v v is in the tree T 2 T^{2} created in the t t ’th iteration, which contains only vertices with 𝗏𝖺𝗅 \mathsf{val} value at least a t a_{t} .
In conclusion, we have shown that a t ≤ b ≤ e ≤ b t a_{t}\leq b\leq e\leq b_{t} .
This means that b b and e e are both contained in the same phrase P t P_{t} .
Therefore, by the definition of 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{pre} , we have 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) = 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b − δ t , e − δ t ) = 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b ′ , e ′ ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e)=\mathsf{pre}(b-\delta_{t},e-\delta_{t})=\mathsf{pre}(b^{\prime},e^{\prime}) .
By the induction hypothesis, we have 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b , e ) = 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b i , e i ) \mathsf{pre}(b,e)=\mathsf{pre}(b_{i},e_{i}) .
It follows that 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b ′ , e ′ ) = 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b i , e i ) \mathsf{pre}(b^{\prime},e^{\prime})=\mathsf{pre}(b_{i},e_{i}) , as required.
∎ The last invariant we need is that when the algorithm removes a node, the value assigned in A A is the correct 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{pre} value for the query represented by this node. 6.4 . When a node v v is removed from T T , it holds that 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) , E ⁡ ( v ) ) = 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) \mathsf{pre}(\mathsf{val}(v),E(v))=\mathsf{val}(v) . Proof. Let t t be the iteration in which v v was removed.
Let P t = S [ a t . . b t ] P_{t}=S[a_{t}..b_{t}] be the t t ’th phrase in 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} .
If P t P_{t} does not have a source, we have P = [ a t ] P=[a_{t}] .
In this case, the fact that v v is removed implies E ⁡ ( v ) ≥ ( a t ) E(v)\geq(a_{t}) .
From Lemma 6.2 we have that E ⁡ ( v ) < b t = a t E(v)<b_{t}=a_{t} , which implies E ⁡ ( v ) = a t E(v)=a_{t} .
We also have from Lemma 6.2 that 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) ≤ E ⁡ ( v ) \mathsf{val}(v)\leq E(v) . If 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) = E ⁡ ( v ) \mathsf{val}(v)=E(v) , then we have that [ 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ( v ) . . E ( v ) ] = [ a t . . a t ] [\mathsf{val}(v)..E(v)]=[a_{t}..a_{t}] is completely contained in the phrase P t P_{t} which has no source. Otherwise, we have 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) < E ⁡ ( v ) = a t \mathsf{val}(v)<E(v)=a_{t} , which means that 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) \mathsf{val}(v) is not in the phrase P t P_{t} , while E ⁡ ( v ) E(v) is in the phrase P t P_{t} .
It follows that 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) \mathsf{val}(v) and E ⁡ ( v ) E(v) are in different phrases. We have shown that in both cases, 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) \mathsf{val}(v) and E ⁡ ( v ) E(v) are not contained in the same phrase which has a source.
Therefore, 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) , E ⁡ ( v ) ) = 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) \mathsf{pre}(\mathsf{val}(v),E(v))=\mathsf{val}(v) from the definition of 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{pre} .
∎ Let us conclude the correctness of the algorithm.
In every iteration, we either delete vertices or change their value.
When the algorithm concludes, all vertices are deleted since the first phrase in 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} must be S ⁡ [ 1..1 ] S[1..1] .
Therefore, at iteration 1 1 we will remove all vertices v v with 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) ≥ 1 \mathsf{val}(v)\geq 1 , which is satisfied for all remaining vertices by Lemma 6.2 . When the vertex v v with I ⁡ ( v ) = i I(v)=i is deleted, we set A ⁡ [ i ] = 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) A[i]=\mathsf{val}(v) .
By Lemma 6.4 , it holds that 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) = 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) , E ⁡ ( v ) ) \mathsf{val}(v)=\mathsf{pre}(\mathsf{val}(v),E(v)) .
By Lemma 6.3 , it holds that 𝗏𝖺𝗅 ⁡ ( v ) = 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ⁡ ( b i , e i ) \mathsf{val}(v)=\mathsf{pre}(b_{i},e_{i}) , as required. Substituting log ⁡ n \log n factors with log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} . We now describe how to implement the above approach, replacing the log ⁡ n \log n factors with log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} factors.
We uniformly partition the domain [ 1 . . n ] [1..n] into z z intervals U i = ( i ⋅ n z . . ( i + 1 ) n z ] U_{i}=(i\cdot\frac{n}{z}..(i+1)\frac{n}{z}] , each of size n z \frac{n}{z} .
Instead of maintaining one tree containing all queries, we maintain a separate tree T i T_{i} maintaining queries ( b , e ) (b,e) with b ∈ U i b\in U_{i} .
The queries are sorted according to the primary order b b , and the secondary order e e . When we implement a split, the split interval corresponds to some phrase P i = S [ a i . . b i ] P_{i}=S[a_{i}..b_{i}] .
Due to Lemma 2.1 , we have that | P i | ≤ n z |P_{i}|\leq\frac{n}{z} , which means that [ a i . . b i ] [a_{i}..b_{i}] spans at most two intervals U x U_{x} , U x + 1 U_{x+1} .
We partition the range [ a i . , b i ] [a_{i}.,b_{i}] into at most two sub-ranges, and remove each of them from the corresponding U i U_{i} .
Similarly, when we merge the shifted version of [ a i . . b i ] [a_{i}..b_{i}] back intro a tree, we first find in constant time the at most two intervals U x , U x + 1 U_{x},U_{x+1} intersecting the (shifted) range, split [ a i . . b i ] [a_{i}..b_{i}] accordingly and merge each piece into the corresponding U x U_{x} , U x + 1 U_{x+1} .
Finding the U x U_{x} intersecting [ a i . . b i ] [a_{i}..b_{i}] in both of these cases can be done in constant time. We would maintain the invariant that every tree is of size O ⁡ ( ( n z ) 2 ) O((\frac{n}{z})^{2}) , so each search tree operation costs O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) .
Since T i T_{i} contains only queries ( b , e ) (b,e) with b ∈ U i b\in U_{i} , the number of distinct b b values in T i T_{i} is bounded by n z \frac{n}{z} .
Notice that the distance between b b and e e never changes throughout the lifetime of a node.
Since initially we have e − b ≤ n z e-b\leq\frac{n}{z} for every query, we have that this inequality holds for every query throughout the running time of the algorithm.
Therefore, for every b b there are at most n z \frac{n}{z} possible values of e e , and the possible distinct ( b , e ) (b,e) pairs that can be in a tree T i T_{i} is bounded by ( n z ) 2 (\frac{n}{z})^{2} .
In T i T_{i} , every node with values ( b , e ) (b,e) stores, as auxiliary information, a linked list of all i i such that the query q i q_{i} in the initial input currently corresponds to this node.
Initially, each node is attached with a single i i , but we may combine nodes with identical b b and e e values throughout the running time of the algorithm, connecting their corresponding lists. Whenever the size of a tree T i T_{i} exceeds 2 ​ ( n z ) 2 2(\frac{n}{z})^{2} , we iterate T i T_{i} and merge every duplication we find in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
Since | T i | ≥ ( n z ) 2 |T_{i}|\geq(\frac{n}{z})^{2} and we have less than ( n z ) 2 (\frac{n}{z})^{2} distinct values of T i T_{i} that may belong in T i T_{i} , we are guaranteed to find at least ( n z ) 2 (\frac{n}{z})^{2} duplications in this process.
It follows that we find a duplication in amortized constant time.
After applying the above, the size of T i T_{i} is again bounded by ( n z ) 2 (\frac{n}{z})^{2} . Since every merge increase the total number of queries across all trees by 1, we will have at most z z merges throughout the running time of the algorithm, each found and implemented in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time. Another change that the partition into intervals introduces to the algorithm is in the tests for the maximal value e e across all queries in our data structures.
We would not like to check all T i T_{i} ’s individually, and even storing a heap over all of T i T_{i} ’s maximal elements is too costly (This will introduce an extra cost of O ⁡ ( log ⁡ z ) O(\log z) ).
Instead, recall that every ( b , e ) (b,e) pair has e ∈ [ b . . b + n z ] e\in[b..b+\frac{n}{z}] .
We also recall that at every point throughout the running time of the algorithm, we have some prefix of the domain still containing queries, so U 1 , U 2 , … ​ U x U_{1},U_{2},\ldots U_{x} are active, and every U y U_{y} with y > x y>x is empty.
Together, these two properties imply that the maximal e e values is either in T x T_{x} or in T x − 1 T_{x-1} , so it is sufficient to query both T i T_{i} and T i − 1 T_{i-1} for their maximal value in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time. 7 Data Structure for Finding a Bad Parent In this section, we prove Lemma 4.19 .
We will describe a data structure with running time O ⁡ ( z ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n z + ( log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) 2 ) ) O(z(\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}+(\log\log n)^{2})) .
Then, we will discuss how to replace the log ⁡ log ⁡ n \log\log n factor with log ⁡ log ⁡ n z \log\log\frac{n}{z} .
In this section, k k -bad parent corresponds to the definition presented in Section 4 (following Lemma 4.18 ), i.e., the k k -bad parent of P P is the parent of P P in T k T_{k} . Our data structure consist of two main component.
The primary ingredient is a mechanism that allows us, given a query phrase P P and k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , to retrieve a set of O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) candidates for being the k k -bad parent of P P in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
The second, complementary component is a verification algorithm, allowing us to check if a given phrase is the k k -bad parent of P P in constant time. We start by presenting the verification algorithm.

This is not fast enough for our final goal - which is supporting such queries in constant time.

Now, given a query phrase P = [ a . . b ] P=[a..b] and k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , we can find J ⁡ ( b − k ) J(b-k) (If b − k b-k is not in P P , then P P clearly do not have a k k -bad parent) in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
We can then use the data structure of Lemma 4.7 to find the phrase P ′ P^{\prime} containing J ⁡ ( b − k ) J(b-k) in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time. Due to Lemma 7.1 , we know that if P P has a k k -bad parent, it must be P ′ P^{\prime} .
We check if P ′ P^{\prime} is the bad parent of P P in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time using Corollary 7.2 .
∎ We are now ready to describe the main part of the data structure for finding the k k -bad parent of a given P P .
We define the notion of critical bad values. 7.4 Critical Bad Values. For a phrase P P in 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} and k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} such that | P | ≥ 2 ​ k |P|\geq 2k .
Let k ′ = 1.5 ​ k k^{\prime}=1.5k be the successor of k k in 𝕂 \mathbb{K} .
We say that k k is a critical bad value for P P if P P has a k k -bad parent P ′ P^{\prime} , and P ′ P^{\prime} is not the k ′ k^{\prime} -bad parent of P P . Intuitively, one can consider the process of fixing a phrase P P and increasing k k from min ⁡ ( 𝕂 ) \min(\mathbb{K}) to max ⁡ ( 𝕂 ) \max(\mathbb{K}) .
The critical bad values of P P are the values of k k where the k k -bad parent changes in this process (excluding very large k k values with k > | P | / 2 k>|P|/2 ).
We prove several useful properties of the k k -critical bad values.
We start by showing that for k k -critical values, the k k -bad parents of P P is of size roughly k k . 7.5 . Let P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] be a phrase in 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} with source S [ a ′ . . b ′ ] S[a^{\prime}..b^{\prime}] .
Let k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} be a critical value of P P .
Let P k P_{k} be the k k -bad parent of P P .
It holds that | P k | ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) |P_{k}|\in[k..1.5k) Proof. It should be clear that | P k | ≥ k |P_{k}|\geq k , as P k P_{k} contains an index j j with r j ≥ k r_{j}\geq k .
Assume to the contrary that | P k | ≥ 1.5 ​ k |P_{k}|\geq 1.5k .
Since P k = S [ a k . . b k ] P_{k}=S[a_{k}..b_{k}] is a k k -bad parent, there is an index i ∈ [ a . . b ] i\in[a..b] with j = J ( i ) ∈ [ a k . . b k ] j=J(i)\in[a_{k}..b_{k}] and r i , r j ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i},r_{j}\in[k..1.5k) .
Let us assume that i i is maximal among the indices satisfying these conditions.
This means that either r i = k r_{i}=k or r j = k r_{j}=k , as otherwise i + 1 i+1 satisfies these conditions as well.
Notice that in both cases, it must hold that r j = k r_{j}=k , since r j ∈ [ k . . r i ] r_{j}\in[k..r_{i}] . Now consider the index i ′ = i − 0.5 ​ k i^{\prime}=i-0.5k and let k ′ = 1.5 ​ k k^{\prime}=1.5k .

This is a contradiction to k k being a critical bad value. Notice that r i ′ = b − r i − 0.5 ​ k ≥ b − 2 ​ k r_{i^{\prime}}=b-r_{i}-0.5k\geq b-2k .
Since | P | ≥ 2 ​ k |P|\geq 2k , we have that i ′ i^{\prime} is in P P .

∎ Next, we show that the set of values k k such that a phrase P ′ P^{\prime} is the k k -bad parent of P P form a consecutive interval. 7.6 . Let P P be a phrase and let k 1 < k 2 < k 3 ∈ 𝕂 k_{1}<k_{2}<k_{3}\in\mathbb{K} .
If P ′ P^{\prime} is the k 1 k_{1} -bad parent of P P and the k 3 k_{3} -bad parent of P P , then P ′ P^{\prime} is also the k 2 k_{2} -bad parent of P P . Proof. We will prove that under the specified assumptions, P ′ P^{\prime} is also the 1.5 ​ k 1 1.5k_{1} bad parent of P P .
This can be applied inductively until k 2 = 1.5 ​ k 1 k_{2}=1.5k_{1} to obtain the claim. Let P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] and let P ′ = S [ a ^ . . b ^ ] P^{\prime}=S[\hat{a}..\hat{b}] .
Let the source of P P be S [ a ′ . . b ′ ] S[a^{\prime}..b^{\prime}] .
For every index i ∈ [ a . . b ] i\in[a..b] such that J ( i ) ∈ [ a ^ . . b ^ ] J(i)\in[\hat{a}..\hat{b}] , the difference r J ⁡ ( i ) − r i r_{J(i)}-r_{i} is the same.
Denote this difference as δ \delta .
Since P ′ P^{\prime} is both the k 1 k_{1} and the k 3 k_{3} -bad parent of P P , we have from Lemma 7.1 that b ′ − k 1 , b ′ − k 3 ∈ [ a ^ . . b ^ ] b^{\prime}-k_{1},b^{\prime}-k_{3}\in[\hat{a}..\hat{b}] .
Since P ′ P^{\prime} is the k 1 k_{1} -bad parent, there is an index i 1 ∈ [ a . . b ] i_{1}\in[a..b] with j 1 = J ( i 1 ) ∈ [ a ^ . . b ^ ] j_{1}=J(i_{1})\in[\hat{a}..\hat{b}] such that r i 1 , r j 1 ∈ [ k 1 ..1.5 k 1 ) r_{i_{1}},r_{j_{1}}\in[k_{1}..1.5k_{1}) .
Similarly, since P ′ P^{\prime} is the k 3 k_{3} -bad parent we have some i 3 ∈ [ a . . b ] i_{3}\in[a..b] and j 3 = J ( i 3 ) ∈ [ a ^ . . b ^ ] j_{3}=J(i_{3})\in[\hat{a}..\hat{b}] with r i 3 , r j 3 ∈ [ k 3 ​ ..1.5 ​ k 3 ] r_{i_{3}},r_{j_{3}}\in[k_{3}..1.5k_{3}] In particular, since r j 1 = r i 1 − δ r_{j_{1}}=r_{i_{1}}-\delta , we must have δ < 0.5 ​ k 1 < 0.5 ​ k 2 \delta<0.5k_{1}<0.5k_{2} .
Now, consider the index i 2 = b − k 2 − δ i_{2}=b-k_{2}-\delta . We claim that i 2 ∈ [ i 3 . . i 1 ] i_{2}\in[i_{3}..i_{1}] .
It follows from i 3 = b − r i 3 ≤ b − k 3 ≤ b − 1.5 ​ k 2 ≤ b − k 2 − δ = i 2 i_{3}=b-r_{i_{3}}\leq b-k_{3}\leq b-1.5k_{2}\leq b-k_{2}-\delta=i_{2} , and from i 1 = b − r 1 ≥ b − 1.5 ​ k 1 ≥ b − k 2 ≥ b − k 2 − δ = i 2 i_{1}=b-r_{1}\geq b-1.5k_{1}\geq b-k_{2}\geq b-k_{2}-\delta=i_{2} .
Therefore, i 2 ∈ [ a . . b ] i_{2}\in[a..b] is an index within P P , and due to the same reasoning, we have that j 2 = J ( i 2 ) ∈ [ a ^ . . b ^ ] j_{2}=J(i_{2})\in[\hat{a}..\hat{b}] .
Now notice that r i 2 = k 2 + δ ∈ [ k 2 ..1.5 k 2 ) r_{i_{2}}=k_{2}+\delta\in[k_{2}..1.5k_{2}) and that r j 2 = r i 2 − δ = k 2 r_{j_{2}}=r_{i_{2}}-\delta=k_{2} .
We have shown that i 2 i_{2} is an index in P P with j 2 j_{2} in P ′ P^{\prime} such that r i 2 , r j 2 ∈ [ k 2 ​ ..1.5 ​ k 2 ] r_{i_{2}},r_{j_{2}}\in[k_{2}..1.5k_{2}] , which means that P ′ P^{\prime} is the k 2 k_{2} bad parent of P P .
∎ We are ready to present our data structure for efficiently finding the k k -bad parent.
For every phrase P = S [ a . . b ] ∈ 𝒵 P=S[a..b]\in\mathcal{Z} we store the following information. 1. k ⁡ ( P ) ⊆ 𝕂 k(P)\subseteq\mathbb{K} : the at most two unique values k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} such that | P | ∈ [ k ..2 k ) |P|\in[k..2k) 2. P ′ ​ ( P ) ⊆ 𝒵 P^{\prime}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{Z} : for every k ∈ k ⁡ ( P ) k\in k(P) , the k k -bad phrase of P P , if it exists. 3. C ⁡ ( P ) C(P) : a bit vector of size log 1.5 ⁡ | P | ∈ O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) \log_{1.5}|P|\in O(\log n) . If k k is a critical bad value of P P , then C ​ ( P ) ​ [ k ] = 1 C(P)[k]=1 , otherwise C ​ ( P ) ​ [ k ] = 0 C(P)[k]=0 . 4. K ⁡ ( P ) ∈ 𝕂 K(P)\in\mathbb{K} : the unique power of 1.5 1.5 satisfying | P | ∈ [ K ( P ) ..1.5 K ( P ) ) |P|\in[K(P)..1.5K(P)) . 5. h ( P ) ∈ [ 1 . . n ] h(P)\in[1..n] : the minimal integer multiple of K ⁡ ( P ) K(P) contained in P P .
Formally, h P = min ( { i ⋅ K ( P ) ∣ i ∈ 𝒵 } ∩ [ a . . b ] ) h_{P}=\min(\{i\cdot K(P)\mid i\in\mathcal{Z}\}\cap[a..b]) Additionally, we build the hash table H H in which for every P P , we have H ⁡ ( h ⁡ ( P ) ) = P H(h(P))=P ( P P is represented as its index in 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} ).
The construction of H H requires O ⁡ ( z ​ ( log ⁡ log ⁡ z ) 2 ) O(z(\log\log z)^{2}) time with Lemma 2.2 .
The stored information for each phrase can be computed in O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time for every phrase as follows.
The values k ⁡ ( P ) k(P) , K ⁡ ( P ) K(P) and h ⁡ ( P ) h(P) can be obtained in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time via simple arithmetic operations. P ′ ​ ( P ) P^{\prime}(P) can be obtained in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n k ) O(\log\frac{n}{k}) time using the data structure of Lemma 7.3 .
To construct C ⁡ ( P ) C(P) , we iterate all values in 𝕂 \mathbb{K} .
For each such value, we check if it is a critical bad value directly according to the definition.
To do that, we need to be able to retrieve the k k -bad parent of P P for a given k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , which we do in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n k ) O(\log\frac{n}{k}) time using Lemma 7.3 .
The total running time for finding C ⁡ ( P ) C(P) is O ⁡ ( | 𝕂 | ⋅ log ⁡ n z ) = O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(|\mathbb{K}|\cdot\log\frac{n}{z})=O(\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) . Let us prove that the above information is sufficient to retrieve the k k -bad parent of P P in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time. 7.7 . Given a phrase P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] of 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} with its source S [ a ′ . . b ′ ] S[a^{\prime}..b^{\prime}] and the values k ⁡ ( P ) , P ′ ​ ( P ) , C ⁡ ( P ) k(P),P^{\prime}(P),C(P) .
Given the hash H H , we can find for a given k k the k k -bad parent of P P (or report that it does not exist) in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time. Proof. We describe an algorithm for finding the k k -bad parent of P P or reporting that it does not exist.
The algorithm constructs a set of candidate phrases 𝒫 \mathcal{P} for being the k k -bad parent of P P .
Then, the algorithm tests each candidate for being the k k -bad parent of P P using Corollary 7.2 . The set of candidates is constructed as follows.
First, we add the phrases in P ′ ​ ( P ) P^{\prime}(P) to 𝒫 \mathcal{P} .
Then, we find the smallest critical bad value k ′ k^{\prime} of P P that is at least k k using C ⁡ ( P ) C(P) .
If there is indeed such value k ′ k^{\prime} , we iterate each integer x x that is an integer multiple of k ′ k^{\prime} contained in [ b ′ − 2.5 k ′ . . b ′ + 0.5 k ′ ] [b^{\prime}-2.5k^{\prime}..b^{\prime}+0.5k^{\prime}] .
For each such x x , we add P x = H ⁡ ( x ) P_{x}=H(x) to 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , if such P x P_{x} it exists (i.e. if x x is a key in H H ). This concludes the construction of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} .
Clearly, | 𝒫 | ∈ O ⁡ ( 1 ) |\mathcal{P}|\in O(1) .
This follows from | P ′ ​ ( P ) | ≤ 2 |P^{\prime}(P)|\leq 2 and from the fact that there are at most 4 4 integer multiples of k ′ k^{\prime} in [ b ′ − 2.5 k ′ . . b ′ + 0.5 k ′ ] [b^{\prime}-2.5k^{\prime}..b^{\prime}+0.5k^{\prime}] .
It should be clear that each element in 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is retrieved in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time.
In order to obtain k ′ k^{\prime} , we need to find the minimal bit that is at least k k and is equal to 1 1 in C ⁡ ( B ) C(B) .
This can be implemented by first computing the bitwise AND of C ⁡ ( B ) C(B) and its negation, which leaves
only the rightmost bit set to 1 1 . Then, we can lookup the position of the unique bit set to 1 1 by
using a preprocessed perfect hash table that can be constructed in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ⋅ ( log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) 2 ) = O ⁡ ( z ​ log ⁡ ( n / z ) ) O(\log n\cdot(\log\log n)^{2})=O(z\log(n/z)) time
with Lemma 2.2 .
Then, we verify each of the candidates in O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time using Corollary 7.2 .
It should be clear that the total running time is O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) . To prove the correctness of the algorithm, we claim that if there is a k k -bad parent P ′ P^{\prime} for P P , we indeed include P P in 𝒫 \mathcal{P} .
Assume that there is a k k -bad parent P ′ P^{\prime} .
Let k ′ ≥ k k^{\prime}\geq k be the maximal value in 𝕂 \mathbb{K} such that P ′ P^{\prime} is a k ′ k^{\prime} -bad parent of P P .
If k ′ ∈ k ⁡ ( P ) k^{\prime}\in k(P) , then P ′ ∈ C ⁡ ( P ) ⊆ 𝒫 P^{\prime}\in C(P)\subseteq\mathcal{P} , as required.
If k ′ ∉ k ⁡ ( P ) k^{\prime}\notin k(P) , it follows that | P | ≥ 2 ​ k ′ |P|\geq 2k^{\prime} .
We claim that in this case, k ′ k^{\prime} is exactly the minimal critical bad value of P P that is at least k k . 7.8 . Let P ′ P^{\prime} be the k k -bad parent of P P and let k ′ k^{\prime} be the maximal k k such that P ′ P^{\prime} is the k ′ k^{\prime} -bad parent of P P .
Let c c be the minimal critical bad value of P P that is at least k k .
If | P | ≥ 2 ​ k ′ |P|\geq 2k^{\prime} , then k ′ = c k^{\prime}=c . Proof. First, we prove that c ≤ k ′ c\leq k^{\prime} by showing that P ′ P^{\prime} is a c c -bad parent of P P .
Assume to the contrary that P ′ P^{\prime} is not the c c -bad parent of P P .
Let c ′ c^{\prime} be the minimal value in 𝕂 \mathbb{K} such that P ′ P^{\prime} is not the c ′ c^{\prime} -bad parent of P P .
Notice that c ′ ∈ [ 1.5 k . . k ′ ] ∩ 𝕂 c^{\prime}\in[1.5k..k^{\prime}]\cap\mathbb{K} .
For this values, we have that P ′ P^{\prime} is the c ′ 1.5 \frac{c^{\prime}}{1.5} -bad parent of P P but not the c ′ c^{\prime} -bad parent of P P .
We also have 2 ​ c ′ 1.5 ≤ 2 ​ c ′ ≤ 2 ​ k ′ ≤ 2 ​ | P | 2\frac{c^{\prime}}{1.5}\leq 2c^{\prime}\leq 2k^{\prime}\leq 2|P| , which together indicates that c ′ c^{\prime} is a critical bad value of P P that is at least k k and is smaller than k ′ k^{\prime} , a contradiction. Now, let us show that for any c ≥ k ′ c\geq k^{\prime} .
Assume to the contrary that k ′ > c k^{\prime}>c .
We have already shown that P ′ P^{\prime} is the c c -bad parent of P P .
Since k ′ , c ∈ 𝕂 k^{\prime},c\in\mathbb{K} , we have k ′ ≥ 1.5 ​ c k^{\prime}\geq 1.5c .
From P ′ P^{\prime} being both the k ′ k^{\prime} -bad parent and the c c -bad parent of P P , Lemma 7.6 implies that P ′ P^{\prime} is also the 1.5 ​ c 1.5c -bad parent of P P .
In particular, P ′ P^{\prime} is both the c c and the 1.5 ​ c 1.5c bad parent of P P with 2 ​ c ≤ 2 ​ k ′ ≤ | P | 2c\leq 2k^{\prime}\leq|P| , a contradiction to c c being a critical bad value.
∎ It follows from claim 7.8 that P ′ P^{\prime} is indeed the k ′ k^{\prime} -bad parent of P P for the value k ′ k^{\prime} we find using C ⁡ ( P ) C(P) .
According to Lemma 7.5 , we have that K ⁡ ( P ) = k ′ K(P)=k^{\prime} , and according to Lemma 7.1 we have that P ′ P^{\prime} contains the index b ′ − k ′ b^{\prime}-k^{\prime} .
Therefore, since | P ′ | ≤ 1.5 ​ k ′ |P^{\prime}|\leq 1.5k^{\prime} we have that P ′ P^{\prime} is contained in [ b ′ − 2.5 k ′ . . b ′ + 0.5 k ′ ] [b^{\prime}-2.5k^{\prime}..b^{\prime}+0.5k^{\prime}] , and in particular h ( P ′ ) ∈ [ b ′ − 2.5 k ′ . . b ′ + 0.5 k ′ ] h(P^{\prime})\in[b^{\prime}-2.5k^{\prime}..b^{\prime}+0.5k^{\prime}] .
Since h ⁡ ( P ′ ) h(P^{\prime}) is a multiple of k ′ k^{\prime} , and we check every multiple of k ′ k^{\prime} in this range, we have found H ⁡ ( h ⁡ ( P ′ ) ) = P ′ H(h(P^{\prime}))=P^{\prime} and added it to 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , as required.
∎ Substituting ( log ⁡ log ⁡ z ) 2 (\log\log z)^{2} with log ⁡ n z \log\frac{n}{z} . We notice that the ( log ⁡ log ⁡ z ) 2 (\log\log z)^{2} factor arises from computing the deterministic hash of [ 23 ] over z z elements from domain of size n n .
We can instead partition the domain n n into z z uniform intervals U 1 , U 2 , … ​ U z U_{1},U_{2},\ldots U_{z} of size n z \frac{n}{z} .
Then, we construct a separate hash H i H_{i} for every U i U_{i} , only mapping the values in U i U_{i} to their respective elements in [ z ] [z] .
When constructing the hash of U i = ( i ⋅ n z . . ( i + 1 ) ⋅ n z ] U_{i}=(i\cdot\frac{n}{z}..(i+1)\cdot\frac{n}{z}] , we use relative values instead of absolute values (i.e., x ∈ U i x\in U_{i} is encoded as x − i ⋅ n z x-i\cdot\frac{n}{z} ).
Therefore, the hash is over a domain of size n z \frac{n}{z} .
Now, when we wish to find H ⁡ ( x ) H(x) , we first find U i U_{i} containing x x in constant time, and query H i ​ [ x ′ ] H_{i}[x^{\prime}] with x ′ − i ⋅ n z x^{\prime}-i\cdot\frac{n}{z} in constant time.
The time for constructing H i H_{i} is z i ​ ( log ⁡ log ⁡ z i ) 2 = O ⁡ ( z i ​ log ⁡ n z ) z_{i}(\log\log z_{i})^{2}=O(z_{i}\log\frac{n}{z}) , where z i z_{i} is the number of hashed values within U i U_{i} .
Since ∑ i = 1 z ( z i ) = z \sum_{i=1}^{z}(z_{i})=z , the total running time for constructing all hashes is O ⁡ ( z ​ log ⁡ n z ) O(z\log\frac{n}{z}) as required.

Appendix A Shift-Split-Merge Structure This section is dedicated to proving the following lemma. As mentioned earlier, it has been already proven
by Farach and Thorup [ 10 ] , and we only present our proof for completeness.
Also, we make it more clear that the time complexity depends on n n , the maximum number of distinct elements in a tree.
We briefly comment that Iacono and Özkan [ 15 ] claimed a faster data structure, but
a complete description of the shift operation isn’t present in the published version, and it is not immediately clear
if their complexity could be made dependant on the number of distinct elements. See 6.1 We implement each balanced search tree with e.g. red-black trees [ 13 ] . Recall that a red-black tree T T has the property that, given a node u ∈ T u\in T , we can split T T into a tree containing all elements strictly smaller or equal to u u and a tree
containing all elements larger than u u in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ N ) O(\log N) time. Similarly, given two trees T 1 T_{1} and T 2 T_{2} such that
every element stored in T 1 T_{1} is smaller or equal to every element stored in T 2 T_{2} , we can obtain a tree storing all elements
in T 1 T_{1} and T 2 T_{2} in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ N ) O(\log N) time. We call the former operation splitting and the latter joining (to distinguish from
merging, which is defined as in the statement of the lemma). Of course, because the tree is balanced, we are able to find
the (strict) successor/predecessor of any element in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ N ) O(\log N) time, and insert/delete new elements in the same time complexity. Each node v v of the tree
is decorated with a shift δ v \delta_{v} , and the invariant is that the, a node u u physically storing an element x x actually corresponds
to x x increased by the sum of δ v \delta_{v} , over every v v that is an ancestor of u u . It i straightforward to maintain the invariant
during a rotation. Initialize, insertion/deletion, split, and shift are immediate to implement, with the first working in worst-case
constant time, and the remaining operations working in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ N ) O(\log N) worst-case time. The non-trivial step is implementing a merge. For a tree T T , let 𝖽𝗂𝗌𝗍𝗂𝗇𝖼𝗍 ⁡ ( T ) \mathsf{distinct}(T) denote the number of distinct elements in T T . We first observe that, given two trees T 1 T_{1} and T 2 T_{2} ,
we can compute their merge in O ⁡ ( min ⁡ { 𝖽𝗂𝗌𝗍𝗂𝗇𝖼𝗍 ⁡ ( T 1 ) , 𝖽𝗂𝗌𝗍𝗂𝗇𝖼𝗍 ⁡ ( T 2 ) } ​ log ​ N ) O(\min\{\mathsf{distinct}(T_{1}),\mathsf{distinct}(T_{2})\}\log N) time. This is done as follows.
We first retrieve the smallest elements of T 1 T_{1} and T 2 T_{2} , denoted x 1 x_{1} and y 1 y_{1} , respectively.
By symmetry, let us assume that x 1 ≤ y 1 x_{1}\leq y_{1} . We split T 1 T_{1} at y 1 y_{1} to obtain T 1 ′ T^{\prime}_{1} and T 1 ′ T^{\prime}_{1} and we similarly split T 2 T_{2} at y 1 y_{1} to obtain T 2 ′ T^{\prime}_{2} (containing only elements equal to y 1 y_{1} ) and T 2 ′′ T^{\prime\prime}_{2} .
We join T 1 ′ T^{\prime}_{1} , T 2 ′ T^{\prime}_{2} , and the result of repeating the procedure on T 1 ′′ T^{\prime\prime}_{1} and T 2 ′′ T^{\prime\prime}_{2} .
The time complexity is O ⁡ ( min ⁡ { 𝖽𝗂𝗌𝗍𝗂𝗇𝖼𝗍 ⁡ ( T 1 ) , 𝖽𝗂𝗌𝗍𝗂𝗇𝖼𝗍 ⁡ ( T 2 ) } ​ log ​ N ) O(\min\{\mathsf{distinct}(T_{1}),\mathsf{distinct}(T_{2})\}\log N) , and more precisely it is upper bounded by the interleave
between the distinct elements of T 1 T_{1} and T 2 T_{2} times O ⁡ ( log ⁡ N ) O(\log N) . However, we will establish that the amortized complexity
is actually much better. To analyze the amortized complexity of a merge, we use the following potential function, where to avoid clutter
each unit of potential suffices to pay for O ⁡ ( log ⁡ N ) O(\log N) time.
Consider a tree T T , and let its elements (ignoring the duplicates) be x 1 < x 2 < … < x k x_{1}<x_{2}<\ldots<x_{k} . We define
potential of T T to be ∑ i 1 + log ⁡ ( x i + 1 − x i ) \sum_{i}1+\log(x_{i+1}-x_{i}) , and then the potential of the whole structure is the sum of the potentials
of its constituent trees.
It is immediate that the amortized cost of initialization is O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) , the amortized complexity of insertion
is O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ​ log ⁡ N ) O(\log n\log N) as we must account for the increase in the potential, while the amortized complexity of deletion,
split and shift is O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ​ log ⁡ N ) O(\log n\log N) as the potential cannot increase. To analyze the change in the potential after
a merge operation, we first observe that the additional 1 1 in the definition allows us to absorb the cost for the elements
that occur in both trees. Thus, we will assume that the elements stored in both trees are distinct.
To analyze the change in the potential, it is enough to analyze the situation after inserting a range of k k elements x i < x i + 1 < … ​ x i + k − 1 x_{i}<x_{i+1}<\ldots x_{i+k-1} from one tree between two consecutive elements y j < y j + 1 y_{j}<y_{j+1} of the other tree,
which corresponds to a single step of the merging procedure.
Then, the distance of y j y_{j} to its successor is decreased by at least half while the distance of every other
element to its successor does not increase,
or the distance of y j y_{j} to its predecessor is decreased by at least half while the distance of every other
element to its predecessor does not increase.
The potential can be equivalently defined by considering the distance of each element to its successor or the distance
of each element to its predecessor, so we obtain that for at least half steps of the merging procedure the potential
decreases by at least 1 1 , which allows us to pay for all the steps. Appendix B Eliminating Large Phrases This section is dedicated to proving the following lemma.
See 2.1 We start by showing that a large phrase can be replaced by a small number of smaller phrases.
We then show how to apply this replacement iteratively and efficiently. B.1 . Let 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} be an LZ-End partition with z z phrases of a string S S such that all the phrases have length at most n z \frac{n}{z} except for the last phrase P i P_{i} .
We can partition P i P_{i} into at most 2 ​ z ​ | P i | n + 1 \frac{2z|P_{i}|}{n}+1 substrings Q 1 , Q 2 , … Q_{1},Q_{2},\ldots such that for every Q j = S [ c 1 . . d 1 ] Q_{j}=S[c_{1}..d_{1}] , there is a phrase P i j = S [ a i j . . b i j ] P_{i_{j}}=S[a_{i_{j}}..b_{i_{j}}] for some i j < i i_{j}<i such that Q j = S [ b i j − | Q j | + 1 . . b i j ] Q_{j}=S[b_{i_{j}}-|Q_{j}|+1..b_{i_{j}}] .
Equivalently, P i P_{i} can be replaced with Q 1 , Q 2 , … Q_{1},Q_{2},\ldots in 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} with respective sources P i 1 , P i 2 , … P_{i_{1}},P_{i_{2}},\ldots , resulting in a valid LZ-End partition of S S . Proof. For i ∈ [ z ] i\in[z] , denote c ​ u ​ t ​ ( i ) = j cut(i)=j as the index of the unique phrase P j P_{j} containing b i − n z b_{i}-\frac{n}{z} .
