∎ We are now ready to prove Lemma 2.1 . Proof. We process 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} from left to right.
As long as we encounter phrases smaller than n z \frac{n}{z} , we simply append them to 𝒵 ′ \mathcal{Z}^{\prime} (which is initially empty).
When we reach a phrase P i P_{i} with | P i | ≥ n z |P_{i}|\geq\frac{n}{z} with O ⁡ ( 2 ​ z ​ | P i | n + 1 ) O(\frac{2z|P_{i}|}{n}+1) phrases by applying claim B.1 on 𝒵 ′ , P i \mathcal{Z}^{\prime},P_{i} (notice that 𝒵 ′ , P i \mathcal{Z}^{\prime},P_{i} is an LZ-End partition of S [ 1 . . b i ] S[1..b_{i}] with only the last phrase larger than n z \frac{n}{z} ), and add the obtained pieces Q 1 , Q 2 , … Q_{1},Q_{2},\ldots to 𝒵 ′ \mathcal{Z}^{\prime} from left to right (instead of adding P i P_{i} ). In order to apply the decomposition of P i P_{i} into smaller phrases described in claim B.1 , we need to be able to access c ​ u ​ t ​ ( j ) cut(j) , the phrase containing b j − n z b_{j}-\frac{n}{z} in constant time.
To support this, we compute and store c ​ u ​ t ​ ( j ) cut(j) for every phrase P i ′ P^{\prime}_{i} that is added to 𝒵 ′ \mathcal{Z}^{\prime} as we process 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} from left to right.
With access to c ​ u ​ t ​ ( j ) cut(j) , a straightforward implementation of Lemma 4.18 will takes O ⁡ ( n i ) O(n_{i}) where n i n_{i} is the number of newly created phrases. When we append P x ′ = [ a x ′ . . b x ′ ] P^{\prime}_{x}=[a^{\prime}_{x}..b^{\prime}_{x}] to 𝒵 ′ \mathcal{Z}^{\prime} , we first check if P j ′ ′ P^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}} with j ′ = c ​ u ​ t ​ ( x ′ − 1 ) j^{\prime}=cut(x^{\prime}-1) contains b x ′ − n z b^{\prime}_{x}-\frac{n}{z} .
If it does, we set c ​ u ​ t ​ ( x ) = j ′ cut(x)=j^{\prime} .
Otherwise, it must be the case that b x ′ − n z b^{\prime}_{x}-\frac{n}{z} is to the right of P j ′ P_{j^{\prime}} .
We check P j ′ + 1 , P j ′ + 2 , … P_{j^{\prime}+1},P_{j^{\prime}+2},\ldots until finally reaching the phrase P c ′ P^{\prime}_{c} containing b x ′ − n z b^{\prime}_{x}-\frac{n}{z} and set c ​ u ​ t ​ ( x ) = c cut(x)=c .
Since every phrase is only eliminated once as c ​ u ​ t ​ ( x ) cut(x) , the overall evaluation of all c ​ u ​ t ​ ( x ) cut(x) values requires O ⁡ ( z ′ ) = O ⁡ ( z ) O(z^{\prime})=O(z) time (we justify z ′ = O ⁡ ( z ) z^{\prime}=O(z) below). The number of new pieces created is bounded by ∑ P ( 2 ​ z ⋅ | P | n + 1 ) \sum_{P}(\frac{2z\cdot|P|}{n}+1) where the sum is taken over the phrases we split.
Since we only split phrases with length at least n z \frac{n}{z} and the sum of the lengths of all phrases is n n , the number of components in this sum is O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) .
We therefore have that the total number of added phrases is bounded by 2 ​ z ​ n n + z = 3 ​ z \frac{2zn}{n}+z=3z .
It follows that the newly created partition has at most 4 ​ z 4z phrases. Since we create every new phrase in constant time, the time complexity is proportional to the number of phrases, which is O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) .
∎ Appendix C Data Structure for Substring Extraction In this section, we present an efficient compressed data structure for substring extraction, proving Theorem 1.2 .
That is, given [ i . . j ] [i..j] , return S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] .
We will present a data structure that uses O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space and supports queries in O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n z + j − i ) O(\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}+j-i) time.
That is, the best conceivable query time without improving upon the query time for random access presented in Theorem 1.1 . We will assume that j − i ≤ log 2 ⁡ n z j-i\leq\log^{2}\frac{n}{z} , and show an algorithm with running time O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) .
In the general case where j − i j-i is large, we can break S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] into pieces of size log 2 ⁡ n z \log^{2}\frac{n}{z} and extract each piece separately for a total running time of O ⁡ ( j − i ) O(j-i) . Intuition and Overview. As a primary tool, we will first present an optimal data structure with O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space and O ⁡ ( j − i ) O(j-i) query time for the special case where j j is the ending index of a phrase P P (also given at query time) 1 1 1 Kreft and Navarro [ 19 ] also introduced an algorithm for this case, but their definition of LZ-End is slightly different from the one we use, which is the common definition in recent works on LZ-End. .
We call this data structure a suffix extraction data structure, and we will make extensive use of this data structure in our general substring extraction data structure. The existence of the suffix extraction data structure naturally defined an ’advantageous’ setting where general substring extraction can be done efficiently.
For instance, if S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] contains a phrase boundary b b , we can extract S [ i . . b ] S[i..b] in O ⁡ ( b − i + log ⁡ n z ) O(b-i+\log\frac{n}{z}) time (the O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) factor is required to find b b via Lemma 4.7 ).
This allows us to assume that our input does not contain a phrase boundary With this assumption in place, another convenient case for extraction arises.
If r i ∈ O ⁡ ( j − i ) r_{i}\in O(j-i) we can use suffix extraction to extract S [ i . . i + r i ] S[i..i+r_{i}] in O ⁡ ( j − i ) O(j-i) time.
Due to our assumption that [ i . . j ] [i..j] has no phrase boundaries, we have i + r i > j i+r_{i}>j and we therefore have S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] at hand as a prefix of S [ i . . i + r i ] S[i..i+r_{i}] . Our goal now shifts to reducing a general input i , j i,j into an input with r i ∈ O ⁡ ( j − i ) r_{i}\in O(j-i) .
To this end, we will employ the epoch approach of the random access algorithm.
Intuitively, we would like to say that this goal is obtainable by simply running the random access algorithm on i i , and halting the algorithm prematurely when a sufficiently small r r -value is obtained.
Recall that the random access algorithm consists of epochs, each reducing the r r -value to the next exponential level compared to the value seen at the start of the epoch.
Therefore, one may expect that when running a sequence of epochs on index i i , we will encounter a sequence of indices i = i 1 , i 2 , … ​ i x i=i_{1},i_{2},\ldots i_{x} such that if r i x ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i_{x}}\in[k..1.5k) , then r i x + 1 ∈ [ k / 1.5 . . k ) r_{i_{x+1}}\in[k/1.5..k) (i.e., in the next smallest exponential level).
Combined with the observation that as long as r i x > j − i r_{i_{x}}>j-i , it holds that S [ i x . . i x + j − i ] = S [ i . . j ] S[i_{x}..i_{x}+j-i]=S[i..j] , this structure immediately leads to an extraction algorithm.
We can run the random access algorithm on i i , and instead of halting upon reaching i ′ i^{\prime} with r i ′ = 0 r_{i^{\prime}}=0 , we halt upon reaching r i ′ ∈ [ j − i ​ ..1.5 ​ ( j − i ) ] r_{i^{\prime}}\in[j-i..1.5(j-i)] This should occur within O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) epochs, leading to O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) running time, with an additional O ⁡ ( j − i ) O(j-i) running time to apply suffix extraction on the reduced instance. Unfortunately, the above structure does not necessarily occur in our random access algorithm.
It may be the case that the r r -value of i x i_{x} is arbitrarily smaller than r i x − 1 r_{i_{x-1}} , leading to an r r -value smaller than j − i j-i .
This means that following the source of the phrase containing i x i_{x} will not necessarily lead to an occurrence of S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] , making any further step following i x i_{x} potentially lose some of the information we are required to extract. To bypass this problem, we define a modified variant of the epoch procedure.
Our epoch procedure takes indices i , j i,j as input with r i ∈ [ max ( 10 ( j − i ) , k ) ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[\max(10(j-i),k)..1.5k) for some k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} .
The implied assumption that r i > 10 ​ ( j − i ) r_{i}>10(j-i) can be enforced as we established in the previous discussion. The epoch procedure has two outputs.
First, it outputs the prefix S [ i . . i ′ ) S[i..i^{\prime}) for some index i ′ ∈ [ i . . j + 1 ] i^{\prime}\in[i..j+1] (notice that if i ′ = i i^{\prime}=i , this component of the output is trivial).
Additionally, the epoch outputs an index i ∗ i^{*} on which we can recourse, and that makes progress towards a termination condition.
That is, the index i ∗ i^{*} has S [ i ∗ . . j ∗ ] = S [ i ′ . . j ] S[i^{*}..j^{*}]=S[i^{\prime}..j] for j ∗ = i ∗ − i ′ + j j^{*}=i^{*}-i^{\prime}+j , which means that the remaining suffix to be extracted from S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] occurs at i ∗ i^{*} .
We also demand that the index i ∗ i^{*} has r i ∗ ∈ [ j − i . . k ) r_{i^{*}}\in[j-i..k) , bringing us closer towards the termination condition of the algorithm.
The running time is O ⁡ ( i ′ − i + log ⁡ n z ) O(i^{\prime}-i+\log\frac{n}{z}) . For the special case where i ′ = j + 1 i^{\prime}=j+1 , we simply have that the epoch procedure extracted S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] in its entirety, and completing the execution of the extraction query.
In this special case, we allow the epoch to consume O ⁡ ( j − i + log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(j-i+\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time.
For the formal presentation of the epoch procedure, see Lemma C.3 . We implement the epoch by essentially running the epoch of the extraction procedure on i i .
If it happen to result in a gradual decrease in r r -value, we can simply return its output.
Otherwise, we carefully analyze the cases that arise from an r r -value below j − i j-i , and exploit the structure that arises in each of these cases to extract some prefix of S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] while locating an alternative i ∗ i^{*} with a sufficiently small r r -value. C.1 Suffix Extraction .
We start by solving the special case where j j is an ending index of a phrase.
We assume that the query specifies the phrase P P that ends in index j j .
We will show a data structure with O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space, O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O(z\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) construction time, and O ⁡ ( j − i ) O(j-i) query time. Preprocessing and tree structure In preprocessing time, we compute δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( a , b ) \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(a,b) for every phrase P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] in O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O(z\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) using Lemma 4.10 .
We defined for each P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] a parent phrase P ′ P^{\prime} as follows.
Let b ′ = b − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( a , b ) b^{\prime}=b-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(a,b) .
The phrase containing b ′ b^{\prime} is P ′ P^{\prime} .
This defines a forest structure over the phrases of 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} , we compute and store this forest T T .
Finally, we construct an unweighted ancestor data structure for T T . Query Our query procedure is recursive.
Given a query S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] with the phrase ending at index j j , we simply return S ⁡ [ j ] S[j] if i = j i=j . Otherwise, we recurse on the LZ-End phrases strictly contained within S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] .
We find the phrases intersecting S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] in time proportional to their number by simply starting with the phrase P = P y P=P_{y} ending in j j and traversing P y − 1 , P y − 2 ​ … P_{y-1},P_{y-2}\ldots until we reach some P x = [ a x . . b x ] P_{x}=[a_{x}..b_{x}] such that a x ≤ i a_{x}\leq i .
For every p ∈ [ x . . y ] p\in[x..y] seen in this process, we recursively extract S [ a p . . b p ] S[a_{p}..b_{p}] by recursing on S [ a p ′ . . b p ′ ] S[a^{\prime}_{p}..b^{\prime}_{p}] where a p ′ = a p − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( a p , b p ) a^{\prime}_{p}=a_{p}-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(a_{p},b_{p}) and b p ′ = b p − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( a p , b p ) b^{\prime}_{p}=b_{p}-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(a_{p},b_{p}) . The leftmost phrase P x = S [ a x . . b x ] P_{x}=S[a_{x}..b_{x}] intersecting S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] requires special care
We use an ancestor query to find the root of the tree containing P x P_{x} in the forest T T .
Notice that this must be a phrase of length 1 1 , so it is equal to a suffix of S [ a x . . b x ] S[a_{x}..b_{x}] (of length 1) contained within S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] .
Further notice that every ancestor of P x P_{x} is an occurrence of some suffix S [ b x − ℓ . . b x ] S[b_{x}-\ell..b_{x}] of P x P_{x} .
Let P 1 P^{1} be the root of the tree in T T containing P x P_{x} .
We generate the sequence P 1 , P 2 , … ​ P x ′ P^{1},P^{2},\ldots P^{x^{\prime}} of phrases as follows.
For k ≤ x k\leq x , if | P k | < b x − i |P_{k}|<b_{x}-i we set P k + 1 P^{k+1} to be the child of P k P^{k} in T T towards P x P_{x} .
Otherwise, if | P k | ≥ b x − i |P^{k}|\geq b_{x}-i , we set P k − 1 = P x ′ P^{k-1}=P^{x^{\prime}} as the last element of the sequence and terminate.
In words, this is the prefix of the path from the root to P x P_{x} that is truncated before the first phrase with length at least b x − i b_{x}-i .
Notice that the lengths | P 1 | , | P 2 | , … |P^{1}|,|P^{2}|,\ldots form an increasing sequence. Denote for every k ∈ [ 1 . . x ′ ] k\in[1..x^{\prime}] the endpoints P k = S [ a k . . b k ] P^{k}=S[a^{k}..b^{k}] .
The algorithm recurses on S [ b k − | P k + 1 | . . b k − | P k | ] S[b^{k}-|P^{k+1}|..b^{k}-|P^{k}|] .
Finally for P x ′ P^{x^{\prime}} the algorithm recurses on S [ b x ′ − ( b x − i ) . . b x ′ − | P x ′ | ] S[b^{x^{\prime}}-(b_{x}-i)..b^{x^{\prime}}-|P^{x^{\prime}}|] Correctness. Clearly, P x + 1 , P x + 2 , … , P y P_{x+1},P_{x+2},\ldots,P_{y} form a suffix of P [ i . . j ] P[i..j] , so extracting each of them leads to the extraction of this suffix. As for the prefix covered by P x P_{x} , we claim that the recursive calls induced by P 1 , P 2 , … ​ P x ′ P^{1},P^{2},\ldots P^{x^{\prime}} are for strings that form a partition of S [ i . . b x ] S[i..b_{x}] .
More precisely, we claim that for every k ∈ [ 1 . . x ′ ) k\in[1..x^{\prime}) it holds that S [ b x − | P k + 1 | . . b x − | P k | ] = S [ b k − | P k + 1 | . . b k − | P k | ] S[b_{x}-|P^{k+1}|..b_{x}-|P^{k}|]=S[b^{k}-|P^{k+1}|..b^{k}-|P^{k}|] , and that S [ i . . b x − | P x ′ ] = S [ b x ′ − ( j − b x ) . . b x ′ − | P x ′ | ] S[i..b_{x}-|P^{x^{\prime}}]=S[b^{x^{\prime}}-(j-b_{x})..b^{x^{\prime}}-|P^{x^{\prime}}|] . This follows immediately from the following stronger property C.1 . Let P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] be a phrase in 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} .
Let P ′′ P^{\prime\prime} be an ancestor of P P in T T and let P ′′ = S [ a ′′ . . b ′′ ] P^{\prime\prime}=S[a^{\prime\prime}..b^{\prime\prime}] be the parent of P ′ P^{\prime} in T T .
It holds that S [ b − ( b ′ − a ′ ) . . b ] = S [ b ′′ − ( b ′ − a ′ ) . . b ′′ ] S[b-(b^{\prime}-a^{\prime})..b]=S[b^{\prime\prime}-(b^{\prime}-a^{\prime})..b^{\prime\prime}] . Proof. We prove by induction on the distance from P P to P ′ P^{\prime} .
For the case of distance 0, we have that P = P ′ = S [ a . . b ] P=P^{\prime}=S[a..b] and P ′′ = S [ a ′′ . . b ′′ ] P^{\prime\prime}=S[a^{\prime\prime}..b^{\prime\prime}] such that b ′′ = b − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( a , b ) b^{\prime\prime}=b-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(a,b) .
It is immediately implies by the definition of δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{} that S [ a . . b ] = S [ a ′′ . . b ′′ ] S[a..b]=S[a^{\prime\prime}..b^{\prime\prime}] , as required. For the inductive vase, we have that P ′ P^{\prime} is at distance d > 0 d>0 from P P .
Let P ^ = S [ a ^ . . b ^ ] \hat{P}=S[\hat{a}..\hat{b}] be the child of P ′ P^{\prime} towards P P .
From the induction hypothesis, we have that S [ b − ( b ^ − a ^ ) . . b ] = S [ b ′ − ( b ^ − a ^ ) . . b ′ ] S[b-(\hat{b}-\hat{a})..b]=S[b^{\prime}-(\hat{b}-\hat{a})..b^{\prime}] .
Since b ^ − a ^ > b ′ − a ′ \hat{b}-\hat{a}>b^{\prime}-a^{\prime} , we have in particular S [ a ′ . . b ′ ] = S [ b ′ − ( b ′ − a ′ ) . . b ′ ] = S [ b − ( b ′ − a ′ ) . . b ] S[a^{\prime}..b^{\prime}]=S[b^{\prime}-(b^{\prime}-a^{\prime})..b^{\prime}]=S[b-(b^{\prime}-a^{\prime})..b] .
By definition, P ′′ = S [ a ′′ . . b ′′ ] P^{\prime\prime}=S[a^{\prime\prime}..b^{\prime\prime}] such that b ′′ = b ′ − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( a ′ , b ′ ) b^{\prime\prime}=b^{\prime}-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}) .
It is immediately implies by the definition of δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{} that S [ b ′′ − ( b ′ − a ′ ) . . b ′′ ] = S [ a ′ . . b ′ ] = S [ b − ( b ′ − a ′ ) . . b ] S[b^{\prime\prime}-(b^{\prime}-a^{\prime})..b^{\prime\prime}]=S[a^{\prime}..b^{\prime}]=S[b-(b^{\prime}-a^{\prime})..b] , as required.
∎ Lemma C.1 shows that the equality S [ b x − | P k + 1 | . . b x − | P k | ] = S [ b k − | P k + 1 | . . b k − | P k | ] S[b_{x}-|P^{k+1}|..b_{x}-|P^{k}|]=S[b^{k}-|P^{k+1}|..b^{k}-|P^{k}|] indeed holds.
Further notice that the substring S [ b k − | P k + 1 | . . b k − | P k | ] S[b^{k}-|P^{k+1}|..b^{k}-|P^{k}|] ends in a phrase boundary, as b k − | P k | + 1 b^{k}-|P^{k}|+1 is the first index of P k P^{k} , so . b k − | P k | .b^{k}-|P^{k}| is the last index of the phrase preceding P k P^{k} . In conclusion, we have shown that the recursive calls we made are for strings that together form a partition of S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] , and that each of them is smaller than S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] .
It follows that the algorithm returns the correct answer, and that it terminates. Complexity. During the running time of the query, we are either in a termination case of the recursion - and return S ⁡ [ j ] S[j] in constant time, or we make R R recursive calls, and work O ⁡ ( R ) O(R) time in total to make these calls for some integer R > 1 R>1 . This process can be described as a tree, where the children of each recursive call are the recursive calls made by it.
As every recursive call partition the substring it is responsible for, this tree has j − i j-i leaves, and each leaf correspond to O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) time of the algorithm.
Every external node of the tree with degree R R corresponds to a recursive calls which is executed in O ⁡ ( R ) O(R) time. It follows that the total running time is bounded by the number of edges in a tree with j − i j-i leafs such that all internal degrees are at least 2, which is O ⁡ ( j − i ) O(j-i) as required. We conclude with the following. C.2 . Given an LZ-End factorization 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} with z z phrases of a string S S with length n n , we can construct in O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space and O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O(z\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time a data structure that given i , j i,j , and a phrase P P that ends in index j j , returns S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] in O ⁡ ( j − i ) O(j-i) time. C.2 The General Case As in the random access algorithm of Theorem 1.1 , our algorithm is partition into epochs, where each epoch may extract some prefix S [ i . . i ′ ) S[i..i^{\prime}) of S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] , and provide an index i ∗ i^{*} that is an occurrence of S [ i ′ . . j ] S[i^{\prime}..j] , and i ∗ i^{*} has a significantly lower r r -value than i i .
An epoch is described as the following procedure. C.3 . For a string S S with LZ-End factorization 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} of size z z , there is a data structure supporting the following query. Input: i ≤ j ∈ [ n ] i\leq j\in[n] such that r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) for k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , and r i > 10 ​ ( j − i ) r_{i}>10(j-i) . Output: For some i ′ ∈ [ i . . j ] i^{\prime}\in[i..j] , return S [ i . . i ′ ) S[i..i^{\prime}) and an index i ∗ ∈ [ n ] i^{*}\in[n] such that S [ i ∗ . . i ∗ − i ′ + j ] = S [ i ′ . . j ] S[i^{*}..i^{*}-i^{\prime}+j]=S[i^{\prime}..j] and r i ∗ ∈ [ j − i ′ . . k ) r_{i^{*}}\in[j-i^{\prime}..k) . The data structure can be constructed from 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} in O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ ( n z ) ) O(z\log^{2}(\frac{n}{z})) .
The query time is O ⁡ ( j − i + log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(j-i+\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) if the output is S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] .
Otherwise, the query time is O ⁡ ( i ′ − i + log ⁡ n z ) O(i^{\prime}-i+\log\frac{n}{z}) . Given Lemmas C.3 and C.7 , we can prove Theorem 1.2 . Proof of Theorem 1.2 . For the preprocessing, we construct the data structure of Lemma C.3 .
For a query i , j i,j , we repeatedly apply the following. First, we would like to enforce r i > j − i r_{i}>j-i .
To this end, we find the starting index i ′ i^{\prime} of the phrase containing j j using Lemma 4.7 .
If i ′ ≤ i i^{\prime}\leq i , we already have r i ≥ j − i r_{i}\geq j-i .
Otherwise, the prefix S [ i . . i ′ ] S[i..i^{\prime}] can be retrieved in O ⁡ ( i ′ − i ) O(i^{\prime}-i) using Lemma C.2 , and the remaining task is to retrieve S [ i ′ . . j ] S[i^{\prime}..j] , where r i ′ ≥ j − i ′ r_{i^{\prime}}\geq j-i^{\prime} .
To avoid clutter, let us simply assume that r i ≥ j − i r_{i}\geq j-i (implicitly using i i in reference to i ′ i^{\prime} if the above reduction was required). If r i ≤ 10 ​ ( j − i ) r_{i}\leq 10(j-i) we can use Lemma C.2 to retrieve S [ i . . i + r i ] S[i..i+r_{i}] in O ⁡ ( r i ) = O ⁡ ( j − i ) O(r_{i})=O(j-i) time.
Otherwise, we have that r i > 10 ​ ( j − i ) r_{i}>10(j-i) and therefore we can apply Lemma C.3 .
If Lemma C.3 outputs S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] , we are done.
Otherwise, let k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} such that r i ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[k..1.5k) .
The output of Lemma C.3 is S [ i . . i ′ ) S[i..i^{\prime}) and an index i ∗ i^{*} such that S [ i ′ . . j ] = S [ i ∗ . . i ∗ − i + j ] S[i^{\prime}..j]=S[i^{*}..i^{*}-i+j] , and r i ∗ < k r_{i^{*}}<k .
Our task is therefore reduced to extracting S [ i ∗ . . i ∗ − i + j ] S[i^{*}..i^{*}-i+j] .
We obtain this reduced instance in total O ⁡ ( i ′ − i + log ⁡ n z ) O(i^{\prime}-i+\log\frac{n}{z}) time. When iteratively applying the above, every call for Lemma C.3 results in i ′ i^{\prime} with r i ′ ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{i^{\prime}}\in[k..1.5k) for a k ∈ K k\in K strictly smaller than the one seen in the previous call.
Therefore, we will have a total of at most O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) calls before we finally reach r i ∈ [ j − i ​ ..10 ​ ( j − i ) ] r_{i}\in[j-i..10(j-i)] .
At this point, we can extract S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] as previously described. Since every call to Lemma C.3 retrieves a disjoint fragment of the initial S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] , the total time contributed by the O ⁡ ( i ′ − i ) O(i^{\prime}-i) component of all calls to Lemma C.3 is O ⁡ ( j − i ) O(j-i) .
Since we make a total of O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) calls, the total time contributed by the O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) components of the calls to Lemma C.3 is O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) .
In total, the running time is O ⁡ ( j − i + log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(j-i+\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) , as required. It may also be the case that the last call we make for Lemma C.3 returned the remaining suffix.
In this case, the running time of this call has an O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) factor instead of O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) .
Since we immediately terminate when this happens, the total running time would not exceed ( j − i + log 2 ⁡ n z ) (j-i+\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) ∎ For the rest of this section, we prove Lemma C.3 .
As in the random access algorithm, we split the epoch into a naive part and a stable part.
We will reuse a lot of concepts and terminology from the random access algorithm.
Let us prove some helpful properties of the 𝗉𝗋𝖾 \mathsf{pre} function. C.4 . Let i ∈ [ a . . b ] i\in[a..b] with r i ≥ b − i r_{i}\geq b-i , and let i ′ = i − δ ​ - ​ 𝗉𝗋𝖾 ​ ( a , b ) i^{\prime}=i-\mathsf{\delta}\text{-}\mathsf{pre}{}(a,b) .

∎ We now present an adjusted version of the naive part of the epoch from the random access algorithm ( Lemma 3.1 ). C.7 . Given an LZ-End factorization 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} with z z phrases of a string S S with length n n , we can construct in O ⁡ ( z ​ log 2 ​ n z ) O(z\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time a data structure taking O ⁡ ( z ) O(z) space that supports the following query. Given i , j ∈ [ n ] 2 i,j\in[n]^{2} with r i ∈ [ max ( 10 ( j − i ) , k ) ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[\max(10(j-i),k)..1.5k) for some k ∈ 𝕂 k\in\mathbb{K} , return an occurrence S [ i ′ . . j ′ ] S[i^{\prime}..j^{\prime}] of S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] (specified by its endpoints) satisfying one of the following. 1. r i ′ ∈ [ j − i . . k ) r_{i^{\prime}}\in[j-i..k) , 2. There is a phrase boundary in S [ i ′ . . j ′ ] S[i^{\prime}..j^{\prime}] , and ℓ j ′ + r j ′ + 1 < 1.5 ​ k \ell_{j^{\prime}}+r_{j^{\prime}}+1<1.5k , or 3. S [ i ′ . . j ′ ] S[i^{\prime}..j^{\prime}] is contained some a marked phrase and r i ′ < 1.5 ​ k r_{i^{\prime}}<1.5k . The running time of the query is O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) . Proof. In preprocessing time, we construct the data structure of Lemma 3.1 . In query time,we run the query algorithm of Lemma 3.1 with the following modification.
Recall that the query of Lemma 3.1 applies steps of the form J ⁡ ( i ) J(i) , J log ⁡ n z ​ ( i ) J^{\log\frac{n}{z}}(i) until the first time an index i ′ i^{\prime} is reached that either has r i < k r_{i}<k or is contained within a marked phrase. We apply exactly the same steps, but we stop when we either reach r i ′ < max ⁡ ( k , j − i ) r_{i^{\prime}}<\max(k,j-i) or then we reach an i ′ i^{\prime} contained in a marked phrase.
Since our stopping condition is strictly stronger than that of Lemma 3.1 , the running time until one of our conditions is met is bounded by the query time of Lemma 3.1 , which is O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) (notice that checking that these conditions are satisfied is done in constant time, as we have access to r i ′ r_{i^{\prime}} for every index i ′ i^{\prime} visited throughout the query). Let i ′ i^{\prime} be the first index seen throughout the query satisfying r i ′ < max ⁡ ( k , j − i ) r_{i^{\prime}}<\max(k,j-i) or i ′ i^{\prime} is in a marked phrase.
Firstly, we wish to ensure that i ′ i^{\prime} is an occurrence of S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] .
Let i ′′ i^{\prime\prime} be the last index seen before i ′ i^{\prime} in the execution of the query.
It must hold that r i ′′ > j − i r_{i^{\prime\prime}}>j-i , and since r r -values are monotonic, it holds that the r r -values of all seen indices preceding i ′′ i^{\prime\prime} is also at least j − i j-i .
This implies that S [ i ′′ . . i ′′ + j − i ] = S [ i . . j ] S[i^{\prime\prime}..i^{\prime\prime}+j-i]=S[i..j] .
If i ′ = J ⁡ ( i ′′ ) i^{\prime}=J(i^{\prime\prime}) , it follows from the definition of J J that S [ i ′ . . i ′ + j − i ] = S [ i ′′ . . i ′′ + j − i ] = S [ i . . j ] S[i^{\prime}..i^{\prime}+j-i]=S[i^{\prime\prime}..i^{\prime\prime}+j-i]=S[i..j] . Otherwise, if i ′ = J log ⁡ n z ​ ( i ′′ ) i^{\prime}=J^{\log\frac{n}{z}}(i^{\prime\prime}) , it is not necessarily the case that S [ i ′ . . i ′ + j − i ] = S [ i . . j ] S[i^{\prime}..i^{\prime}+j-i]=S[i..j] .
However, if we iteratively apply J ⁡ ( i ′′ ) , J 2 ​ ( i ′′ ) , … J(i^{\prime\prime}),J^{2}(i^{\prime\prime}),\ldots until we finally reach an index i ∗ i^{*} with r r -value less than max ⁡ ( k , j − i ) \max(k,j-i) , then i ∗ i^{*} will be an occurrence of S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] due to the same argument as before.
Notice that this can be implemented in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time, since we are guaranteed to meet this condition at J log ⁡ n z ​ ( i ′′ ) J^{\log\frac{n}{z}}(i^{\prime\prime}) (and all of those steps correspond to an upward path in T 𝖻𝖺𝖽 T_{\mathsf{bad}} , since i ′ i^{\prime} was in the ( log ⁡ n z ) (\log\frac{n}{z}) -bad interval of its containing phrase). So far, we have shown a procedure that finds an occurrence S [ i ′ . . j ′ ] S[i^{\prime}..j^{\prime}] of S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] with either r i ′ < max ⁡ ( j − i , k ) r_{i^{\prime}}<\max(j-i,k) or i ′ i^{\prime} in a marked phrase.
Let us show that this S [ i ′ . . j ′ ] S[i^{\prime}..j^{\prime}] is a valid output, satisfying one of the three conditions specified in the statement of the lemma. If r i ′ ∈ [ j − i . . k ) r_{i^{\prime}}\in[j-i..k) , then it is a valid output (satisfying the first condition in the statement of the lemma).
If r i ′ < j − i r_{i^{\prime}}<j-i , then [ i ′ . . j ′ ] [i^{\prime}..j^{\prime}] contains a phrase boundary.
Furthermore, recall that r i ′ ≤ r i < 1.5 ​ k r_{i^{\prime}}\leq r_{i}<1.5k .
Therefore, there is a phrase boundary at most 1.5 ​ k 1.5k indices to the right of i ′ i^{\prime} , and since j ′ j^{\prime} is trapped between the phrase boundary in [ i ′ . . j ′ ] [i^{\prime}..j^{\prime}] and the phrase boundary in [ i ′ . . i ′ + 1.5 k ) [i^{\prime}..i^{\prime}+1.5k) , the phrase containing j ′ j^{\prime} is of size less than 1.5 ​ k 1.5k , and we have that S [ i ′ . . j ′ ] S[i^{\prime}..j^{\prime}] satisfies the second condition statement of the lemma. If i ′ i^{\prime} is contained in a marked phrase, we have two cases.
If [ i ′ . . j ′ ] [i^{\prime}..j^{\prime}] is fully contained in the marked phrase, we have that the last condition of the lemma is satisfied.
Otherwise, we have a phrase boundary in [ i ′ . . j ′ ] [i^{\prime}..j^{\prime}] , and due to the same argument as before, we have that the phrase containing j ′ j^{\prime} is of size less than 1.5 ​ k 1.5k .
Therefore, S [ i ′ . . j ′ ] S[i^{\prime}..j^{\prime}] satisfies the second condition of the lemma in this case. The running time consists of the O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) running time of the query of Lemma 3.1 , plus possibly O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) time to compute J log ⁡ n z ​ ( i ′′ ) J^{\log\frac{n}{z}}(i^{\prime\prime}) (or some lower number of J J applications) step-by-step.
The total running time is O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) , as required.
∎ Similarly to the random access algorithm, we have that the naive part, specified above as Lemma C.7 , may already return a satisfactory output as S [ i ′ . . j ′ ] = S [ i . . j ] S[i^{\prime}..j^{\prime}]=S[i..j] with r i ∈ [ j − i . . k ) r_{i}\in[j-i..k) .
We prove the following useful properties of s ​ J ​ ( ) sJ() . C.8 . Let P = S [ a . . b ] P=S[a..b] be a phrase with bad parent P ′ = S [ a ′ . . b ′ ] P^{\prime}=S[a^{\prime}..b^{\prime}] .

∎ We are finally ready to put it all together and prove Lemma C.3 . Proof of Lemma C.3 . In preprocessing time, we construct the data structures of Lemmas C.7 and C.9 . Notice that this is a valid call, since r i ∈ [ 10 ( j − i ) ..1.5 k ) r_{i}\in[10(j-i)..1.5k) and therefore 1.5 ​ k > 10 ​ ( j − i ) 1.5k>10(j-i) which in particular implies k > 2 ​ ( j − i ) k>2(j-i) .
The extracted prefix and the index i ∗ i^{*} are a valid output, and the running time is as required. Case 3: S [ i ′ . . j ′ ] S[i^{\prime}..j^{\prime}] is contained in a marked phrase and r i ′ < 1.5 ​ k r_{i^{\prime}}<1.5k . Let S [ x . . y ] S[x..y] be the canonical interval containing i ′ i^{\prime} , and let M ⁡ ( i ′ ) = i ^ M(i^{\prime})=\hat{i} .
Notice that S [ i ^ . . i ^ + r i ^ ] = S [ i . . i + r i ^ ] S[\hat{i}..\hat{i}+r_{\hat{i}}]=S[i..i+r_{\hat{i}}] .
We consider three sub-cases. 1. Case 3.A : r i ^ ∈ [ k ..1.5 k ) r_{\hat{i}}\in[k..1.5k) . In particular, we have that S [ i ^ . . j ^ ] = S [ i . . j ] S[\hat{i}..\hat{j}]=S[i..j] for j ^ = i ^ + j − i \hat{j}=\hat{i}+j-i (since k > j − i k>j-i ).
It follows from r i ^ ≥ k r_{\hat{i}}\geq k and from claim 4.14 that ℓ j < k \ell_{j}<k , which leads to i ^ ∈ L ⁡ ( P ^ ) \hat{i}\in L(\hat{P}) where P ^ \hat{P} is the phrase containing i ^ \hat{i} .
We can apply Lemma C.10 to i ^ , j ^ \hat{i},\hat{j} to obtain S [ i ^ . . j ^ ] = S [ i . . j ] S[\hat{i}..\hat{j}]=S[i..j] in O ⁡ ( j − i + log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(j-i+\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) time or to obtain S [ i . . i ′ ) S[i..i^{\prime}) and i ∗ , j ∗ i^{*},j^{*} that are valid outputs in O ⁡ ( i ′ − i + log ⁡ n z ) O(i^{\prime}-i+\log\frac{n}{z}) time. 2. Case 3.B : r i ^ ∈ [ j − i . . k ) r_{\hat{i}}\in[j-i..k) . In this case we can simply return i ^ \hat{i} and extract nothing. 3. Case 3.C.I : r i ^ < j − i r_{\hat{i}}<j-i , and j ′ ∈ [ x . . y ] j^{\prime}\in[x..y] . In this case, we have S [ i ^ . . j ^ ] = S [ i . . j ] S[\hat{i}..\hat{j}]=S[i..j] and M ⁡ ( j ) = j ^ M(j)=\hat{j} .
This follows from the fact that M ⁡ ( ) M() maps i ′ i^{\prime} to an occurrence S [ x ^ . . y ^ ] S[\hat{x}..\hat{y}] of S [ x . . y ] S[x..y] , which contains an occurrence of S [ i . . j ] S[i..j] .
Since M ⁡ ( i ′ ) = i ^ M(i^{\prime})=\hat{i} and since r i ′ < 1.5 ​ k r_{i^{\prime}}<1.5k , it follows from Corollary C.5 that the phrase containing j ^ \hat{j} has size less than 1.5 ​ k 1.5k and we can apply Lemma C.9 to obtain a valid output. 4. Case 3.C.II : r i ^ < j − i r_{\hat{i}}<j-i , and j ′ ∉ [ x . . y ] j^{\prime}\notin[x..y] .
Here we find the rightmost phrase boundary a ′ a^{\prime} in [ x ′ . . y ′ ] [x^{\prime}..y^{\prime}] , and check what is r a ′ r_{a^{\prime}} .
If a ′ + r a ′ − i ^ < j − i a^{\prime}+r_{a^{\prime}}-\hat{i}<j-i , we extract S [ i ^ . . a ′ + r a ′ ] = S [ i . . i + r a ′ + a ′ − i ^ ] S[\hat{i}..a^{\prime}+r_{a^{\prime}}]=S[i..i+r_{a^{\prime}}+a^{\prime}-\hat{i}] in time linear to the length of this prefix using Lemma C.2 .
After that, we proceed to extract the remaining suffix using the next interval [ x ∗ . . y ∗ ] [x^{*}..y^{*}] that contains non-extracted indices.
If a ′ + r a ′ − i ^ > j − i a^{\prime}+r_{a^{\prime}}-\hat{i}>j-i , we are in the same case as 3.C.I as the phrase containing a ′ a^{\prime} must have length less than 1.5 ​ k 1.5k due Corollary C.5 . In each of the cases above, we either return a valid output directly, or delegate to Lemmas C.9 and C.10 to return a valid output in O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n z ) O(\log\frac{n}{z}) plus time proportional to the length of an extracted prefix (or possibly O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n z ) O(\log^{2}\frac{n}{z}) if the entire string is extracted). The only exception is Case 3.C.II where we extract some prefix in time proportional to its length and then proceed to extract the remaining suffix.
In this case, notice that we will remain in Case 3.C.II, and proceed to extract non-empty prefixes in time proportional to their length, or eventually land in another one of the subbases of Case 3.
As long as we remain in Case 3.C.II, the running time can be charged on the lengths of the extracted prefixes.
